<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1 Introduction and Background 
 
</p>
<p>1.1 BACKGROUND 
</p>
<p>Heat exchangers surround us in our daily lives, finding application in our homes, 
</p>
<p>offices, transportation systems, and various industrial sectors responsible for 
</p>
<p>producing the goods and energy we consume. These devices play a crucial role in the 
</p>
<p>efficient management and regulation of temperatures. In our homes, miniature heat 
</p>
<p>sinks dissipate the heat generated by processing chips in our computers, while in 
</p>
<p>vehicles, radiators circulate cooling fluids, maintaining engine temperatures and 
</p>
<p>transferring energy to the surrounding air. Even the climate within buildings is 
</p>
<p>regulated by heat exchangers, with plate heat exchangers and earth tubes enabling 
</p>
<p>environmentally friendly heating and cooling systems. In contrast, the colossal shell 
</p>
<p>and tube heat exchangers, resembling buildings in size, are integral to processes in 
</p>
<p>petroleum refineries, chemical and steel industries, and power generation, facilitating 
</p>
<p>efficient heat transfer between fluid mediums [1]. 
</p>
<p>The comprehension of the physics underlying the functioning of heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>and their critical role in our world has matured over decades, if not centuries, of 
</p>
<p>practical application. The evolution of heat exchanger complexity is evident, 
</p>
<p>progressing from rudimentary clay pots to high-tech ceramics and advanced metal 
</p>
<p>alloys featuring intricate internal design features and channels. Today, the ability to 
</p>
<p>manufacture thin-walled heat exchanger structures using high-temperature metal 
</p>
<p>alloys, while actively flowing fluids through labyrinthine channels, has propelled 
</p>
<p>modern advancements in industrial, power, and aerospace sectors. The demanding 
</p>
<p>environments of aviation and space flight necessitate materials characterized by high 
</p>
<p>strength, resistance to elevated temperatures, and high strength-to-weight ratio. The 
</p>
<p>aerospace sector is often the first to adopt these advanced materials and processing 
</p>
<p>thereof. These advancements in aerospace materials, design optimization methods, 
</p>
<p>and specialized manufacturing processes often infuse into various industries 
</p>
<p>including power and energy, oil and gas, industrial manufacturing, and many others, 
</p>
<p>resulting in far-reaching societal impacts beyond aerospace applications.  
</p>
<p>Heat exchangers for use in aerospace applications are critical components 
</p>
<p>because they must be efficient, compact, and low mass and often operate with 
</p>
<p>working fluids at extreme temperatures or pressures or both. Various components and 
</p>
<p>systems use heat exchangers such as power plants, chemical processing plants, gas 
</p>
<p>turbines, hypersonic leading edges and control surfaces, fuel cells (air supply and 
</p>
<p>thermal management), electric batteries and avionics cooling (thermal management), 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>evaporators and recuperators of waste-heat-to-power systems, and rocket engines 
</p>
<p>[2,3]. Ultra-compact and ultra-efficient heat exchangers are enablers of new 
</p>
<p>propulsion systems, such as rotating detonation rocket engines [4]. 
</p>
<p>The focus of the work documented in this thesis is on a novel manufacturing 
</p>
<p>technology for extreme environment heat exchangers, namely Directed Energy 
</p>
<p>Deposition (DED), and on the characterization of the achievable fluid dynamic 
</p>
<p>performance if this technology is applied to these heat exchangers. These components 
</p>
<p>are thin-walled (&lt; 2 mm) and contain pressurized working fluids at cryogenic (-253 
</p>
<p>°C) or extreme temperatures (&gt; 3300 °C) and high pressures (&gt; 400 bar), or both [5]. 
</p>
<p>Currently, a prominent application for this type of heat exchangers is their use for the 
</p>
<p>regenerative cooling of liquid fuel rocket engine nozzles. They can also be used for 
</p>
<p>the cooling of the combustion chambers of these rockets, and for the repressurization 
</p>
<p>systems of launch vehicles. Examples of demonstrator parts fabricated in parallel 
</p>
<p>with this research are shown in Figure 1.1. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.1. Examples of LP-DED manufacturing demonstrator heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers built. A) LP-DED process shown depositing integral channels, B) 
</p>
<p>Integral channel nozzle shown in LP-DED machine at 1.52 m diameter and 
</p>
<p>1.78 m height, C) Example of complex geometry using LP-DED aluminum 
</p>
<p>6061-RAM2, D) Examples of channels with 1.5 mm wall thickness in A6061-
</p>
<p>RAM2. From Ref. [6,7] </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>While there are many applications of heat exchangers, one of the most extreme 
</p>
<p>environments they are used within is liquid rocket engines. In a liquid rocket engine 
</p>
<p>system, the functionality of the overall system relies critically on heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>components, specifically, the regeneratively-cooled or channel-cooled combustion 
</p>
<p>chamber and nozzle. These system considerations involve a complex balance 
</p>
<p>between component pressure drop, temperature rise, mass, cost, and reliability, in 
</p>
<p>addition to other technical and programmatic metrics [8,9]. While the combustion 
</p>
<p>chamber and nozzle direct the hot combustion products to create thrust, they 
</p>
<p>incorporate integral channels to allow for active cooling of the internal surface to 
</p>
<p>maintain adequate structural margins to prevent failure [10]. These internal channels 
</p>
<p>act as a heat exchanger between the hot combustion gases and propellant being used 
</p>
<p>for active cooling to increase the performance of the thermodynamic cycle. Heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers allow for performance optimization through increases or decreases in 
</p>
<p>fluid temperature, or through the reduction of structural wall temperatures through 
</p>
<p>active cooling for high heat flux environments [1]. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.2 TRADITIONAL MANUFACTURING 
</p>
<p>METHODS 
</p>
<p>Compact heat exchangers are designed and realized using tubes, micro-channels, 
</p>
<p>passages of various shapes, or fins to increase the surface area and require thin-walls 
</p>
<p>(&lt; 2 mm) and compact form factors to minimize overall mass. While complex designs 
</p>
<p>can be conceptualized and analyzed, the heat exchanger must be manufacturable with 
</p>
<p>tight tolerances to contain high pressure fluids, which often limit the design space. In 
</p>
<p>addition to meeting technical performance and manufacturing requirements, 
</p>
<p>programmatic requirements (cost, schedule, reliability, maintainability, risk 
</p>
<p>tolerance) must also be considered to ensure that a part is optimized for integration 
</p>
<p>and use in the overall system. Given that the environments that these propulsion 
</p>
<p>components must endure can be challenging or even extreme, the manufacturing to 
</p>
<p>meet these specifications often require long lead times due to specialty processes and 
</p>
<p>unique tooling associated with the combined thin-wall integral channel and large-
</p>
<p>scale structures. 
</p>
<p>Traditional manufacturing technologies for actively-cooled combustion 
</p>
<p>chambers and nozzle heat exchangers include either tube-wall fabrication or channel-
</p>
<p>wall fabrication methods [11]. Tube-wall regeneratively-cooled components use a 
</p>
<p>series of individual tubes that are bonded together by brazing or other joining methods 
</p>
<p>in order to form the complete structure (Figure 1.2) [12]. Channel-wall manufacturing 
</p>
<p>methods use an internal liner with slotted channels that are closed out using plating, 
</p>
<p>brazing, laser welding or other bonding methods [13]. This forms the channels that 
</p>
<p>contain the high pressure fluid [14]. With either of the fabrication methods, a series 
</p>
<p>of manifolds distributes the fluid (fuel or oxidizer) to the individual channels or tubes. 
</p>
<p>Alternate methods of cooling include film cooling through slots or transpiration 
</p>
<p>cooling, but dependent on the engine cycle selected [12].  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.2. Schematic cross-section of cooling channels for actively-cooled 
</p>
<p>chambers or nozzles which are different, depending on the adopted 
</p>
<p>manufacturing method. From Ref. [15]. 
</p>
<p>Various manufacturing techniques for fabrication of combustion chambers and 
</p>
<p>nozzles have been evaluated and matured. An overview of the various steps to 
</p>
<p>produce a traditional channel-wall component, including the liner perform, formation 
</p>
<p>of the coolant channels, closeout of the coolant channels, and assembly of the 
</p>
<p>manifolds, are illustrated in Figure 1.3 [13]. The traditional manufacturing process 
</p>
<p>includes brazing, joining (welding), and plating. These techniques are actively used 
</p>
<p>for production of flight components and can produce cooling channels capable of 
</p>
<p>containing the high pressure propellants [16–18]. Several advanced techniques, some 
</p>
<p>of which achieved flight maturity, include pressure-assisted hot isostatic pressing 
</p>
<p>(HIP) bonding, vacuum plasma spraying (VPS), and laser welding of sandwich wall 
</p>
<p>nozzles [19,20]. These novel techniques have proven advantageous over the more 
</p>
<p>traditional brazing and plating processes.  
</p>
<p>Pressure-assisted HIP bonding has been used to improve bonding between the 
</p>
<p>liner and structural jacket. This process uses pressure to deform the liner into close 
</p>
<p>contact with the jacket and the brazing foil is melted to create the bond [21]. This 
</p>
<p>approach has successfully reduced the amount of scrapped units and established a 
</p>
<p>robust metallurgical bond. However, its limitations include the need for significant 
</p>
<p>tooling, experimental development for heating and cooling rates of the HIP cycle, 
</p>
<p>and detailed models to predict liner deformation for determining appropriate brazing 
</p>
<p>gaps. VPS has been utilized for the closeout of chambers and nozzles by depositing 
</p>
<p>material onto a slotted liner forming the closeout of the channels which must resist to 
</p>
<p>the pressure of the propellant. This method improved the strength of the bond 
</p>
<p>between the liner and jacket, but the channels must be filled with a sacrificial 
</p>
<p>material, which is later removed. Challenges arise as the filler material needs to be 
</p>
<p>compatible with the elevated temperatures of the deposition, leading to common 
</p>
<p>blockages within channels. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The laser-welded sandwich nozzle process employs a laser welding technique to 
</p>
<p>weld each channel rib/land between the internal liner and closeout jacket. This 
</p>
<p>process actively tracks each rib seam in real-time using X-ray technology, followed 
</p>
<p>by the laser weld bead [22]. While this process automates the closeout process, it still 
</p>
<p>requires the initial forming and slotting of the inner and outer cones to ensure proper 
</p>
<p>gaps for welding. The overall process involves multiple pieces and post-processing 
</p>
<p>steps to complete a nozzle. 
</p>
<p>These traditional manufacturing techniques and their associated lifecycles 
</p>
<p>involve sequential steps and potential long-lead times for materials or processing 
</p>
<p>(e.g., forging, liner machining, slotting, closeout, etc.), which in turn introduce the 
</p>
<p>possibility of issues [15,23]. Despite advancements, these methods still necessitate 
</p>
<p>sheet forming or forgings, tight-tolerance machining, slotting of coolant channels, 
</p>
<p>and assembly operations to ensure leak-free joints. The extensive tooling required for 
</p>
<p>these processes often causes higher costs than the actual components being built. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.3. Process steps and fabrication options for channel wall nozzles and 
</p>
<p>combustion chambers of rocket engines [13]. 
</p>
<p>Manufacturing technologies are continually being developed to meet the need 
</p>
<p>for higher performance components in terms of increased efficiency and reliability, 
</p>
<p>decreased cost, decreased mass, or increased production rates [17,24,25]. Several 
</p>
<p>designs have been conceived, only to discover that certain parts cannot be fabricated, 
</p>
<p>that manufacturing limitations result in unintended performance consequences, or 
</p>
<p>that the manufacturing cost is prohibitively high. All manufacturing processes are 
</p>
<p>affected by limitations which in turn have repercussions on the entire lifecycle, 
</p>
<p>therefore they must be well understood and solutions for these limitations must be </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>incorporated into the design at an early stage [26]. Therefore, when a manufacturing 
</p>
<p>process is being industrialized, it must be fully characterized to determine limits, 
</p>
<p>tolerances, repeatability, and reproducibility to allow designers to achieve optimal 
</p>
<p>solutions [27]. Once a manufacturing process has been baselined, adjustments or 
</p>
<p>improvements can be pursued to further optimize it, like, for example, the inclusion 
</p>
<p>of secondary process steps, if gaps were identified.  
</p>
<p>These manufacturing methods are complex because of the extremely tight 
</p>
<p>tolerances required to form fluid passages that are leak-free. The surface texture 
</p>
<p>inside these small cross-sectional area channels is extremely critical as it is directly 
</p>
<p>related to the heat transfer and fluid dynamic performance of the component [28]. 
</p>
<p>Traditional manufacturing methods, such as milling, allow for a roughness of the 
</p>
<p>channels surfaces that is typically less than 3.2 µm [29]. All these system and 
</p>
<p>component level considerations must be accounted for in the design and then 
</p>
<p>translated into manufacturing requirements that can meet the design intent once 
</p>
<p>fabrication is completed. The fabrication and manufacturing of the complex features 
</p>
<p>in these components is extremely challenging and as complexity increases so does 
</p>
<p>cost [30].  These consideration put into evidence an opportunity to develop and 
</p>
<p>advance new manufacturing techniques for extreme-environment heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>aimed at improving the following attributes [13]: 
</p>
<p>• Thinner hotwalls and ribs for effective cooling in a high heat flux 
</p>
<p>environment. 
</p>
<p>• Complex internal features, or channels, to allow for increased fluid dynamic 
</p>
<p>and heat transfer performance. 
</p>
<p>• Tailoring the surface texture (roughness) and optimized channel geometry 
</p>
<p>to balance pressure drop and heat transfer effectiveness. 
</p>
<p>• Ability to eliminate joints or produce robust joints at increased bond 
</p>
<p>temperatures. 
</p>
<p>• Ability to inspect the internal channel features and quality of the material. 
</p>
<p>• Reduction or elimination of non-recurring tooling.  
</p>
<p>• Reduction in assembly build hours and elimination of manual processing. 
</p>
<p>• Reduction in lead time for materials or processes. 
</p>
<p>• Provide new options for materials and material combinations (i.e. 
</p>
<p>monolithic, bimetallic and multi-metallic). 
</p>
<p>• Direct build and/or simplified attachment of fluid distribution manifolds. 
</p>
<p>• Increased system performance through component mass reduction or 
</p>
<p>hydraulic performance. 
</p>
<p>• Scalable to large scale components (&gt;1 m diameter).  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.3 ADDITIVE MANUFACTURING FOR HEAT 
</p>
<p>EXCHANGERS 
</p>
<p>Additive manufacturing (AM) is gaining recognition as a viable production 
</p>
<p>technology in the aerospace industry, and particularly for thermal equipment of 
</p>
<p>propulsion systems, transitioning from a mere prototyping tool to a full flight-</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>qualified production of end-use parts and subsystems [31]. The key advantages of 
</p>
<p>AM distinguishing it from conventional manufacturing methods include the ability 
</p>
<p>to make very complex geometries, cost reduction, reduction of processing time, and 
</p>
<p>elimination of joints and consolidation of parts. AM allows for the realization of 
</p>
<p>complex part features that would be unattainable through traditional or emerging 
</p>
<p>manufacturing techniques, even more so if novel metal alloys must be employed. 
</p>
<p>Metal AM encompasses a myriad of processes, with unique benefits and 
</p>
<p>disadvantages affecting different build sizes, microstructures arising from various 
</p>
<p>melting or solid-state bonding methods, and geometries, ranging from coarse to finely 
</p>
<p>detailed [32–35]. These high-level metal AM categories include: 
</p>
<p>• powder bed fusion (PBF),  
</p>
<p>• directed energy deposition (DED), and  
</p>
<p>• solid-state (cold spray, ultrasonic additive manufacturing, additive friction 
</p>
<p>stir deposition, and binder jetting) [36]. 
</p>
<p>These metal AM processes are being developed and actively used in aerospace, 
</p>
<p>medical, and industrial applications [37]. As each AM process evolves, it becomes 
</p>
<p>imperative to comprehensively characterize the achievable geometries (features and 
</p>
<p>resolution), feedstock, microstructure, resulting properties, part size, deposition rates, 
</p>
<p>industrial maturity, post-processing requirements, and lifecycle economics associated 
</p>
<p>with each specific technology and the corresponding metal alloys [36,38–40]. Design 
</p>
<p>for additive manufacturing (DfAM) guidelines are unique for each AM process and 
</p>
<p>cannot be applied more broadly therefore using a DfAM guideline specific for one 
</p>
<p>process for another. Without a proper application of specific guidelines, an end-use 
</p>
<p>AM component may not meet the intended structural, thermal, dynamic, fluid flow, 
</p>
<p>or life requirements [41]. Although AM offers significant design complexity, certain 
</p>
<p>guidelines must still be followed to ensure that parts are correctly built and processed 
</p>
<p>for their final application.  Most AM research on PBF and DED processes focuses on 
</p>
<p>microstructure and related mechanical and thermophysical properties. There is 
</p>
<p>limited research on the development of DfAM guidelines for these processes, 
</p>
<p>therefore on how these processes relate to geometry capabilities and limitations along 
</p>
<p>with their impact on component and system performance [42–45].  
</p>
<p>Laser Powder Bed Fusion (L-PBF) is one of the most widely adopted AM 
</p>
<p>processes, providing the ability to fabricate thin-walls (0.2 to 0.4 mm), small passages 
</p>
<p>and channels (0.2 mm diameter), and complex shapes [46–48]. L-PBF is conducted 
</p>
<p>within an inert build box, initiated with the uniform distribution of powder feedstock 
</p>
<p>on a build plate (Figure 1.4). The laser melts the material within a layer whose 
</p>
<p>thickness ranges from 30 to 100 µm, following a specified scan path derived from the 
</p>
<p>CAD model geometry sliced into layers. Inert gas is blown from the side of the build 
</p>
<p>plate to remove particle ejecta, minimizing the contamination of the material forming 
</p>
<p>the part being built. Subsequently, the recoater arm disperses a fresh layer of powder, 
</p>
<p>and this cyclic process is repeated numerous times (i.e., hundreds or thousands) until 
</p>
<p>completion of the part. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.4. Schematic representation of a Laser Powder Bed Fusion (L-PBF) 
</p>
<p>System (from Ref. [49]) and exemplary photograph of a part being built with 
</p>
<p>L-PBF (image courtesy of NASA). 
</p>
<p>The ability to manufacture very complexity parts makes L-PBF an ideal 
</p>
<p>manufacturing process for the production of heat exchangers used in aerospace 
</p>
<p>vehicles, power plants and energy storage systems, electronics cooling systems, 
</p>
<p>chemical plants, and oil and gas plants [50]. L-PBF has reached a good state of </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—9 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>maturity (i.e., high technology readiness level) and is widely available and actively 
</p>
<p>employed in commercial and military aviation and spaceflight. Several integral-
</p>
<p>channel combustion chambers manufactured with L-PBF equipping propulsion 
</p>
<p>systems from Rocket Lab, SpaceX, Relativity Space, ABL Space Systems, Ursa 
</p>
<p>Major, and Aerojet Rocketdyne have successfully flown [26,51]. Commercial space 
</p>
<p>companies worldwide, the National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA), 
</p>
<p>European Space Agency (ESA), Japanese Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA) 
</p>
<p>and several other organizations and academic institutions have tested combustion 
</p>
<p>chambers and nozzles made with L-PBF on the ground [51–54]. Figure 1.5 shows 
</p>
<p>several examples of L-PBF combustion chambers. 
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—10 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.5. Examples of various integral channel combustion chambers and 
</p>
<p>nozzles manufactured with L-PBF and tested by NASA. A) Various thrust-
</p>
<p>class GRCop-42 and bimetallic AM combustion chambers, B) Liquid Oxygen 
</p>
<p>(LOX)/Liquid Methane (LCH4) Testing of 31 kN GRCop-42 Chamber and 
</p>
<p>LP-DED nozzle, C) LOX/Liquid Hydrogen (LH2) Testing of 111 kN L-
</p>
<p>PBF/DED Bimetallic Chamber, D) LOX/RP-1 10kN Bimetallic L-PBF 
</p>
<p>Chamber, E) LOX/LH2 150 kN Testing of Bimetallic L-PBF/DED Chamber 
</p>
<p>and LP-DED nozzle (Courtesy: NASA)  [26,52,55–57] </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—11 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Research related to additive manufacturing for heat exchangers has grown 
</p>
<p>significantly starting from 2006.  In an initial study conducted in 2006, L-PBF was 
</p>
<p>employed with a primary focus on the manufacturing of cooling systems for 
</p>
<p>electronic components [58]. Research continued in diverse areas, and a notable study 
</p>
<p>by Lu et al. documents the evaluation of the flow performance of micro-scale coolant 
</p>
<p>channels with a 2:1 aspect ratio made with L-PBF and metal micromolding [28]. The 
</p>
<p>predominate use of AM microchannels (&lt; 3 mm) has focused on the L-PBF process 
</p>
<p>[59–62].  
</p>
<p>While L-PBF has been shown to be of great benefit for the fine feature 
</p>
<p>manufacturing of ultra-compact heat exchangers, its main limitation is the maximum 
</p>
<p>volume of the component that can be built [63,64]. This size limitation is due to 
</p>
<p>maximum dimensions of the (powder filled) build box, which typically must be less 
</p>
<p>than 400 mm in each direction (X, Y, Z) and to the maximum build diameter which 
</p>
<p>is 600 mm [26,36]. Custom machines capable of dealing with up to 1 m build 
</p>
<p>diameter and 1.2 m in height have been developed, but they are not readily available 
</p>
<p>in industry. The manufacturing of compact heat exchangers for aerospace propulsion 
</p>
<p>systems and especially those of large rocket engines requires the capability to handle 
</p>
<p>large volumes, given the growing need to fulfill ever increasing thrust requirements 
</p>
<p>and therefore larger dimensions. For engines and combustion chambers produced 
</p>
<p>using L-PBF, the thrust class is generally limited to 1,100 kN. For components, such 
</p>
<p>as regeneratively-cooled nozzles, which are inherently larger (600 mm diameter), 
</p>
<p>directed energy deposition (DED) is a suitable manufacturing technology [5,36]. 
</p>
<p>Figure 1.6 shows a comparison of various AM processes in terms of build volumes 
</p>
<p>and minimum feature sizes. Heat exchangers for power and propulsion applications 
</p>
<p>require obtaining thin metal walls for the flow channels (typically below 1.5 mm). 
</p>
<p>Zhang et al. provided a comprehensive report on emerging AM processes for 
</p>
<p>heat exchangers but did not acknowledge the possibility of employing directed 
</p>
<p>energy deposition (DED) techniques [65]. Despite several authors have published 
</p>
<p>extensive review papers on the potential of AM for heat exchangers, none of them 
</p>
<p>has identified the DED process as suitable for this purpose [63,64,66–69].  Careri et 
</p>
<p>al., in a review article on heat exchangers [70], explicitly state that DED is not 
</p>
<p>suitable due to the poor surface finishing that can be obtained, its inability to make 
</p>
<p>thin features, and its low dimensional accuracy. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—12 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.6. Comparison of AM processes considering feature size and build 
</p>
<p>diameter. The green band groups AM processes that can produce wall 
</p>
<p>thicknesses below 1.5 mm, while the yellow and orange bands indicate AM 
</p>
<p>techniques that may require additional processing. 
</p>
<p>While AM offers numerous technical and programmatic advantages, it also 
</p>
<p>presents several challenges that necessitate further research. Technical challenges 
</p>
<p>include high surface texture, distortion, residual stresses, mechanical and geometric 
</p>
<p>variations across different AM processes and machines, alloy weldability issues, 
</p>
<p>limited process capability data, and insufficient mechanical and flow performance 
</p>
<p>data [71]. Programmatic challenges encompass limited production volumes, potential 
</p>
<p>risks associated with new technology, the need for training and education throughout 
</p>
<p>the AM lifecycle, significant capital investments, and the cultural shift required for 
</p>
<p>industry adoption of the technology. 
</p>
<p>To mature AM, it is essential to characterize each of the AM processes and the 
</p>
<p>specific alloys of interest to generate data relevant to industrial applications. Unlike 
</p>
<p>traditional manufacturing technologies, which have benefited from decades and 
</p>
<p>centuries of experience, AM is relatively new, resulting in limited materials and 
</p>
<p>process data. While thousands of metal alloys have been optimized for traditional 
</p>
<p>manufacturing techniques, fewer than 100 alloys have been refined for AM processes 
</p>
<p>and less than 10 qualified for flight applications [72]. This challenge is further 
</p>
<p>compounded by the unique microstructures and properties produced by each AM </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—13 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>process, as well as the process limitations related to specific alloys and geometries 
</p>
<p>required for end-use parts [36].  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.4 THE LP-DED PROCESS  
</p>
<p>LP-DED (Figure 1.7) is a process in which a laser beam is directed to a powder 
</p>
<p>stream which is blown in its focus forming a melt pool which subsequently solidifies 
</p>
<p>thus forming the part by deposition of successive layers [73]. The blown powder 
</p>
<p>deposition head and the laser beam optics are mounted on a gantry (or robot) to allow 
</p>
<p>for the precise motion control needed to manufacture thin-wall features [74]. The 
</p>
<p>laser is typically central to the integrated deposition head and the powder being blown 
</p>
<p>is concentric with the laser. The blown powder deposition nozzles can be located in 
</p>
<p>a coaxial annular ring or they are separate nozzles, typically 3 or 4, spaced equally 
</p>
<p>around the laser beam [75].  
</p>
<p>The LP-DED process allows large scale fabrication (&gt;3 meters) using metal 
</p>
<p>powder feedstock, only limited by the robotic or Cartesian gantry system [76]. 
</p>
<p>Freeform structures are fabricated based on toolpaths generated from CAD models. 
</p>
<p>The size of the powder particles and particle size distributions (PSD) can vary from 
</p>
<p>coarse (45 – 105 µm) to fine (15 – 45 µm) [75]. Examples of LP-DED applications 
</p>
<p>include casting and forging replacements, cladding and repair, fabrication of primary 
</p>
<p>structures, as well as thin-wall large diameter structures such as heat exchangers and 
</p>
<p>rocket nozzles with integral channels [5,77]. However, the manufacturing of thin-
</p>
<p>wall components with integral channels with LP-DED is at a low maturity level: the 
</p>
<p>characterization of the process is limited and DfAM practices are not established. 
</p>
<p>As indicated in Figure 1.6, Laser Powder DED (LP-DED) can overcome the 
</p>
<p>limitation regarding overall object size affecting L-PBF, while still allowing to 
</p>
<p>incorporate thin-wall (1 mm) internal flow passages or microchannels into the part 
</p>
<p>[13,78].  
</p>
<p>The work reported in this dissertation provides design engineers with 
</p>
<p>information about process limitations, design guidelines, post-processing 
</p>
<p>requirements, and experimental test data related to the manufacturing of 
</p>
<p>microchannels with LP-DED.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—14 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.7. Laser Powder DED System. A) Large scale integral channel nozzle 
</p>
<p>(&gt; 1 m diameter) during fabrication, B) Schematic overview of the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>system with its components [51], C) Metal powder flow blown through a three-
</p>
<p>nozzle configuration (From Zhong [79]; CC BY doi.org/10.3390/met7100443), 
</p>
<p>D) Macroscopic view of metal powder blown into the melt pool and excess 
</p>
<p>powder (Image courtesy of Formalloy, Ref. [49]). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.5 POTENTIAL AND CHALLENGES OF LP-DED 
</p>
<p>Contrary to L-PBF, for which extensive research about the design and 
</p>
<p>manufacturing of thin-wall features has been performed [80–82], significant research 
</p>
<p>gaps in the detailed characterization of the LP-DED process affect its more 
</p>
<p>widespread adoption. These gaps encompass surface texture characterization, thin-
</p>
<p>wall geometry feasibility, repeatability, build angle limitations assessment, wall 
</p>
<p>thickness control, and the characterization of the microstructure (including density) 
</p>
<p>of the material of thin-wall components. Notably, the melt pool generated by LP-</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—15 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>DED is considerably larger (approximately 1 mm) compared to that generated with 
</p>
<p>the L-PBF process (~120 µm), and it is determined by the laser spot size [83,84]. Due 
</p>
<p>to this larger melt pool, the formation of thin walls necessitates a single-pass bead, 
</p>
<p>precluding the use of a contour pass—a method employed in L-PBF to enhance 
</p>
<p>surface finishing [85].  
</p>
<p>Few fundamental studies on material microstructure have been conducted on 
</p>
<p>thin-walls (&lt;3 mm) produced using the LP-DED process, however these studies do 
</p>
<p>not yield sufficient data to enable the design of microchannels. Liu and Li 
</p>
<p>demonstrated the feasibility of thin-walls in Steel 24 for a thickness down to 0.8 mm, 
</p>
<p>employing a coaxial deposition head and 45-80 µm sized powder [86]. Margerit et 
</p>
<p>al. characterized the microstructure of stainless steel 316L thin wall obtained using a 
</p>
<p>coaxial deposition head with a 0.2 mm layer thickness and a wall thickness of 0.8 
</p>
<p>mm [87]. Other studies on 316L as a build material focused on microstructure and 
</p>
<p>mechanical properties and were conducted by various researchers who investigated 
</p>
<p>samples with wall thickness ranging from 0.7 to 2.1 mm [88–91]. The evaluation of 
</p>
<p>superalloy Inconel 718 as a build material for thin-wall components was also 
</p>
<p>completed. The experiments concerned single-track wall thicknesses ranging from 
</p>
<p>1.3 to 2.5 mm [92–94]. Additional research on the use of LP-DED for the 
</p>
<p>manufacturing of thin-wall components referenced in literature include the adoption 
</p>
<p>of Hastelloy X as build material with wall thicknesses ranging from 1.7 to 3.2 mm 
</p>
<p>[95,96], Stainless Steel 410 [97], Stainless Steel 304L (1.2 mm thin-wall thickness) 
</p>
<p>[98], Titanium Ti-6Al-4V [99,100], Gamma Titanium Aluminide (1.5 mm thin-wall 
</p>
<p>thickness) [101], Inconel 625 [102], CrCoNi [103], M4 high speed Steel (1.7 mm 
</p>
<p>thin-wall thickness) [104], and Al-Mg-Mn-Sc-Zr (1.6 mm thin-wall thickness) [105]. 
</p>
<p>Most of these studies focused on parameter development and the resulting 
</p>
<p>microstructure and mechanical properties. The built specimens were often limited in 
</p>
<p>overall height, with many using single beads, and most specimens restricted to &lt;10 
</p>
<p>mm overall height. While some studies briefly mentioned surface roughness, 
</p>
<p>quantified observations were often limited (e.g., contact profilometry, visual 
</p>
<p>inspection) or not provided. 
</p>
<p>Surface texture, encompassing roughness and waviness and commonly referred 
</p>
<p>to as surface finish, stands out as a notable drawback of AM if compared to traditional 
</p>
<p>manufacturing technologies [106,107]. This texture significantly influences various 
</p>
<p>characteristics related to heat exchangers, including fluid flow regime, related heat 
</p>
<p>transfer properties, and mechanical properties. Elevated surface texture can 
</p>
<p>detrimentally impact components’ performance by diminishing fatigue resistance 
</p>
<p>and, consequently, operational lifespan. It can also increase mechanical friction and 
</p>
<p>wear, negatively influence fluid friction factors within flow channels, leading to 
</p>
<p>higher pressure drop, and enhance corrosion potential in harsh environments [108–
</p>
<p>111]. The surface roughness of L-PBF-manufactured thin-wall samples made of 
</p>
<p>various metal alloys has been extensively examined in relation to diverse industrial 
</p>
<p>applications [45,112,113]. However, detailed characterization of surface texture of 
</p>
<p>thin-wall surfaces generated with LP-DED remains scarcely documented in the 
</p>
<p>literature [114,115]. Existing studies suggest that surface texture and geometric 
</p>
<p>inaccuracies are drawbacks of the LP-DED process, emphasizing the need for a </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—16 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>deeper understanding of processing parameters to facilitate broader commercial 
</p>
<p>adoption [88,116].  
</p>
<p>Common challenges associated with the use of LP-DED for heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>manufacturing include aspects of surface finishing connected with various surface 
</p>
<p>texture components, from shape deviation (form) to excessive waviness and 
</p>
<p>roughness. Shape deviation can stem from residual stresses and resulting distortion 
</p>
<p>influenced by factors such as part geometry, alloy, build plate geometry, build 
</p>
<p>parameters, and powder feedstock. All these factors collectively affect the thermal 
</p>
<p>history, on which the final quality of the component so much depends [91,117]. 
</p>
<p>Surface waviness is frequently observed in the form of lateral periodic menisci, a 
</p>
<p>consequence of stacked deposited build layers or the stair-stepping effect [118,119]. 
</p>
<p>The identified mechanism causing roughness in LP-DED involves residual unmelted 
</p>
<p>and melted powder emanating from the melt pool, ricocheting, or excess powder not 
</p>
<p>captured in the melt pool, leading to powder agglomerations or adherence to the wall 
</p>
<p>[84]. This powder adherence can be further exacerbated with smaller spot sizes, 
</p>
<p>necessary for thin-walls, due to the low powder efficiency (5-20%) captured in the 
</p>
<p>melt pool [120,121].  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.6 SURFACE TEXTURE AND ENHANCEMENTS 
</p>
<p>FOR LP-DED  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The level of the overall surface finish or roughness, as it is  often referred to, is 
</p>
<p>identified as one of the key issues affecting the use of additively manufactured parts, 
</p>
<p>and in particular for heat exchangers [122]. Azarniya et al. offer a similar perspective 
</p>
<p>to Careri et al. and identified both the surface finish and geometric inaccuracies as 
</p>
<p>drawbacks specifically of the LP-DED process [116]: post-processing operations 
</p>
<p>may be necessary, which could increase the production lead time and cost, thus 
</p>
<p>surface finish  “remains a major challenge that requires additional developments.” 
</p>
<p>Alsulami  mentions the DED process in the literature review of one of his articles, 
</p>
<p>see Ref. [69], but only in the context that it could provide the possibility of using 
</p>
<p>multiple materials within a build, thus offering additional advantages for the 
</p>
<p>manufacturing of special heat exchangers.  
</p>
<p>Several references document the knowledge gap affecting specifically the high 
</p>
<p>texture of AM surfaces [44,74,123]. Research conducted by Chua et al. shows that 
</p>
<p>no database about texture of AM surfaces exists yet, and knowledge is lacking 
</p>
<p>regarding the obtainable surface roughness [124]. Although there is extensive 
</p>
<p>discussion on AM surface roughness in various articles, such research predominantly 
</p>
<p>concentrates on its connection to fatigue. Limited information is available regarding 
</p>
<p>its impact on flow performance in case of heat exchangers [125–127]. Hanson et al. 
</p>
<p>analyzed the dependence of flow performance on surface roughness in case L-PBF is 
</p>
<p>the manufacturing technology [128]. This large effect of surface roughness is 
</p>
<p>attributed to the dimension of the irregular structures in comparison to the channel 
</p>
<p>dimensions.  Another such study is the thesis by Tommila in 2017 [129], which </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—17 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>focused on the surface roughness of a rocket nozzle’s interior hotwall at the boundary 
</p>
<p>layer and the theoretical increase in viscous losses in turbulent flows. In another study 
</p>
<p>[130], Stimpson concluded that rough channel surfaces for internal cooling resulting 
</p>
<p>from L-PBF might promote an increase in heat transfer performance and using 
</p>
<p>components in the as-built surface condition may offer benefits.  
</p>
<p>Numerous post-processing techniques have been suggested to mitigate the effect 
</p>
<p>of excessive surface roughness [131–138]. A summary of these techniques is 
</p>
<p>provided in Table 1.1, based on Ref. [51]. Nevertheless, it is acknowledged that 
</p>
<p>smoothing internal surfaces can pose challenges contingent upon the geometry and 
</p>
<p>scale of the geometrical features [139]. Post-build machining is not a viable option 
</p>
<p>since it involves accessing the interiors of channels after the AM process. Hybrid 
</p>
<p>DED and integral machining is an emerging technical solution to this problem, but it 
</p>
<p>is not feasible for the size and complex geometry of heat exchangers channels because 
</p>
<p>it would create a faceted surface [140]. Shen and Fang authored a review article on 
</p>
<p>L-PBF that focused on the polishing of internal surfaces [141]. They proposed several 
</p>
<p>techniques, including abrasive flow machining, barrel finishing, magnetic abrasive 
</p>
<p>finishing, chemical polishing, electropolishing, and hydrodynamic cavitation 
</p>
<p>abrasive finishing.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 1.1. Overview of various surface enhancement techniques for AM and 
</p>
<p>their applicability to LP-DED 
</p>
<p>  
Surface enhancement applicability 
</p>
<p>for LP-DED 
</p>
<p>Process External surfaces 
Internal 
</p>
<p>surfaces 
</p>
<p>In-process smoothing (contours) – – 
</p>
<p>Chemical milling (CM) + + 
</p>
<p>Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP) + + 
</p>
<p>Abrasive flow machining + + 
</p>
<p>Media suspension methods + – 
</p>
<p>Dissolvable / surface sensitization + 0 
</p>
<p>Powder enhanced slurry plating 0 0 
</p>
<p>Electrochemical machining (ECM) or 
</p>
<p>electropolishing (EP) 
+ 0 
</p>
<p>Secondary laser polishing (in-process or post) + – 
</p>
<p>Mechanical or Manual polishing (machining, 
</p>
<p>honing, buffing, burnishing) 
+ – 
</p>
<p>Grit or Bead blasting + – 
</p>
<p>Thermal deburring – – 
</p>
<p>Coatings + 0 
</p>
<p>Peening methods + – 
</p>
<p>Magnetic abrasive finishing (MAF) 0 + 
aNote: +, potential for maturation and/or demonstrated; –, affected by significant challenges; 
</p>
<p>0, unknown and further development required. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—18 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Surface enhancements, often referred to as polishing or surface finishing, modify 
</p>
<p>the profile of the surface by removing partially adhered powder particles, waviness, 
</p>
<p>pores, defects in general, or other surface anomalies resulting from the AM process. 
</p>
<p>Several review papers provide overviews about surface finishing techniques for AM 
</p>
<p>parts [132,142–145]. Many mechanical and chemical processes, laser-treatments, and 
</p>
<p>coatings that modify the surface without removing material, are discussed in the 
</p>
<p>literature. These finishing processes are often used to improve the fatigue life [146]. 
</p>
<p>Many processes for finishing surfaces require line of sight and access for tooling and 
</p>
<p>are limited to external surfaces. These include machining, grinding, vibratory 
</p>
<p>finishing, grit or sand blasting, laser polishing, rolling, burnishing, peening 
</p>
<p>[133,147,148]. A limited number of surface enhancement processes are feasible or 
</p>
<p>hold potential for altering internal surfaces, and more so microchannels. These 
</p>
<p>surface enhancement techniques include chemical milling, electro or electrochemical 
</p>
<p>polishing, abrasive flow machining, chemical mechanical polishing, thermal energy 
</p>
<p>method, and self-terminating sensitization [51,146,149–151]. Other methods, like 
</p>
<p>hydrodynamic cavitation abrasive finishing [152,153] and magnetic assisted 
</p>
<p>finishing [154,155] have been studied for internal channels. However, these 
</p>
<p>approaches have limited applicability and lack industrialization. 
</p>
<p>Laser polishing is one of the few surface enhancement techniques that has been 
</p>
<p>researched for application to components produced with the LP-DED process 
</p>
<p>[148,156,157]. Laser polishing uses either the primary laser during the fabrication 
</p>
<p>process or a secondary laser as part of a subsequent step to remelt specific regions on 
</p>
<p>the surface. When performed in situ, the laser polishing process can be carried out 
</p>
<p>between layers, but it poses challenges due to the need for direct surface access, 
</p>
<p>particularly in the presence of complex geometries, including internal features like 
</p>
<p>channels. The laser parameters employed for local smoothing involve lower power, 
</p>
<p>a smaller spot size, and a higher scanning rate compared to the build laser parameters 
</p>
<p>[158]. Focused laser beams melt targeted areas, creating a liquid melt pool that levels 
</p>
<p>the surface through surface tension and gravity. The molten region solidifies rapidly, 
</p>
<p>both across the surface and into surface valleys [159]. Laser polishing has been shown 
</p>
<p>to reduce the surface roughness of LP-DED parts by 80–96% [160]. Figure 1.8 
</p>
<p>provides an example of laser polishing on an external LP-DED surface from Rosa et 
</p>
<p>al. 
 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.8. Example of laser polishing applied to a Stainless 316L LP-DED 
</p>
<p>sample [161]. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—19 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The laser remelting process has been demonstrated to generate recast layers 
</p>
<p>approximately 22 µm thick in Inconel 718 LP-DED samples [162]. However, a 
</p>
<p>notable limitation of the laser remelting polishing process is that existing research has 
</p>
<p>exclusively focused on large-area surfaces, thus neglecting its applicability to fine 
</p>
<p>feature [163]. In the context of a heat exchanger, where the internal surfaces of 
</p>
<p>channels are crucial, the significant surface area necessitates the use of multi-axis 
</p>
<p>lasing for effective smoothing.  
</p>
<p> Chemical milling (CM) and chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) are 
</p>
<p>especially suitable for internal surfaces, more so if the geometry is intricate, because 
</p>
<p>chemical solutions can flow through small and complex channels. [164]. Favero et 
</p>
<p>al. conducted a study on the CM process by using copper chloride and ferric chloride 
</p>
<p>as the chemical solution to finish the internal surface of vertically built L-PBF 
</p>
<p>channels made of pure copper and featuring various internal geometries and a wetting 
</p>
<p>length of 54 mm [165]. Their experimental findings revealed a reduction in the 
</p>
<p>average areal roughness (Sa) of 83%, with a subsequent pressure drop decrease of 
</p>
<p>78%. However, the study did not delve into a detailed discussion of the specific 
</p>
<p>mechanisms of the altered surface texture that resulted in the reduced pressure drop. 
</p>
<p>Tyagi also evaluated acid-based chemical milling to reduce the average directional 
</p>
<p>roughness (Ra) of an internal surface by 91% [134]. Limited public data on CMP or 
</p>
<p>chemical abrasive polishing exist. Mohammadian et al. demonstrated the possibility 
</p>
<p>of polishing internal wedges of Inconel 625 channels made with L-PBF using a 
</p>
<p>custom CMP setup [166]. A reduction of Ra by 44% and 18% was observed, based 
</p>
<p>on build orientations of 15° and 135°, respectively. Other literature discussed the use 
</p>
<p>of CM and CMP for surface modification of L-PBF-made channels, but 
</p>
<p>comprehensive investigations regarding these surface modifications remain limited 
</p>
<p>[131,167–172]. 
</p>
<p>Min et al. evaluated electrochemical polishing (ECP) for parts built with L-PBF 
</p>
<p>and made of Inconel 718 alloy that included 500 µm holes [173]. A study by Jiang et 
</p>
<p>al. was focused on the use of ECP to polish the inside of 3 mm diameter Hastelloy X 
</p>
<p>channels produced by means of L-PBF. The results indicated an 83% reduction in 
</p>
<p>Ra, but selective dissolution of the cellular structure and dependency on surface build 
</p>
<p>orientation was observed [174]. An et al. demonstrated the use of combined 
</p>
<p>electrochemical and mechanical polishing (ECMP) to finish the internal surface of 
</p>
<p>channels with diameters as small as 18 mm and obtained a reduction of the Sa by 
</p>
<p>79.9% [175].  One limitation of this procedure is that the geometry must be simple 
</p>
<p>enough to accommodate an electrode [138,164]. 
</p>
<p>Ferchow et al. used abrasive flow machining (AFM) to polish 6 mm diameter 
</p>
<p>internal channels obtained with L-PBF, by removing a maximum of 0.33 mm of 
</p>
<p>material and thus reducing the Ra from 27.7 to 5.5 µm [176]. In another study [177], 
</p>
<p>similar results are reported, but using AFM to improve the surface finishing of 8 mm 
</p>
<p>diameter channels made of Inconel 718 and by means of L-PBF; in this case the Ra 
</p>
<p>was reduced by 93%. Han et al. researched the application of AFM to polish 3 mm 
</p>
<p>diameter maraging steel channels made with L-PBF and reduced the Sa from 7.6 µm 
</p>
<p>to 1.3 µm. The peak regions were removed, but some valleys still remained [178]. 
</p>
<p>Additional studies about the use of AFM for the surface finishing of internal channels </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—20 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>were conducted and all concluded that the removal of surface peaks was prevalent 
</p>
<p>but valleys remained [144]. 
</p>
<p>The surface enhancement methods for internal channels detailed here were tested 
</p>
<p>only on parts manufactured with L-PBF, while the use of these surface finishing 
</p>
<p>technologies in association with parts obtained with the LP-DED process is not 
</p>
<p>documented. Researchers have noted that geometry, AM processing, feedstock, 
</p>
<p>microstructure, and heat treatments can all effect post-processing such as surface 
</p>
<p>finishing [179]. Detailed conclusions about surface finishing methods cannot be 
</p>
<p>generalized to different AM processes, alloys, and geometries. If the level of finishing 
</p>
<p>of internal surfaces of microchannels obtained with AM is insufficient to attain the 
</p>
<p>desired flow resistance or fatigue life, then an additional finishing process is required 
</p>
<p>to modify the surface geometry and roughness and to remove defects. However, 
</p>
<p>detailed research must be conducted for each AM process of interest, including LP-
</p>
<p>DED, as discussed in this thesis.   
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>1.7 AIM OF THE RESEARCH 
</p>
<p>Using the LP-DED process to manufacture microchannel heat exchangers, 
</p>
<p>especially for extreme environment applications, introduces challenges related to 
</p>
<p>consistent geometry, wall thickness, and high surface texture. While traditional 
</p>
<p>manufacturing methods like machining do not typically have issues with surface 
</p>
<p>roughness and texture, adopting AM for heat exchangers presents new challenges. 
</p>
<p>These require innovative design or manufacturing solutions, as well as thorough 
</p>
<p>research and characterization. Although the fundamental physics of heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>operation remain unchanged, the application of AM technology offers new design 
</p>
<p>opportunities to optimize performance. Surface texture, in particular, emerges as a 
</p>
<p>significant challenge that demands careful consideration and modifications to meet 
</p>
<p>design intent. The design assumption of ideally smooth surfaces is inappropriate for 
</p>
<p>LP-DED, as the actual component differs significantly from its design representation 
</p>
<p>(Figure 1.9). While the obtainable surface texture may initially seem like a drawback 
</p>
<p>for microchannels, it also presents an opportunity. Surface enhancements, (i.e., 
</p>
<p>finishing processes) can be fine-tuned to optimize fluid flow across the 
</p>
<p>microchannels, thereby optimizing flow resistance and heat transfer depending on the 
</p>
<p>application. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—21 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.9. Indicative comparison between the idealized representation of a 
</p>
<p>micro-channel typical of design models and the image of the actual object 
</p>
<p>manufactured with the LP-DED process as obtained from a nano-Computed 
</p>
<p>Tomography (CT) scan. 
</p>
<p>The results suggest that the LP-DED process can be effectively used to fabricate 
</p>
<p>heat exchangers with integral micro-channels, yielding products of comparable 
</p>
<p>quality and performance to those made with traditional fabrication techniques. This 
</p>
<p>research aims to comprehensively characterize and evaluate microchannel fluid flow 
</p>
<p>phenomena and assess the influence of the LP-DED manufacturing process, 
</p>
<p>including surface modifications. The research thoroughly examines each process 
</p>
<p>step, linking it to fundamental process inputs. Detailed surface characterization, 
</p>
<p>analysis, and testing of heat exchanger channel samples with both as-produced (i.e., 
</p>
<p>as-built) and modified surface textures are conducted to evaluate flow resistance in 
</p>
<p>relation to inner surface characteristics. 
</p>
<p>To achieve this, surface texture – encompassing form, waviness, and roughness 
</p>
<p>– resulting from the LP-DED process and post-processing enhancements have been 
</p>
<p>measured with state-of-the-art techniques. The correlation between surface texture 
</p>
<p>and fluid flow performance was established, demonstrating that LP-DED-made heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger configurations can be obtained by adjusting parameters, or post-processing 
</p>
<p>for specific design requirements. The fundamental aspects of the build process were 
</p>
<p>evaluated, including process parameters and feedstock inputs, microchannel design 
</p>
<p>and geometric limitations, and variations in post-processing techniques that affect 
</p>
<p>channel flow resistance. Figure 1.10 outlines the research steps, emphasizing the 
</p>
<p>variation in surface texture at each stage and its impact on flow, allowing for 
</p>
<p>appropriate surface finish modifications.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—22 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 1.10. Sequential phases of the LP-DED microchannel fabrication 
</p>
<p>process and related studies on surface texture aspects.  
</p>
<p>1.8 THESIS CONTENT 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2 outlines the gaps in the current state of LP-DED microchannel process 
</p>
<p>development, introduces the research questions from which the research documented 
</p>
<p>in this dissertation stems, and details the general methodology following the 
</p>
<p>fabrication aspects presented in Figure 1.10. 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3 delves into LP-DED build process mechanics, build parameters, and 
</p>
<p>their influence on thin-wall surface texture. A literature review illustrates the state of 
</p>
<p>knowledge regarding the use of LP-DED to obtain parts with thin-walls. The 
</p>
<p>sensitivity of the surface texture, porosity, and wall thickness based on changes in 
</p>
<p>process parameters and powder feedstock size is established through a design of 
</p>
<p>experiments (DOE). This chapter is based on a peer-reviewed journal article. 
</p>
<p>In Chapter 4, the scope of the research is expanded by characterizing the effect 
</p>
<p>of the build angle on thin-wall samples. This establishes a baseline for LP-DED 
</p>
<p>Design for Additive Manufacturing (DfAM) guidelines, addressing process 
</p>
<p>limitations, surface texture, and wall thickness. The characterization of surface 
</p>
<p>texture built at various angles is important for practical applications to heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>designs including disparities in texture for internal and external surfaces. A literature 
</p>
<p>review is also provided, highlighting the gaps related to LP-DED build constraints 
</p>
<p>including wall thickness and geometric characteristics. This chapter is also based on 
</p>
<p>a peer-reviewed journal article. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1—23 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5 provides an overview of microchannel heat exchanger geometry 
</p>
<p>characterization. Based on a conference paper presented at the International 
</p>
<p>Astronautical Congress (IAC), it covers variations in square microchannel sizes, 
</p>
<p>internal and external surface texture, wall thickness, and process repeatability. 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6 focuses on post-process surface enhancements applied to LP-DED-
</p>
<p>produced internal microchannels. This chapter evaluates five different surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement processes (i.e. polishing) and presents results from flow testing to 
</p>
<p>determine flow resistance in terms of discharge coefficients. These results aided in 
</p>
<p>identifying the underlying surface texture mechanisms that affect flow resistance in 
</p>
<p>the microchannels. This chapter is based on a peer-reviewed journal article. 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7 presents a discussion based on the data gathered from all the prior 
</p>
<p>chapters and summarizes experimental results and analysis on 700 mm long LP-DED 
</p>
<p>microchannels modified by means of surface enhancements. This experiment focused 
</p>
<p>on differential pressure measurements to determine the effect of surface texture from 
</p>
<p>the surface enhancements and impact on friction factors and the different surface 
</p>
<p>mechanisms that cause the changes in friction factors. The results from the 
</p>
<p>experiments are discussed and correlations presented.  
</p>
<p>Finally, Chapter 8 offers brief discussions and conclusions, relating findings to 
</p>
<p>original research questions. It also outlines potential applications and future research 
</p>
<p>directions to expand the work. 
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—24 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>2 Research Questions and 
</p>
<p>Methodology 
</p>
<p>2.1 GAPS IN KNOWLEDGE 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Additive manufacturing has matured as a fabrication technology for heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers, but currently almost all research, development and deployment efforts 
</p>
<p>are dedicated to the L-PBF technique, as discussed in Chapter 1. However, the L-
</p>
<p>PBF process is affected by several relevant constraints, above all the maximum 
</p>
<p>component size, and LP-DED is a solution to overcome these limitations, provided 
</p>
<p>that further research and development are carried out. 
</p>
<p> In addition to the comprehensive literature review presented in Chapter 1, 
</p>
<p>Chapters 3, 4, 6, and 7 provide additional discussions on the state of the art, 
</p>
<p>specifically related to the LP-DED process. Through this extensive review of current 
</p>
<p>knowledge, several research gaps have been discerned, paving the way for further 
</p>
<p>exploration: 
</p>
<p>1. The application of LP-DED to thin-wall (&lt;1.5 mm) heat exchangers. 
</p>
<p>2. The detailed evaluation of surface texture resulting from the application of 
</p>
<p>the LP-DED process. 
</p>
<p>3. The lack of characterization of thin-walls combined with surface texture for 
</p>
<p>enclosed shapes or microchannels. 
</p>
<p>4. The inadequate investigation of post-build processes like surface 
</p>
<p>enhancements to improve finishing, particularly of the inside of 
</p>
<p>microchannels. 
</p>
<p>5. Insufficient characterization of surface texture throughout the lifecycle of 
</p>
<p>the LP-DED fabrication process, particularly microchannels. 
</p>
<p>6. The modification of the internal LP-DED surfaces to tune the flow 
</p>
<p>resistance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Thin walls are a crucial feature in extreme environment heat exchangers allowing 
</p>
<p>for efficient heat transfer between fluid mediums. As indicated by existing literature, 
</p>
<p>compact heat exchangers require wall thickness ranging from 0.5 to 3 mm, depending 
</p>
<p>on the environmental conditions and anticipated service life [180–182]. However, the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED technique is limited regarding the minimum wall thickness that can be 
</p>
<p>fabricated because the laser spot size and subsequent melt pool cannot be smaller than 
</p>
<p>a certain value and the fabrication time considerably increases with the thinness of 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—25 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>walls. Although wall thicknesses as thin as 0.5 mm have been demonstrated, this 
</p>
<p>approach significantly extends fabrication time, increases the risk of distortion, and 
</p>
<p>imposes constraints on the feasible geometries [74]. In the context of this research, a 
</p>
<p>design wall thickness of 1 mm is chosen as the lower value of all considered samples 
</p>
<p>manufactured for experimental characterization. This results in a melt pool and 
</p>
<p>realized wall thickness ranging from 1 – 1.2 mm [78,183].  
</p>
<p>Several studies, further detailed in chapter 1 and 3, were aimed at the evaluation 
</p>
<p>of surface texture resulting from the application of the LP-DED process and focused 
</p>
<p>on process parameters, deposition nozzle designs, and defect analysis, but limited to 
</p>
<p>single vertical walls. In particular, the microstructure was evaluated and analyzed. 
</p>
<p>Some works mention surface roughness, but characterization is generally limited to 
</p>
<p>average surface texture and the details specific to waviness and roughness aspects are 
</p>
<p>not discussed in detail. Any AM build process results in complex surfaces which 
</p>
<p>necessitate a detailed characterization in order to correlate surface texture with flow 
</p>
<p>performance. The average value of surface roughness is not a reliable indicator if 
</p>
<p>flow resistance and heat transfer are to be predicted.  
</p>
<p>Surface texture of AM-produced parts of heat exchanging equipment must be 
</p>
<p>characterized in terms of form, roughness, and waviness, with appropriate filtering. 
</p>
<p>A useful analogy for comprehending these surface texture attributes is to envision a 
</p>
<p>wavy potato chip (Figure 2.1). The overall potato chip exhibits macroscopic 
</p>
<p>curvature (i.e., form). To characterize the surface finish (i.e., waviness and 
</p>
<p>roughness), the form (or macroscopic curvature) must be eliminated: a perfectly flat 
</p>
<p>surface can be obtain by digitally filtering out the mathematical representation of the 
</p>
<p>macroscopic shape. The resulting flat surface is referred to as the primary profile, 
</p>
<p>which results itself from the summation of both waviness and roughness (see Figure 
</p>
<p>9). For a more detailed characterization of the surface texture, the data representing 
</p>
<p>the measured profile must be further filtered using a high-pass wavelength filter, 
</p>
<p>denoted as λC, to differentiate between waviness and roughness (see Figure 2.1). 
</p>
<p>Waviness is defined as the deviation from flatness characterized by longer spatial 
</p>
<p>wavelengths, perceptible as the macroscopic "ridges" or regular periodic undulations 
</p>
<p>on the surface of the potato chip. Roughness instead refers to microscopic 
</p>
<p>irregularities on the surface, potentially arising from the deep fried or baked potato 
</p>
<p>particles or salt. To eliminate any noise from the roughness profile, a low-pass filter, 
</p>
<p>λS, may also be applied.  
</p>
<p>Surface texture analysis and quantification can be performed across the areal 
</p>
<p>surface or based on a selected directional line or lines. Further attributes describing 
</p>
<p>the waviness and roughness components may include the counting of peaks and 
</p>
<p>valleys or their averages, the evaluation of core depth/height, and the calculation of 
</p>
<p>the density (occurrence per unit length) of any of the mentioned parameters. The 
</p>
<p>measurement of these features necessitates optical microscopy methods for 
</p>
<p>characterization, while the more conventional trace profilometry is insufficient. The 
</p>
<p>directional orientation of surface texture significantly influences the results. With 
</p>
<p>reference to Figure 2.1, thus to the potato chip analogy, the characterization of the 
</p>
<p>surface texture is based on a single profile perpendicular to the ridges results in a 
</p>
<p>large value of waviness. Conversely, if the profile used for characterization is parallel 
</p>
<p>to the ridges, whether on top of a ridge or within a valley, a considerably lower </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—26 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>waviness is obtained, with roughness becoming comparatively higher. This 
</p>
<p>distinction is critical, particularly in the context of fluid flow, as the build direction 
</p>
<p>may play a significant role in influencing the flow characteristics. This research 
</p>
<p>specifically concentrates on surface texture resulting from the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>manufacturing process, an in particular on components made of specialized Iron-
</p>
<p>Nickel-Chromium alloy known as NASA HR-1. Emphasis is placed on the surface 
</p>
<p>texture characterization of both thin-walls and enclosed shapes, particularly those 
</p>
<p>encompassing microchannels. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 2.1. Schematic visualization of the attributes of surface texture (form, 
</p>
<p>waviness and roughness): example of a wavy potato chip. 
</p>
<p>Channels in extreme environment heat exchangers can vary, but are typically 
</p>
<p>measuring less than 4 mm (per side), depending on the design requirements and 
</p>
<p>intended operating environments [184,185]. This experimental research specifically 
</p>
<p>targets square microchannels, a common feature in large extreme environment heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers [186]. Figure 2.2 shows an example of such a LP-DED-made row of 
</p>
<p>channels, featuring a 1 mm wall thickness and 2.5 mm x 2.5 mm channel cross-
</p>
<p>section. The absence of literature related to LP-DED-made thin-wall channels 
</p>
<p>underscores a notable gap in existing research. A detailed characterization of both the 
</p>
<p>internal and external surfaces of these channels addresses this key research gap, 
</p>
<p>laying the groundwork for subsequent investigations into surface enhancements and 
</p>
<p>flow experiments.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—27 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 2.2. Example of typical channel that will be focus of the proposed 
</p>
<p>research using the LP-DED process (Channel details shown in Chapter 5). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The cross-section of a channels row displayed in Figure 2.2 reveals irregularities 
</p>
<p>not only in the surface texture but also in the ribs/lands between the microchannels 
</p>
<p>(see Figure 1.2 for terminology). To address the identified gap, optical techniques 
</p>
<p>were employed to measure both the surface texture and the overall geometry of the 
</p>
<p>channels. This comprehensive evaluation covered various channel sizes, involving 
</p>
<p>measurements and comparisons of cross-sectional areas, with particular attention to 
</p>
<p>the evaluation of repeatability. The aim was to provide a detailed quantification and 
</p>
<p>analysis of the surface features and of the geometric characteristics. 
</p>
<p>The examination of microchannels like those shown in Figure 2.2 led to 
</p>
<p>hypothesize that the surface texture may not meet the requirements for various heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger applications. A post-processing technique may be needed to modify the 
</p>
<p>surface finish to meet pressure drop requirements. However, references on internal 
</p>
<p>post-processing surface finishing techniques have only been demonstrated with the 
</p>
<p>L-PBF process, with none related to the LP-DED process. This lack of information 
</p>
<p>affect the possibility of properly designing heat exchanger because it is the impossible 
</p>
<p>to accurately flow performance, which is an essential merit parameter for this 
</p>
<p>equipment. Another significant knowledge gap is therefore the development and 
</p>
<p>characterization of surface enhancement methods tailored to the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>manufacturing process. 
</p>
<p>The effective integration of LP-DED technology into the manufacturing of heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers for extreme environments requires comprehensive characterization and 
</p>
<p>evaluation throughout the entire component lifecycle, not just a single process step 
</p>
<p>[187].  Users of this technology must understand not only the build process but also 
</p>
<p>the inputs and outputs of the process including post-processing operations, such as 
</p>
<p>heat treatment and surface finishing. This includes understanding how a heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger design integrates into the overall system and how both component and 
</p>
<p>system sensitivities respond to changes in characteristics like surface texture. The 
</p>
<p>research documented in this dissertation prioritizes a systems-level comprehension 
</p>
<p>of surface texture from the conceptual and detailed design stages through 
</p>
<p>manufacturing and operation (i.e., lifecycle). This requires experimental data and 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—28 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>model validation to address all identified knowledge gaps. Therefore, the primary 
</p>
<p>focus of this research is on thin-wall microchannels post-processed using various 
</p>
<p>surface enhancement techniques, followed by flow testing to determine the impact on 
</p>
<p>friction factors. 
</p>
<p>2.2 SURFACE TEXTURE AS A SYSTEMS 
</p>
<p>ENGINEERING CHALLENGE 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Compact heat exchanger performance can vary significantly due to pressure 
</p>
<p>losses, changes in heat transfer, and fatigue life related to surface texture. Conceptual 
</p>
<p>designs often assume a certain surface texture, but uncertainty persists until hardware 
</p>
<p>fabrication. AM surface roughness can vary significantly from traditional methods, 
</p>
<p>with roughness up to 50 µm, compared to less than 3.2 µm achievable with machining 
</p>
<p>[29,68]. Prior studies have shown that sensitivity to surface texture can lead to 
</p>
<p>component pressure drops exceeding 70% [57]. Additionally, the texture of internal 
</p>
<p>microchannels affects fluid flow, while the outer surface influences hot gas flow and 
</p>
<p>heat load. Research indicates that AM surfaces in the as-built condition can cause 20-
</p>
<p>30% increases in heat loads [57,108,129,139]. This can offer potential system 
</p>
<p>performance advantages, such as expander engines or alternately cause potential 
</p>
<p>failures with an increase wall temperature. Effectively addressing surface texture 
</p>
<p>early during component conceptualization and systems design requires a 
</p>
<p>comprehensive understanding of the entire design and fabrication lifecycle, 
</p>
<p>particularly its integration with the intended application. The lifecycle encompasses 
</p>
<p>all steps necessary to manufacture a component, from the initial material selection, 
</p>
<p>machining, fabrication, forming, and assembly through to final validation, testing, 
</p>
<p>and system integration. 
</p>
<p>A component designer must understand potential surface conditions from 
</p>
<p>manufacturing operations to predict pressure and thermal performance accurately. 
</p>
<p>Introducing a new manufacturing method requires establishing a target surface 
</p>
<p>texture to ensure the overall system power balance is met. Performance differences 
</p>
<p>are often tied to the manufacturing process, including incorrect geometry, surface 
</p>
<p>texture, and tolerances. For instance, designers can predict pressure losses through 
</p>
<p>flow passages (i.e., channels or tubes) based on an assumed surface texture for a 
</p>
<p>specific manufacturing method. However, if pressure loss and heat load requirements 
</p>
<p>are not met, secondary manufacturing processes may be needed to modify the surface 
</p>
<p>texture, increasing manufacturing costs. 
</p>
<p>While an initial goal of AM components might be to achieve a surface finish 
</p>
<p>similar to traditional manufacturing (e.g., machining), the ultimate objective of this 
</p>
<p>research is to fine-tune the surface texture providing options to meet broader system 
</p>
<p>requirements. Understanding and measuring surface texture in AM components 
</p>
<p>immediately after the build process and throughout the lifecycle, including potential 
</p>
<p>changes during post-processing or operation, is essential [188].  Post-processing in 
</p>
<p>AM provides an opportunity to adjust surface texture and optimize heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>performance, such as increasing heat loads or cooling pressure performance [28]. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—29 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>A complete understanding of surface texture throughout the AM lifecycle is 
</p>
<p>crucial. Figure 2.3 introduces a holistic view of this lifecycle and addresses various 
</p>
<p>questions a designer may encounter. Integrating these factors into design and 
</p>
<p>manufacturing processes is key to developing effective heat exchangers for extreme 
</p>
<p>environments. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 2.3. A holistic view of surface texture throughout the AM lifecycle. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>2.3 RESEARCH QUESTIONS 
</p>
<p>This research endeavors to showcase the viability of LP-DED technology for the 
</p>
<p>production of heat exchangers with integrated microchannels. As discussed in 
</p>
<p>Chapter 1, the primary objective of this research is to attain a comprehensive 
</p>
<p>characterization of microchannel fluid flow phenomena and assess the influence 
</p>
<p>of the LP-DED manufacturing process, including surface modifications. The 
</p>
<p>tailoring of the surface texture from in-process or post-processing modifications can 
</p>
<p>allow the fluid flow to be optimized based on the specific component and system 
</p>
<p>requirements The following research questions resulting from the analysis of the 
</p>
<p>identified knowledge gaps were addressed, in addition to the listed sub-questions.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Research Question #1 (RQ1) 
</p>
<p>1. How is the surface texture of heat exchanger microchannels affected by the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED fabrication process? 
</p>
<p>a. What are the key surface texture and roughness parameters to 
</p>
<p>evaluate in order to fully characterize the component and its 
</p>
<p>performance?  
</p>
<p>b. What are the key process parameters and functional relationships 
</p>
<p>that can influence surface texture during the initial build? </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—30 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>c. How do various geometries (angles, channel sizes) affect the 
</p>
<p>surface texture? 
</p>
<p>d. How is the surface texture impacted during post-processing stages 
</p>
<p>of the fabrication after the initial build? 
</p>
<p>e. How does the obtained texture compare to the texture obtained with 
</p>
<p>traditional manufacturing technologies? 
</p>
<p>f. What changes can be made to the LP-DED technique to improve 
</p>
<p>the build process? 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Research Question #2 (RQ2) 
</p>
<p>2. What are the geometrical relationships and sensitivities affecting fluid flow 
</p>
<p>performance if heat exchanger channels are manufactured with the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process? 
</p>
<p>a. How can the fluid flow performance be evaluated using LP-DED 
</p>
<p>microchannels? 
</p>
<p>b. Can the LP-DED microchannels be used in an application in the 
</p>
<p>as-fabricated condition? 
</p>
<p>c. How can the information about microchannel surface texture be 
</p>
<p>effectively used during design and analysis? 
</p>
<p>d. What differences are observed with the as-built channel surface 
</p>
<p>texture compared to ideally smooth channels and surface 
</p>
<p>mechanisms that affect the friction factors? 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Research Question #3 (RQ3) 
</p>
<p>3. What improvements can be made to control the surface texture of thin-wall 
</p>
<p>LP-DED internal microchannels? 
</p>
<p>a. What post-processing techniques exists and can be further 
</p>
<p>developed to reduce texture? 
</p>
<p>b. To what extent can they be used to reduce or modify texture? 
</p>
<p>c. What is the level of repeatability of these texture-reducing 
</p>
<p>techniques? 
</p>
<p>d. How can the flow performance resulting from the application of 
</p>
<p>these surface improvements be characterized? 
</p>
<p>e. What differences are observed between analytical methods and 
</p>
<p>experimental flow data and how can models be improved? 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>In this dissertation, each chapter treats the research performed to address the 
</p>
<p>mentioned knowledge gaps and to answer the listed research questions. The research 
</p>
<p>outcomes are also published in several peer-reviewed journal articles and a 
</p>
<p>conference paper on which the chapters are based. Figure 2.4 visually illustrates the 
</p>
<p>chapters and their corresponding papers, highlighting the alignment between research 
</p>
<p>questions and chapter content.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—31 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 2.4. Chapter outline and associated publications that address the 
</p>
<p>research questions (RQ), see Section 2.3. 
</p>
<p>In addition, each research question directly correlates with the identified 
</p>
<p>knowledge gaps, as illustrated in Figure 2.5. </p>
