<p> 
</p>
<p>2.4 INNOVATIONS FROM THE RESEARCH AND 
</p>
<p>METHODOLOGY 
</p>
<p> Several innovations resulting from this research can help facilitate the effective 
</p>
<p>application of the LP-DED process, reducing uncertainty in designing and operating 
</p>
<p>heat exchangers for extreme environments. These innovations include:  
 
</p>
<p>1. Determination of the build factors and relationships associated with the 
</p>
<p>surface texture of thin-walls (&lt;1.5 mm) manufactured with LP-DED using 
</p>
<p>a Fe-Ni Superalloy.  
</p>
<p>2. Characterization and identification of geometric constraints for repeatable 
</p>
<p>LP-DED fabrication of heat exchangers. This information is essential for 
</p>
<p>designers wanting to use this technology. 
</p>
<p>3. Evaluation and characterization of surface enhancement techniques for 
</p>
<p>internal microchannel features (&lt;3 mm) manufactured with the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process to tailor the flow characteristics. 
</p>
<p>4. Development of an analytical model and correlation to estimate the pressure 
</p>
<p>drop of high-roughness channels fabricated with LP-DED. The correlation 
</p>
<p>is validated with experimental data.  
</p>
<p>To address how the surface texture is affected by the LP-DED fabrication 
</p>
<p>process, a series of thin-wall samples (1 mm) were manufactured using the NASA 
</p>
<p>HR-1 alloy, with variations in deposition parameters and powder size. A Design of 
</p>
<p>Experiments (DOE) was set up to elucidate the relationships between input process 
</p>
<p>parameters, powder feedstock with two different Particle Size Distribution (PSD), 
</p>
<p>and the interactions between the parameters affecting the resulting surface texture. 
</p>
<p>The geometry of the samples for this evaluation were enclosed vertical build boxes. 
</p>
<p>The experiments provided insights into the process inputs and parameters and 
</p>
<p>resulting texture for both internal and external surfaces. These samples were 
</p>
<p>destructively sectioned and meticulously evaluated using optical microscopy, 
</p>
<p>scanning electron microscopy, and optical metrology methods to ascertain wall 
</p>
<p>thickness, porosity, and surface texture. The details of this research are discussed in 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3. 
</p>
<p>While the enclosed vertical build box samples addressed the process mechanisms 
</p>
<p>affecting the surface texture, they were limited to a vertical wall. For parts fabricated 
</p>
<p>requiring more complex geometry thus with overhang build angles, a series of angled 
</p>
<p>samples were built. This set of samples provided insights into geometric relationships 
</p>
<p>and a full characterization and evaluation of process mechanisms concerning thin-
</p>
<p>wall samples with overhang angles. This research is described and discussed in 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4. Finally, a set of microchannels with varying sizes were fabricated and 
</p>
<p>characterized through destructive evaluation in Chapter 5. All samples were 
</p>
<p>sectioned and evaluated using optical microscopy, scanning electron microscopy, and 
</p>
<p>optical metrology methods to determine melt pool structure, powder particle 
</p>
<p>adherence, wall thickness, channel area, and surface texture. The results from 
</p>
<p>Chapters 3 and 4 collectively address how the surface texture is affected during the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED fabrication process (RQ1), providing a baseline characterization for the 
</p>
<p>geometric relationships affecting fluid flow performance (RQ2). </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 2 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 2—34 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The improvements and control of the surface texture is addressed through a series 
</p>
<p>of fabrication trials, experimental testing, and model correlations. Microchannel 
</p>
<p>samples were manufactured (based on the results from chapter 3, 4, 5) and post-build 
</p>
<p>surface enhancements were developed to modify the internal surface of the channels. 
</p>
<p>Selected surface modification processes included abrasive flow machining (AFM), 
</p>
<p>chemical milling (CM), chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), pulsed 
</p>
<p>electrochemical machining (PECM), and thermal energy method (TEM), see Table 
</p>
<p>1. Initial water flow testing of these samples whose inner surfaces were improved 
</p>
<p>with the mentioned techniques was completed, as well as tests of as-produced (i.e., 
</p>
<p>as-built) channels, to determine the flow resistance and the decrease of pressure drop 
</p>
<p>due to surface treatment. The channels were then destructively sectioned to determine 
</p>
<p>actual flow area, perimeter, particle adherence, and detailed mechanisms causing the 
</p>
<p>surface texture. This data anchored an analysis to determine the surface texture 
</p>
<p>characteristics affecting flow resistance. 
</p>
<p>To further explore RQ3, a second experiment was conducted using ~600 mm 
</p>
<p>length microchannel samples. Internal surfaces were modified using the AFM, CM, 
</p>
<p>and CMP processes, while additional as-built surface square and round samples were 
</p>
<p>fabricated to use them as baselines for comparison. Each sample underwent water 
</p>
<p>flow testing at various flow rates, with pressure measurements collected to determine 
</p>
<p>the differential pressure drop and resulting friction factors. The samples were 
</p>
<p>destructively sectioned to determine actual flow area, perimeter, particle adherence, 
</p>
<p>and surface texture. These data facilitated the validation of a model to determine the 
</p>
<p>friction factors resulting from various surfaces.  
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—35 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>3 Process Overview and In-process 
</p>
<p>Surface Evaluation 
 
</p>
<p>Major portions of this chapter were published in: 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Gradl, P.R., Cervone, A., Gill, E., 2022. Surface texture characterization for thin-
</p>
<p>wall NASA HR-1 Fe – Ni – Cr alloy using laser powder directed energy deposition 
</p>
<p>(LP-DED). Advances in Industrial and Manufacturing Engineering 4, 100084. 
</p>
<p>https://doi.org/10.1016/j.aime.2022.100084 
</p>
<p>  
</p>
<p>3.1 ABSTRACT 
</p>
<p>This experimental study completed a design of experiments (DOE) to determine 
</p>
<p>the critical build parameters that influence surface texture for enclosed thin-wall 
</p>
<p>samples. This study summarizes the characterization work of the LP-DED process 
</p>
<p>for 1 mm enclosed walls with an Fe-Ni-Cr (NASA HR-1) alloy. The LP-DED 
</p>
<p>parameters including laser power, powder feedrate, travel speed, layer height, and 
</p>
<p>rotary atomized powder feedstock were modified in the experiment. An evaluation of 
</p>
<p>the DOE samples and resulting surface texture is provided along with conclusions 
</p>
<p>from these experiments. Results indicate that 3D areal and 2D profile (directional) 
</p>
<p>surface texture is estimated by 2x the powder diameter that becomes captured or 
</p>
<p>partially melted on the trailing edge of the melt pool. The fine powder showed a 
</p>
<p>higher sensitivity to parameter changes but resulted in a higher density material and 
</p>
<p>23% reduction in roughness. Surface texture was also shown to vary between closed 
</p>
<p>channel shapes (internal) due to ricochets, recirculation, and higher volume of 
</p>
<p>powder available to bond compared to external (outer) surfaces. The understanding 
</p>
<p>of the LP-DED process as-built surface texture is critical to fluid flow applications 
</p>
<p>such as heat exchanges and can modify performance for enhanced heat transfer or 
</p>
<p>can be a detriment to pressure drop.  
</p>
<p>3.2 BACKGROUND 
</p>
<p>Several studies evaluated surface texture using the LP-DED process but were 
</p>
<p>limited in the application to thin-walls (&lt; 3 mm). The LP-DED system configuration, 
</p>
<p>spot size, and deposition parameters can vary significantly based on the part geometry 
</p>
<p>and associated wall thickness. Riede et al. completed a study of 316L using a coaxial 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—36 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>deposition head and powder sized from 44-106 µm and a layer thickness of 0.91 mm 
</p>
<p>and reported an average areal surface roughness, Sa, of 44 µm [189]. Zhang et al. 
</p>
<p>developed a unique technique for thin-wall LP-DED using a central powder 
</p>
<p>deposition head with surrounding lasers and achieved a surface roughness of 3.2 µm 
</p>
<p>with 75-106 µm Fe313 powder and 2.7 mm wall thickness [118]. Lu et al. completed 
</p>
<p>a similar study using Fe313 alloy with a central laser beam and 75-106 µm powder 
</p>
<p>resulting in 3 µm roughness and a wall thickness of 2.7 mm [190]. 
</p>
<p>A study by Mahamood and Akinlabi on LP-DED Ti-6Al-4V suggested 
</p>
<p>increasing laser power using a 2 mm spot size reduced the surface roughness, but was 
</p>
<p>based on a horizontal cladding deposition strategy [191]. This increase in power 
</p>
<p>allows for more of the powder to be melted and less residual powder available to 
</p>
<p>adhere to the adjacent surfaces, but the increase in power will impact wall geometry. 
</p>
<p>Kim et al. discuss that an increase in laser power and powder mass flow rate will 
</p>
<p>result in increased wall thickness in addition to providing a consistent bead with less 
</p>
<p>porosity [102]. A significant increase of laser power solely for thin-wall structures is 
</p>
<p>not a feasible build strategy since this results in uncontrolled geometry. A study by 
</p>
<p>Mazzarisi et al. also confirmed this through deposition of a 1.5 mm wall using Inconel 
</p>
<p>718 and concluded that 150% increase in laser power caused the melt pool to enlarge 
</p>
<p>and resulted in a 44% increase in wall thickness [192]. Jinoop et al. completed an 
</p>
<p>experimental evaluation and concluded that power was the primary contributor to the 
</p>
<p>deposition geometry (width, height, and deposition rate) and powder feed rate and 
</p>
<p>travel speed contributed to a lesser degree [95]. The combination of these parameters 
</p>
<p>is also important since independent adjustments may not always achieve the desired 
</p>
<p>results. 
</p>
<p>Another study by Mahamood and Akinlabi demonstrated that decrease in the 
</p>
<p>travel speed or increase in shielding gas flow rate could reduce surface roughness, 
</p>
<p>but also focused on a horizontal cladding surface [193]. In the thin-wall study by 
</p>
<p>Zhang et al., it was suggested that increasing the travel speed (scan speed) provides 
</p>
<p>a smoother surface due to less dwell time for the excess powder to agglomerate on 
</p>
<p>the surface [118]. Alimardani et al. also provided this conclusion to reduce the travel 
</p>
<p>speed to improve surface roughness, but did not provide any quantification of 
</p>
<p>roughness and only visual [194]. Gharbi et al. completed a more detailed study of 
</p>
<p>surface roughness for the LP-DED process on Ti-6Al-4V with wall thicknesses 
</p>
<p>ranging from 1.7 to 3.5 mm [120]. It was observed that the increased travel speed 
</p>
<p>could reduce the waviness in the form of the periodic menisci by a factor of 5, but 
</p>
<p>only had a limited effect on the roughness (26% reduction) with a short interaction 
</p>
<p>distance. Gharbi et al. concluded that the best surface roughness was obtained using 
</p>
<p>higher travel speeds and higher laser power which results in spreading the melt pool.  
</p>
<p>Motion control parameters such as layer height, injection nozzle stand-off 
</p>
<p>distance, and laser focal plane are referenced in literature with regards to surface 
</p>
<p>roughness. Several articles suggest that reduction in vertical layer height (Z-
</p>
<p>direction), which can be partially accomplished by the increased travel speed, can 
</p>
<p>improve surface roughness but may not be as efficient as other parameter adjustments 
</p>
<p>[89,119,120]. The high degree of layering from height changes, resulting in waviness, 
</p>
<p>is commonly observed in literature [90,92,119]. Zhu et al. evaluated an approach to 
</p>
<p>change the injection plane to modify the surface roughness, but was focused on the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—37 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>undulations of the top surface and no quantification was provided for the side surface 
</p>
<p>roughness [195]. Careri et al. discuss that the stand-off and laser focus distance in 
</p>
<p>LP-DED must be critically controlled to avoid high roughness of the side surfaces 
</p>
<p>and uniformity of the top surface as it relates to post-process machining, but do not 
</p>
<p>quantify this either [196]. 
</p>
<p>Powder feedstock is a critical input to the LP-DED process. There are several 
</p>
<p>studies that reference thin-wall deposition using a particle size distribution (PSD) 
</p>
<p>from 45 to 150 µm and a few studies using 15 to 45 µm. Jardon et al. demonstrated 
</p>
<p>1 mm wall thicknesses using stainless steel 316 L with various PSD, but limited the 
</p>
<p>research to single pass beads on plate and no measurements of surface roughness 
</p>
<p>were provided [84]. Based on visual observations, it was suggested that a lower 
</p>
<p>roughness could be achieved using a finer PSD ranging from 15 to 45 µm. A study 
</p>
<p>by Carroll et al. noted that with continued recycling of gas atomized from 50 to 125 
</p>
<p>µm Waspaloy powder, the surface roughness increased on the sidewalls of 0.6 mm 
</p>
<p>thick samples [197]. The article observed that the mean particle size was similar 
</p>
<p>through continued recycling, while flowability increased (reduced Hall Flow values). 
</p>
<p>Ahsan et al. studied the LP-DED process using Ti-6Al-4V gas atomized and the 
</p>
<p>plasma rotating electrode process (PREP) with 45 to 105 µm powders [198]. The 
</p>
<p>mean particle diameter was found to be 94 µm for gas atomized and 72 µm for PREP. 
</p>
<p>The resulting surface roughness was shown to be lower for the PREP powder 
</p>
<p>compared to gas atomized as well as reduced porosity for 3 mm thick samples, 
</p>
<p>although not conclusive if specific to powder size or other contributors. Based on this 
</p>
<p>literature comparing various PSD ranges for LP-DED, further research is merited 
</p>
<p>since the impact to surface texture and roughness was not fully quantified. 
</p>
<p>Prior literature often suggests that LP-DED requires final machining to achieve 
</p>
<p>desired surface roughness for use in final applications [120,199]. Traditional 
</p>
<p>machining is, however, not feasible for many AM parts, including LP-DED, as 
</p>
<p>complexity increases with internal features such as those for heat exchangers. One of 
</p>
<p>the limitations of the available research is the limited quantification of surface texture 
</p>
<p>relative to as-built thin-walls and enclosed features fabricated using LP-DED. Several 
</p>
<p>publications reference an early study by Li and Ma that evaluated horizontal surface 
</p>
<p>roughness for LP-DED and does not allow roughness to be inferred for vertical 
</p>
<p>surfaces [200], which considers cladding and not vertical thin walls deposited in a 
</p>
<p>single bead. For proper application of surface texture for an industrial application, it 
</p>
<p>must be characterized in the alloy of interest with identical geometry and parameters 
</p>
<p>that are being used for the end part. The surface must be understood beyond an 
</p>
<p>average roughness value (ie. Ra, Sa) since the complexity of the surface can impact 
</p>
<p>the fluid pressure drop, heat transfer, and mechanical fatigue properties. Should post-
</p>
<p>processing surface enhancements be required to help tune the roughness, the value 
</p>
<p>and dominance of peaks and valleys should be characterized in order to determine 
</p>
<p>the proper amount of material to be removed. Considering observations from the 
</p>
<p>literature, the process parameters (e.g., power, travel speed, layer height, stand-off 
</p>
<p>distance, powder feedrate, shielding gas flowrate), nozzle configuration, and both the 
</p>
<p>powder type and size can be modified to change surface roughness but all potentially 
</p>
<p>at a detriment to geometry. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—38 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The results of this research provide an in-depth understanding of surface texture 
</p>
<p>(form, waviness, and roughness) as it relates to 1 mm thick walls using LP-DED 
</p>
<p>processed NASA HR-1 superalloy for heat exchanger applications. The powder 
</p>
<p>feedstock, AM process and associated parameters, geometry, and alloy composition 
</p>
<p>can impact the as-built surface texture and the fluid flow, and the heat transfer 
</p>
<p>performance can vary depending on the surface produced. It is necessary to 
</p>
<p>characterize and understand the sensitivity of the process inputs for each alloy and 
</p>
<p>feedstock of interest so that a designer can properly apply this knowledge for the 
</p>
<p>desired end-use application. NASA HR-1 (Section 3.3.2) is a Fe-Ni-Cr superalloy 
</p>
<p>used for high pressure hydrogen applications, such as heat exchangers and rocket 
</p>
<p>engine nozzles. The advantage of using NASA HR-1 for these applications is its high 
</p>
<p>conductivity, high fatigue strength, increased yield strength and ductility over other 
</p>
<p>hydrogen-resistant alloys, and excellent hydrogen environment embrittlement (HEE) 
</p>
<p>resistance. Prior literature has shown the advancements made with the NASA HR-1 
</p>
<p>alloy using LP-DED, but limited to the metallurgical aspects of the material 
</p>
<p>[201,202]. 
</p>
<p>Other critical aspects of this research include the detailed characterization of the 
</p>
<p>as-built complex LP-DED surface texture of multi-layer single-bead deposition 1 mm 
</p>
<p>walls using different sizes of rotary atomized NASA HR-1 powder. Prior studies 
</p>
<p>focused on gas atomized powder. This study instead considers as-built inner 
</p>
<p>(enclosed) surfaces and outer (external) surfaces with varying parameters and powder 
</p>
<p>sizes, which were not discussed in prior literature for thin-wall LP-DED. A design of 
</p>
<p>experiments (DOE) was conducted using the LP-DED process parameters and non-
</p>
<p>contact measurements to characterize the complex as-built surface texture. The as-
</p>
<p>built surface assumes no post-processing. While the surface texture is critical for 
</p>
<p>design and end-use, the resulting geometry from the build must be balanced with 
</p>
<p>consistency and repeatability of the wall thickness and material density. These were 
</p>
<p>both studied using optical microscopy image analysis. This study provides a 
</p>
<p>quantification for the surface conditions of the LP-DED NASA HR-1 alloy 
</p>
<p>permitting designers to properly account for it and control it for thin-wall 
</p>
<p>applications. This research is relevant for large scale applications (&gt;1 m diameter) 
</p>
<p>where the LP-DED process has advantages for freeform fabrication while still 
</p>
<p>maintaining thin-walls (1–1.2 mm thick). The results presented are relevant to future 
</p>
<p>LP-DED thin-wall components to be used on their as-built surface texture of the walls 
</p>
<p>to reduce post-processing due to costs or the inability to post-process due to 
</p>
<p>complexity. 
</p>
<p>3.3 METHODOLOGY 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The LP-DED process uses a laser beam to create a melt pool and metal powder 
</p>
<p>is injected into this pool using an inert carrier gas through a deposition head. As the 
</p>
<p>deposition head traverses an area that was deposited the material cools and then 
</p>
<p>solidifies creating the bead. The deposition head is attached to a motion control 
</p>
<p>system, such as a multi-axis robotic arm or Cartesian coordinate gantry system. The 
</p>
<p>Cartesian coordinate gantry system is preferred and was used in this study allowing </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—39 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>for better accuracy and repeatability, which is critical for single-width bead wall 
</p>
<p>thickness. After a single layer is built for the samples or part, additional layers are 
</p>
<p>deposited until the final part is created. LP-DED also has the advantage of using 
</p>
<p>multiple axis for complex geometries and is not limited to only 3-axes.  
</p>
<p>The samples created for this study were single-width bead racetracks with 75 
</p>
<p>mm length, 25 mm in height, and 1 mm wall thickness (adjacent walls spaced 25 mm 
</p>
<p>apart). These were deposited as a racetrack as opposed to a freestanding wall to 
</p>
<p>provide geometric stability and eliminate any distortion. Each sample was deposited 
</p>
<p>according to the parameters established for the DOE and then measured using non-
</p>
<p>contact microscopic imaging to evaluate roughness and texture. Further 
</p>
<p>characterization of the wall thickness and porosity was evaluated using microscopic 
</p>
<p>and scanning electron microscope (SEM) imaging. 
</p>
<p>The LP-DED system has several subsystems that include the powder injection 
</p>
<p>and purge gas flow system, the laser power supply and optics, and the motion control. 
</p>
<p>Each of these subsystems must work congruently to provide the desired bead, 
</p>
<p>resulting geometry, and material characteristics. These subsystems use support 
</p>
<p>hardware and input parameters that can be adjusted to affect the material 
</p>
<p>characteristics, including surface roughness. The laser and optical system include 
</p>
<p>many of the laser parameters and optical hardware that derive the beam delivery 
</p>
<p>including the power, spot size, focus position, profile of beam, type of laser, and laser 
</p>
<p>operational mode (such as continuous or pulsed). The powder injection system 
</p>
<p>includes systems and inputs such as the powder feedrate, type of carrier gas, core 
</p>
<p>shielding gas and/or secondary gas flow (mass flow rate and gas type), and the 
</p>
<p>powder nozzle design (annular or multi-nozzle). The motion control system 
</p>
<p>manipulates the movement parameter for the deposition head that includes the travel 
</p>
<p>speed, sets layer height, standoff distance, angle of the head, and hatching (or step-
</p>
<p>over). While these parameters are fundamental to the basic build geometry, other 
</p>
<p>factors may be more specific to component build geometry, such as spacing between 
</p>
<p>features, build plate configuration, and toolpath build strategy. The major subsystems 
</p>
<p>and parameters for LP-DED are shown in Figure 3.1. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—40 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Figure 3.1. Overview of LP-DED Process Parameters. 
</p>
<p>The primary parameters that are critical to the LP-DED process include laser 
</p>
<p>power, travel speed (also called feedrate or scan speed), powder feedrate, laser spot 
</p>
<p>size, hatching (also called stepover spacing or track overlap), and layer height (or 
</p>
<p>layer thickness) [203]. These parameters and some of the other process inputs shown 
</p>
<p>in Figure 3.2 determine the melt pool and resulting geometry. The melt pool and 
</p>
<p>powder carrier gas derive the general surface roughness [204]. An unstable melt pool 
</p>
<p>can cause spatter, porosity, or irregular beads and increase occurrences of unmelted 
</p>
<p>or partially-melted powder adhering to the surface.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>3.3.1 LP-DED Experiment Parameters 
</p>
<p>This study specifically focused on five key input parameters and impacts to the 
</p>
<p>resultant surface texture. The down selected parameters include the laser power, 
</p>
<p>travel speed, layer height, flow rate of powder, and powder size and are highlighted 
</p>
<p>in Figure 3.2. Several parameters were fixed based on the geometry desired and also 
</p>
<p>system configuration. The spot size selected was determined by the desired wall 
</p>
<p>thickness, in addition to the type, mode and subsequent profile of the laser were all </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—41 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>fixed. The standoff was also considered but is accurately controlled in the motion 
</p>
<p>system with monitoring. The angle of the head is fixed normal to the surface. Other 
</p>
<p>parameters such as angle of the head, hatching, and build strategy were not applicable 
</p>
<p>since the experiment involved single bead width thin-wall deposition.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.2. LP-DED parameter inputs that can impact surface texture and the 
</p>
<p>deposited bead geometry. The highlighted parameters were varied in this 
</p>
<p>study. 
</p>
<p>A total of 35 samples were fabricated on an RPM Innovations 557 machine with 
</p>
<p>a 3-nozzle Argon-carrier gas powder injection and Argon central purge deposition 
</p>
<p>head. This was a gantry-type system, and the build box was fully inert with Argon to 
</p>
<p>reduce oxidation. The laser used was an infrared (IR) 1064 nm continuous wave with 
</p>
<p>Gaussian profile. All samples were single bead width deposition tracks in the Z-
</p>
<p>direction. The sample tracks were fabricated on a 13 mm thick mild steel build plate.  
</p>
<p>The baseline parameters were established through initial experimentation to 
</p>
<p>demonstrate a low porosity NASA HR-1 build that would meet geometric 
</p>
<p>requirements as well as optimal deposition time. The variation in parameters for this 
</p>
<p>experiment were selected to be 5% from nominal for power, travel speed, powder 
</p>
<p>feedrate, and 10% for layer height. These were selected to ensure that all build 
</p>
<p>samples would be successful to full height for data analysis in addition to meeting 
</p>
<p>wall thickness range of 1 to 1.2 mm. While parameters could be varied further, this 
</p>
<p>would result in incomplete or failed samples and data could not be collected. Other 
</p>
<p>studies suggest that a higher increase in laser power of 25% or more could improve 
</p>
<p>surface roughness [120]. However, core material microstructure and desired 
</p>
<p>geometry must also be considered.  With increased power for thin-wall LP-DED, the 
</p>
<p>melt pool increases resulting in thicker walls, increased size and quantity of the 
</p>
<p>columnar grains, and results in a more porous structure [102]. Additionally, the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—42 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 could be adversely impacted with increased laser power through 
</p>
<p>vaporizing of strengthening elements or segregation of these elements, both resulting 
</p>
<p>in a poor material microstructure and properties [202].   
</p>
<p> Each sample was deposited using racetrack configuration (flat walls and 180° 
</p>
<p>radii) to provide dimensional stability and allow for surface texture (waviness and 
</p>
<p>roughness) to be measured on both the inner (internal to racetrack) and outer 
</p>
<p>(external) surfaces (Figure 3.3). The samples were 76 mm in length (Y-direction), 25 
</p>
<p>mm in height (Z-direction) and opposing walls spaced 25 mm apart (X-direction). 
</p>
<p>The area selected for surface measurements was 40 mm in width and 13 mm in height. 
</p>
<p>The samples were sectioned from the build plate via bandsaw and were cleaned in 
</p>
<p>Isopropyl alcohol to remove any excess machine oil. No mechanical cleaning was 
</p>
<p>used to preserve the surface. The samples are referenced by a unique indicator (1-35), 
</p>
<p>however the last four samples in the series (32-35) were labeled as A-D to indicate 
</p>
<p>nominal parameters. A built plate with samples (1 through 6, 32, 33) is shown in 
</p>
<p>Figure 3.3A. The typical surface from a coarse powder sample (45-105 µm) is shown 
</p>
<p>in Figure 3.3B and fine powder (10-45 µm) in Figure 3.3C. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>

</p>
<p>build plate, B) Sample A (Run 32) with nominal parameters using 45-105 µm 
</p>
<p>powder, C) Sample C (Run 34) with nominal parameters using 10-45 µm 
</p>
<p>powder. </p>

<p> 
</p>
<p>3.3.2 NASA HR-1 Material 
</p>
<p>The NASA HR-1 alloy was selected for this study due to the importance of this 
</p>
<p>alloy for use in hydrogen based heat exchanger applications [201]. The NASA HR-1 
</p>
<p>alloy was developed at NASA Marshall Space Flight Center in the late 1990’s as a 
</p>
<p>hydrogen environment resistant (ie. “HR”) superalloy for use in high temperature and 
</p>
<p>high pressure hydrogen applications [205]. NASA HR-1 is a Fe-Ni-Cr Superalloy 
</p>
<p>that provides high strength and high ductility in these harsh environments and 
</p>
<p>originally derived from JBK-75. The alloy has been demonstrated in several 
</p>
<p>applications focused on liquid rocket nozzles and high temperature hydrogen 
</p>
<p>environment heat exchangers [77]. While other alloys exist that could meet these 
</p>
<p>general requirements, such as A-286, JBK-75, and the 300 series stainless steel (304, 
</p>
<p>310, 316), they can be limited in strength for such heat exchanger applications [13].  
</p>
<p>Two powder lots (HRA9 and HRA4) of rotary atomized NASA HR-1 from 
</p>
<p>Homogenized Metals Inc. (HMI) were used for the sample deposition in a fine cut 
</p>
<p>(10-45 µm) and coarse cut (45-105 µm). The powder chemistry was within nominal 
</p>
<p>ranges including any trace elements that were measured (C, Mn, Si, P, S, B) as seen 
</p>
<p>in Table 1. Chemistry was measured using Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP) at HMI. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 3.1. Chemistry for NASA HR-1 powder used in this study (Wt. %). 
</p>
<p>Element Powder Lot 
Nominal 
</p>
<p>Chemistry  
</p>
<p>  HRA9 HRA4 Ref [201] 
</p>
<p>PSD 10-45 µm 45-105 µm  
Fe 41.24 41.78 Bal 
</p>
<p>Ni 33.91 33.71 33.7 – 34.3 
</p>
<p>Cr 14.66 14.49 14.3 – 14.9 
</p>
<p>Co 3.79 3.75 3.6 – 4.0 
</p>
<p>Mo 1.83 1.82 1.6 – 2.0 
</p>
<p>Ti 2.41 2.31 2.2 – 2.6 
</p>
<p>Al 0.243 0.24 0.23 – 0.27 
</p>
<p>V 0.302 0.30 0.28 – 0.32 
</p>
<p>W 1.60 1.59 1.4 – 1.8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The rotary atomized powder was characterized using a Microtrac (Version 
</p>
<p>10.1.0.6) and imaged using a Hitachi S3000H Scanning Electron Microscope (SEM) 
</p>
<p>to evaluate the general morphology. The fine and coarse cut powder SEM images can 
</p>
<p>be seen in Figure 3.4.  The Microtrac calculated the peak distribution of the powder 
</p>
<p>at 70 µm for the coarse powder (45-105 µm). The SEM showed mostly spherical 
</p>
<p>shape with a few oblong ellipsoidal particles and some traces of satellites for the 
</p>
<p>coarse powder. Oblong particles can cause issues with flowability or clog the nozzle 
</p>
<p>during deposition. The fine powder (10-45 µm) showed a peak at 34 µm from the 
</p>
<p>Microtrac characterization. While a majority of the finer powder is spherical observed 
</p>
<p>in the SEM, the oblong shaped morphology is also observed along with several sphere 
</p>
<p>and satellites and granular particles.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—44 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.4. SEM Images of the NASA HR-1 Powder. A) Coarse cut 45-105 µm 
</p>
<p>powder and B) Fine cut 10-45 µm powder. Note: both images are at the same 
</p>
<p>magnification (100X) 
</p>
<p>3.3.3 Design of Experiment (DOE) Samples 
</p>
<p>A DOE was developed to understand the relationships between the input process 
</p>
<p>parameters, powder feedstock in two different PSDs, as well as significant 
</p>
<p>interactions of the parameters on the resulting surface texture and roughness. A split 
</p>
<p>design grouped by powder size was selected for the DOE because of the difficulty of 
</p>
<p>powder changes. This avoided changing powder every run, which was not feasible 
</p>
<p>for the experiment. A total of 35 runs (Runs 1 – 35) were completed at varying 
</p>
<p>parameters including four distinct runs (two with each powder size) at the 
</p>
<p>predetermined optimal set of parameters from prior development work on this alloy. 
</p>
<p>The predetermined optimal set of parameters was laser power = 350 W, powder 
</p>
<p>feedrate = 23 g/s, travel speed = 762 mm/s, and layer height = 0.254 mm. The 
</p>
<p>summary of parameters used for this study is shown in Table 3.2.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—45 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The DOE was analyzed using analysis of variance (ANOVA) to determine the 
</p>
<p>significance and extent of influence for the LP-DED process parameters that impact 
</p>
<p>surface texture [206]. The overall model and influence of the individual factors and 
</p>
<p>interactions were considered significant if the probability value (p-value) was less 
</p>
<p>than the set significance level (α = 0.05). This analysis was conducted using 
</p>
<p>DesignExpert software. The final model that best fit the data was a two-factor 
</p>
<p>interaction (2FI), with several terms eliminated that were not significant. Other 
</p>
<p>models were evaluated including quadratic but did not provide any significance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 3.2. Selected Parameters and Powder for DOE runs.

<p>Chapter 3—46 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>3.3.4 Roughness Measurements and Microscopy 
</p>
<p> Each sample was then measured using a non-contact Keyence VR-5200 
</p>
<p>patterned light projection profilometer with three telecentric lenses at 80x 
</p>
<p>magnification and image stitching with an overlap of 20%. The samples were first 
</p>
<p>measured on the outer surface, then sectioned inside the radii and measurements were 
</p>
<p>completed on the inner surface. A surface form correction was used on the entire area 
</p>
<p>to remove the tilt and any curvature, and a reference plane was then established. No 
</p>
<p>filtering was used in the analysis of the surface topography data since the entire 
</p>
<p>surface is of interest for potential thin-wall applications. All measurements were 
</p>
<p>completed and reported according to ASME B46.1 [207]. The surface scanning 
</p>
<p>covered an area of 43 mm by 16 mm to remove any edge effects, and areal 
</p>
<p>measurements for average roughness (Sa), max height of surface (Sz), max peak 
</p>
<p>height (Sp), max valley depth (Sv), skewness (Ssk), kurtosis (Sku) were determined 
</p>
<p>from this region. Waviness was obtained in the horizontal and vertical directions with 
</p>
<p>a high-pass filter (λc) of 0.25 mm and low-pass (λs) of 0. The focus of this study was 
</p>
<p>on the sidewall vertical surface roughness, which is in the direction of flow for heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger applications. The measurement uncertainty was obtained by repeating 
</p>
<p>measurements on a single sample for each fine and coarse powder PSD samples. A 
</p>
<p>99% confidence interval for measurement uncertainty is calculated and presented in 
</p>
<p>the data plots. 
</p>
<p>In addition to areal surface analysis, five vertical lines and three horizontal line 
</p>
<p>profiles were obtained from the surface areal profile. The vertical direction 
</p>
<p>corresponds to the build direction and also flow direction in heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>fabricated with LP-DED. The 2D profile directional line texture parameters analyzed 
</p>
<p>were average profile height (Pa), average maximum profile height (Pz), reduced peak 
</p>
<p>height (Pp), reduced valley depth (Pv), skewness (Psk), and kurtosis (Pku). The 
</p>
<p>profile texture measurements (Pa, Pz, Pp, Pv, Psk, Pku) were used for this experiment 
</p>
<p>which is inclusive of waviness and roughness that could impact the application for 
</p>
<p>flow in heat exchangers. This is synonymous with the traditional Ra (average 
</p>
<p>roughness) but are not filtered for waviness. The line profile data is important to the 
</p>
<p>end application, and directionality should be considered for specific applications 
</p>
<p>[208], such as fluid flow, and included as an outcome of this study. The 2D directional 
</p>
<p>profile measurements were equally spaced across the region of interest in the vertical 
</p>
<p>and horizontal directions. The regions of interest for the areal surface roughness and 
</p>
<p>directional texture measurements are shown in Figure 3.5. For data reporting, the five 
</p>
<p>vertical texture measurements were averaged, as well as the three horizontal 
</p>
<p>measurements averaged, to provide a single value. All measurements were repeated 
</p>
<p>for the inner and outer surfaces for each sample. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—47 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.5. Nomenclature and measurement locations for areal and directional 
</p>
<p>profile line texture measurements. The build direction also corresponds to the 
</p>
<p>flow direction for heat exchanger applications.  
</p>
<p>Following the texture measurements, SEM imaging was completed on a Hitachi 
</p>
<p>S3000H for select samples on the inner and outer surfaces within the central region 
</p>
<p>of the sample. The samples were then sectioned and mounted to evaluate porosity 
</p>
<p>and microstructure. The micrographs were imaged using a Keyence VHX digital 
</p>
<p>microscope. The samples were prepared per ASTM E3 using a Presi automatic 
</p>
<p>polisher. The samples were prepared to evaluate porosity were polished using 0.05-
</p>
<p>micron colloidal silica, but not etched. This allowed for image characterization of the 
</p>
<p>potential defects independent of the visible grain structure. Select samples were 
</p>
<p>etched using etchant #13 (10% Oxalic Acid, Electrolytic) to determine the deposition 
</p>
<p>melt patterns and general grain structure. 
</p>
<p>The porosity was measured on the polished samples using ImageJ software 
</p>
<p>(Version 1.53e). The image was adjusted to 8-bit to allow for differentiation between 
</p>
<p>pores and other stains or burns that can occur from polishing. The entire area of the 
</p>
<p>sample was then selected, and the areas of porosity quantified; any edge effects were 
</p>
<p>eliminated to minimize error. The samples thickness was measured using ImageJ at 
</p>
<p>five transverse locations and then averaged to provide a single value. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>3.4 EXPERIMENTAL RESULTS 
</p>
<p>3.4.1 Visual and SEM 
</p>
<p>All samples from the LP-DED process were built successfully (no failures, no 
</p>
<p>process stops, no issues noted) to the nominal dimensions. The samples were 
</p>
<p>removed from the build plates and evaluated in the as-built condition (i.e., no stress 
</p>
<p>relief or subsequent heat treatments; no surface modifications). The coarse powder 
</p>
<p>samples appeared to have a higher degree of texturing across the surface, and the fine </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—48 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>powder samples emerged smoother from visual inspections. The SEM images show 
</p>
<p>the surface roughness being impacted by the powder size, as seen in Figure 3.6. The 
</p>
<p>coarse powder samples have a random powder scattering across the surface (Figure 
</p>
<p>3.6A) of partially melted and loosely adhered powder (Figure 3.6B). Several powder 
</p>
<p>particles can be seen barely protruding from the surface encapsulated by the solidified 
</p>
<p>surrounding material, while the partially melted and solidified material forming a 
</p>
<p>radius with the underside of the particles. The fine powder samples (Figure 3.6C, 
</p>
<p>Figure 3.6D) show a higher number of particles adhered, covering adhered covering 
</p>
<p>almost the entire surface. There is minor visual evidence of the macro waviness 
</p>
<p>observed in Figure 3.6C, potentially from layer height. There are many instances of 
</p>
<p>fully melted and partially melted fine particles similar to the coarse powder. The 
</p>
<p>diameter of the particles measured normal to the surface on the SEM images match 
</p>
<p>the PSD of the powder sizes used for respective samples.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.6. SEM images of select samples. A) Sample 32 inner surface with 
</p>
<p>coarse powder at 50x, B) Sample 32 inner surface with coarse powder at 250x, 
</p>
<p>C) Sample 34 inner surface with fine powder at 50x, D) Sample 34 inner 
</p>
<p>surface with fine powder at 250x. 
</p>
<p>3.4.2 Microstructure 
</p>
<p>Representative sectioned and etched samples for coarse and fine powder are 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 3.7. The sample with coarse powder (Fig. 7A, B, C) displays the 
</p>
<p>particles adhered to the surface with solidified material forming a tangential radius </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—49 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>between. In most instances, the melted and solidified material extends about 50% of 
</p>
<p>the width of the particle, forming the roughness on the surface assuming powder 
</p>
<p>particles protrusions are defined as roughness and the layering is defined as waviness, 
</p>
<p>as suggested by Diaz [127]. There are a few instances observed with subsurface 
</p>
<p>particles that are not fully melted into the surrounding material and shown to be a 
</p>
<p>maximum of 2x the diameter of the powder PSD. The unmelted coarse particles 
</p>
<p>extend about 150 µm subsurface measured normal from the surface. The fine powder 
</p>
<p>sample (Fig. 7D, E, F) shows a higher number of instances and higher density of 
</p>
<p>unmelted powder across the surface and subsurface. There are multiple layers of 
</p>
<p>unmelted or partially melted fine powder observed subsurface with solidified material 
</p>
<p>between the particles. The unmelted particles extend about 100 µm into the 
</p>
<p>subsurface when measured normal from the external surface.  
</p>
<p>The melt pools are clearly visible in the etched samples. The microstructure is 
</p>
<p>characteristic of the LP-DED process with epitaxial dendrite grain growth that 
</p>
<p>traverse across melt pools [209–211]. The dendrite arm growth is random, but also 
</p>
<p>shows a tendency to solidify from the outer surface of the melt pool towards the 
</p>
<p>center. Small grains are observed on the external surface of the samples due to the 
</p>
<p>rapid cooling [212]. The rapid cooling of the exterior surfaces trap excess powder 
</p>
<p>with solid material, surrounding these loose particles.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—50 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.7. Sectioned and etched samples with inner and outer surfaces. A, B, 
</p>
<p>C) Sample 32 using coarse powder, D, E, F) Sample 34 using fine powder. 
</p>
<p>3.4.3 Areal Measurements 
</p>
<p> The areal roughness measurements (Sa, Sz, Sku, and Ssk) for all of the 
</p>
<p>specimens are summarized in Figure 3.8. Sa is the typical areal parameter used to 
</p>
<p>quantify roughness of surfaces but does not capture the complexity and discriminate 
</p>
<p>between peaks and valleys. The Sz, Sp, Sv, Sku, and Ssk parameters define the 
</p>
<p>magnitude of the peaks and values that can be used in fluid flow evaluations for heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers or material removal estimates for post-processing operations (e.g. 
</p>
<p>machining or polishing). The DOE samples were each built with a varying set of 
</p>
<p>parameters and identified by sample number on the X-axis of plots, which is 
</p>
<p>synonymous with the run number (Table 3.2). The average areal roughness 
</p>
<p>(arithmetic mean deviation), Sa, in Figure 3.8A clearly shows the fine powder is 
</p>
<p>lower compared to the coarse powder in almost all samples, which is consistent with 
</p>
<p>prior observations [84,213,214]. The dotted lines shown in Figure 3.8 pair the inner 
</p>
<p>and outer wall measurments for readability.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—51 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.8. Areal texture measurements for all samples. A) Average area 
</p>
<p>roughness [Sa], B) Maximum height of surface [Sz], C) Kurtosis [Sku], D) 
</p>
<p>Skewness [Ssk]. 
</p>
<p>Another trend observed is the difference between the inner and outer surface with 
</p>
<p>the inner surface showing higher roughness in almost all instances for both fine and 
</p>
<p>coarse powders. This is due to ricochets, recirculation, and higher volume of powder 
</p>
<p>available on the internal volume of the racetrack sample to bond to the trailing edge 
</p>
<p>of the melt pool during solidification. There is a high volume of powder available due 
</p>
<p>to the low efficiency (&lt;10%) with thin-wall depositions such as this. The Sa for the 
</p>
<p>coarse powder shows a tight grouping of data independent of the changes in 
</p>
<p>parameters, where the fine powder has more variation. The fine powder shows higher 
</p>
<p>sensitivity in changes to build parameters. The areal roughness, Sa, for the fine 
</p>
<p>powder showed a downward trend as a function of energy density. The coarse powder 
</p>
<p>was stable as a function of the energy density. From the dataset, sample 30 appears 
</p>
<p>to be a clear outlier, which is partially impacted from the waviness from the layering. 
</p>
<p>This was the only sample where all build parameters were set to the higher range </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—52 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>(power = 368 W; powder feedrate = 24.2 g/s; travel speed = 800 mm/s; layer height 
</p>
<p>= 0.279 mm).  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> The amplitude parameters for Kurtosis (Sku) and skewness (Ssk) were 
</p>
<p>evaluated and shown in Figure 3.8C and D. The high Sku (&gt; 3.0) indicates that all 
</p>
<p>samples have a high degree of peaks and valleys across the surface. The coarse and 
</p>
<p>fine powder samples show minor variation within the groups and indicates the 
</p>
<p>predominance of peaks across the surface (based on Ssk). Most of the coarse samples 
</p>
<p>are dominated by peaks. Ssk trends downward as energy density increases 
</p>
<p>independent of powder PSD, but only for the inner surface.  
</p>
<p>3.4.4 Directional Profile Measurements 
</p>
<p> The 2D directional profile texture results are similar to the areal parameters 
</p>
<p>and shown in Figure 3.9. These parameters were measured in the vertical direction at 
</p>
<p>a length of 16 mm. The trends are very similar with the coarse powder PSD showing 
</p>
<p>higher values than the fine powder PSD. The dotted lines in Figure 3.9A pairs the 
</p>
<p>samples together for readability. For a majority of samples the average profile height 
</p>
<p>of the inner surface (Pa) has a higher texture than the outer surface, although some 
</p>
<p>are reverse compared to Sa such as samples 1, 8, 25. Pa is a localized measurement 
</p>
<p>and any excess powder, particularly with the coarse PSD, in a localized area could 
</p>
<p>change the values. The largest disparity is 9% (sample 8) and with the measurement 
</p>
<p>uncertainty cannot be shown statistically different. 
</p>
<p> The average maximum profile height for the vertical (Pz) measurement is 
</p>
<p>grouped tighter for the coarse powder and similar for the fine powder. The high 
</p>
<p>measurement uncertainty for the coarse powder represents the random coarse particle 
</p>
<p>adherence causing larger peaks and valleys. The Pz of the inner surface is generally 
</p>
<p>higher than the outer surface, but variations can be attributed to location dependency. 
</p>
<p>The Pku is similar to the Sku and the surface is dominated by a high degree of peaks 
</p>
<p>and valleys for all coarse samples and to a lesser degree the outer surface fine powder 
</p>
<p>samples. The peaks (Pp) and valleys (Pv) for the inner surface is observed in Figure 
</p>
<p>3.9A and Figure 3.9D for the outer surface. The Psk indicates that peaks are still 
</p>
<p>dominate on most of the samples. The 2D profile values, specifically Pa, Pz, Pp, Pv, 
</p>
<p>Pku, Psk are also important to report since fluid flow in heat exchangers is directional 
</p>
<p>and may be used in flow performance evaluations such as friction factors. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—53 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.9. Directional vertical texture per sample. A) Average profile height 
</p>
<p>[Pa], B) Average maximum profile height [Pz], C) Measured peaks [Pp] and 
</p>
<p>valleys [Pv] for inner surface, D) Measured peaks [Pp] and valleys [Pv] for 
</p>
<p>inner surface. 
</p>
<p>3.4.5 Waviness 
</p>
<p>Typical LP-DED samples have shown layering undulations from the dilution of 
</p>
<p>vertical melt pools created on the sidewalls forming waviness [120,215]. In prior 
</p>
<p>literature this layering is apparent in the cross-section of the deposited samples 
</p>
<p>[92,119]. Within this current study, the layering and undulations are not visible in the 
</p>
<p>sectioned samples. The vertical waviness is shown in Figure 3.10 and shown to be 
</p>
<p>more preferential to the inner surface. The dotted lines are provided to enhance 
</p>
<p>pairing of samples for readability. The coarse samples vary by 15% of the average, 
</p>
<p>with the exception of sample 12. The coarse samples did not show any statistical 
</p>
<p>trends in waviness compared to layer height, while the fine samples indicated 
</p>
<p>increased waviness with increased layer height. The finer powder PSD samples have 
</p>
<p>a higher degree of variation showing higher sensitivity to the process parameters and </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—54 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>resulting melt pool geometry [213]. Sample 30 has higher waviness compared to 
</p>
<p>other samples with all the deposition parameters set at the highest values. The 
</p>
<p>increased layer height in addition to higher power and increased powder feedrate 
</p>
<p>causes the melt pool to spread, resulting in higher waviness. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.10. Vertical waviness for inner and outer surfaces. 
</p>
<p>3.4.6 DOE Results 
</p>
<p> The DOE data were analyzed using ANOVA with the process parameters as 
</p>
<p>the factors and the surface roughness (Sa) data as the response. The final model that 
</p>
<p>best fit the data was a two-factor interaction (2FI), with several terms eliminated that 
</p>
<p>were not significant. The results from the ANOVA of the inner surface roughness are 
</p>
<p>provided in Table 3.3 with an overall R2 fit of 97.8% for the selected factors. The 
</p>
<p>results show that powder size is the major contributor at 71% of the overall model, 
</p>
<p>as observed in the average areal roughness data. The layer height is also a contributor 
</p>
<p>and the interaction between layer height and powder size at 10% each. Other 
</p>
<p>interactions of the parameters were significant in the model, but not major 
</p>
<p>contributors to the texture or roughness. Prior studies by Gharbi et al. and also Ahsan 
</p>
<p>showed a correlation between the roughness and the energy density that as energy 
</p>
<p>density increased the roughness also increased [120,216]. A major difference in those 
</p>
<p>studies compared to this was a significant variation in power, where this study 
</p>
<p>focused on a 5% difference. This study was also focused on maintaining a consistent </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—55 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>and thin wall, so significant increases in energy density would increase the melt pool 
</p>
<p>diameter and subsequent wall thickness. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 3.3. ANOVA for average inner surface roughness (Sa). 
</p>

<p>3.4.7  Porosity 
</p>
<p>The porosity of each sample compared to the Sa values is shown in Figure 3.11. 
</p>
<p>All fine powder samples have a measured porosity of less than 0.061%, average of 
</p>
<p>0.031% ±0.01% and low value of 0.014% (range of 0.047%) consistent among all 
</p>
<p>fine PSD samples. The coarse powder samples had a much wider distribution of 
</p>
<p>porosity and ranged from 0.052% up to 0.307% and average of 0.13% ±0.07%.   
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—56 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.11. Porosity measurements in samples compared to inner average 
</p>
<p>areal surface roughness (Sa). 
</p>
<p>3.4.8 Sample Wall Thickness 
</p>
<p>Thickness variation was evaluated through direct measurements of the cross 
</p>
<p>sectioned samples. The results of the overall thicknesses along with powder size and 
</p>
<p>laser power are shown in Figure 3.12. An ANOVA was also established to evaluate 
</p>
<p>the build parameters impacting thickness. While the model showed several factors 
</p>
<p>were significant for several factors and interactions, the R2 fit was only 82.7%. A 
</p>
<p>major contributor to the thickness model was laser power (25.3%) and travel speed 
</p>
<p>(22.3%). Layer height had a small contribution (5.5%) as well as powder size (4.6%) 
</p>
<p>and interactions of the parameters (16.6%). Figure 3.12 indicates some visual 
</p>
<p>correlation of thickness to laser power, although all parameters are being adjusted 
</p>
<p>simultaneously.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—57 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.12. Sample cross-sectional thickness compared to powder PSD and 
</p>
<p>laser power. 
</p>
<p> The process hardware and setup for building the desired wall thickness can 
</p>
<p>have an impact on the final surface roughness. The melt pool resulting in 1 mm wall 
</p>
<p>thickness results in a lower catchment efficiency of 5-20% [120]. The powder that is 
</p>
<p>captured in the melt pool is often drawn to the center due to Marangoni flow [75]. 
</p>
<p>Particles that are not fully molten can be expelled from the melt pool and adhere to 
</p>
<p>the sidewalls or trailing edge [208]. The residual powder (80-95%) not captured is 
</p>
<p>then available to bond to the trailing edge of the melt pool in the outmost surface and 
</p>
<p>then rapidly cooled [217,218]. This is further exasperated in thin-walls due to low 
</p>
<p>thermal mass and accelerated cooling rate. For practical applications of the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process, the geometry also needs to be considered.  There can be differences in 
</p>
<p>powder consumed during the process based on primitive (simple) geometry compared 
</p>
<p>to complex surface geometry and dependent upon laser-on time (building versus 
</p>
<p>positional movements).  
</p>
<p> The Pz parameter is compared with the Pa parameter and plotted as a 
</p>
<p>function of layer height in Figure 3.13. The coarse powder does not show a sensitivity 
</p>
<p>to average or maximum texture profile based on layer height. The fine powder shows 
</p>
<p>a more apparent trend with the lower layer height providing a lower average and 
</p>
<p>maximum texture profile, although data does not match for all samples with other 
</p>
<p>parameter variations.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—58 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.13. Comparison of average profile height (Pa) to average maximum 
</p>
<p>profile height (Pz). 
</p>
<p>The vertical orientation along the direction of the build was the primary interest 
</p>
<p>for surface texture, but horizontal profile texture was also evaluated to evaluate 
</p>
<p>differences. This is shown in Figure 3.14. The line on the graph is provided as a 
</p>
<p>reference assuming an arbitrary slope of 1. The coarse powder PSD indicates little 
</p>
<p>difference (1%) between the vertical and horizontal directions. The fine powder 
</p>
<p>profile is biased toward the vertical direction with a difference of 9%.   
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
