</p>
<p>Chapter 3—59 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.14. Comparison of vertical and horizontal average profile height, Pa. 
</p>
<p>3.5 DISCUSSION 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The finer powder has more instances of subsurface particles than the coarse 
</p>
<p>particles observed in the micrographs, because of the propensity for rapid 
</p>
<p>solidification during the build [219]. This does not impact the surface texture and the 
</p>
<p>finer powder still provides lower values overall. While literature reports that the finer 
</p>
<p>powder tends to have flowability issues [220,221], there were no issues observed with 
</p>
<p>the 3-nozzle system used in this study. The fine powder samples indicate that the 
</p>
<p>inner surface is predominately peaks while the outer surface is mostly valleys. The 
</p>
<p>high degree of peaks for the inner surface indicates that powder particles are adhering 
</p>
<p>to the surface as the NASA HR-1 starts to cool (ie. excess powder capture). Due to 
</p>
<p>the reduced volume, the finer powder requires less thermal energy to melt [222]. The 
</p>
<p>finer powder PSD combined with the higher likelihood of powder trapped and 
</p>
<p>recirculating in the enclosed racetrack increases the excess powder capture to the 
</p>
<p>inner surface. During deposition of the fine powder, it was observed that powder was 
</p>
<p>suspended in the build chamber, which was likely the lower end of the fine PSD (less 
</p>
<p>than 25-30 µm). The higher velocity of the fine powder in the gas stream is ejected 
</p>
<p>from the nozzle and causes rebounds off solidified surfaces [223]. The coarse </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—60 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>particles were shown to be more likely to break through the surface tension of the 
</p>
<p>melt pool and likely to be melted or partially solidified [75]. 
</p>
<p>While the Sa had a tighter range for the coarse powder than the fine powder, the 
</p>
<p>opposite was true when evaluating with the Sz parameter (Figure 3.8B). This 
</p>
<p>indicates a higher range in the peaks and valleys with the coarse powder, which 
</p>
<p>follows the visual observations of the full adherence or partial melting of the coarse 
</p>
<p>powder overcoming the surface tension. There are limited instances of unmelted 
</p>
<p>coarse powder observed subsurface. The coarse powder samples are dominated by 
</p>
<p>the peaks for the inner and outer surface with the solidified material forming a 
</p>
<p>tangential radius between the partially melted particles. The fine powder has a high 
</p>
<p>concentration of unmelted particles across the surface and subsurface. The fine 
</p>
<p>powder shows an approximate split of peaks and valleys, although valleys shown to 
</p>
<p>be marginally higher for most samples. The concentration of these unmelted particles 
</p>
<p>make up these peaks and valleys bonded by solidified material (Figure 7F).  
</p>
<p>Uniform and consistent roughness is a significant consideration in component 
</p>
<p>design fabricated from LP-DED, but the material quality and process ability to meet 
</p>
<p>geometric requirements consistently is equally important. These factors can include 
</p>
<p>the material density and the wall thickness. The lower porosity with fine powder has 
</p>
<p>been observed in studies in other alloys [224,225], which can also be related to the 
</p>
<p>increase in efficiency for the finer powder [226]. Townsend et al. points out that 
</p>
<p>surface conditions may act as a fundamental observation to more underlying material 
</p>
<p>defects [227]. The results from the current study align with this observation in that 
</p>
<p>the reduced surface texture also resulted in a material with lower levels of porosity, 
</p>
<p>although no significant defects were noted in either set of samples with varying 
</p>
<p>powder PSD. This alone does not indicate that mechanical and thermophysical 
</p>
<p>properties will match intended requirements, and the properties need to be confirmed 
</p>
<p>with testing.  
</p>
<p> A prior study by Jinoop et al. indicates 38% increases in wall thickness with 
</p>
<p>83% increase in power [95]. Other studies also reference the increase of the wall 
</p>
<p>thickness due to the larger melt pool observed with increased laser power [228,229]. 
</p>
<p>Low laser power can generate lack of fusion and detrimental effects on surface 
</p>
<p>roughness and mechanical behavior, so a proper balance is required [230]. The 
</p>
<p>reduction of travel speed also leads to increased wall thickness [231]. Fine powders 
</p>
<p>allow for uniform flow in the deposition nozzle and promote a small melt pool from 
</p>
<p>the laser [68]. However, in this study there was not a significant difference observed 
</p>
<p>in the wall thicknesses between the fine and coarse powder with the power changes 
</p>
<p>observed. The NASA HR-1 alloy also has tendency to be more sluggish during the 
</p>
<p>build and the melt pool does not tend to flow out compared to other alloys.  
</p>
<p> The mechanisms that produce surface texture in the vertical and horizontal 
</p>
<p>directions differ for thin-walls. The vertical roughness is related to the bead geometry 
</p>
<p>(width and height), layer height, and the adherence of unmelted (residual) and 
</p>
<p>partially melted powder to the outmost surfaces. The horizontal roughness relates to 
</p>
<p>the adherence of the residual and partially melted powder assuming measurements 
</p>
<p>are made nearly parallel with the bead. The low difference between the vertical and 
</p>
<p>horizontal surfaces from this study provides validation that the surface roughness is 
</p>
<p>near uniform, and the anisotropy is coming from the waviness. The parameters </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—61 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>selected in this study provide good dilution of the melt pool eliminating 
</p>
<p>discontinuities between layers for an optimal processing window [232,233].  
</p>
<p> The major contributor to roughness in this study is the powder PSD with 
</p>
<p>fine powder providing lower areal roughness and directional profile texture. The 
</p>
<p>surface roughness map for samples 32 (coarse powder) and 34 (fine powder) is shown 
</p>
<p>in Figure 3.15. The particles are bonded at various time periods during the 
</p>
<p>solidification of the NASA HR-1 with some fully encapsulated with melted material 
</p>
<p>and others partially bonded. The fine powder has a much higher sensitivity to the 
</p>
<p>build parameters compared to the coarse powder when build parameters are changed. 
</p>
<p>The volumetric heat capacity of the fine powder is 18% of the coarse powder and will 
</p>
<p>tend to heat up quickly in the laser beam and become fully molten as it is injected 
</p>
<p>into the melt pool [234].  
</p>
<p> The layer height did have a significant impact, albeit lower influence on 
</p>
<p>surface texture. There was a higher degree of waviness present on the inner surface 
</p>
<p>of the racetracks. Many prior studies showed the layering and obvious lateral menisci 
</p>
<p>from LP-DED process. This was not readily apparent in the 1 mm thick NASA HR-
</p>
<p>1 samples, and waviness is not impacted by layer height. The directional surface 
</p>
<p>roughness was within 9% for horizontal and vertical directions and considered 
</p>
<p>uniform. 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.15. Comparison of areal surface roughness map for coarse and fine 
</p>
<p>powder, inner and outer surface. 
</p>
<p> Based on the average, the inner surface has 1.67 µm higher roughness than 
</p>
<p>the outer surface. This difference was also confirmed to be significant with a paired 
</p>
<p>t-test for the mean at 0.01 significance level (99% confidence). This difference was 
</p>
<p>also more pronounced in the fine powder sample (2.3 µm average) compared to the 
</p>
<p>coarse powder (1.2 µm). It should be noted that there are four runs where the outer 
</p>
<p>surface is higher than the inner surface. However, the differences were similar at 0.2 
</p>
<p>– 0.3 µm, while one sample was 1 µm, and all parameters were modified in unison. 
</p>
<p>The commonality of these four runs is when layer height is set to the lowest (0.229 
</p>
<p>mm) or travel speed is set to the highest (800 mm/s).  
</p>
<p> Based on the DOE results and observed changes in parameters, the surface 
</p>
<p>roughness can be reduced. The contour plots observing Sa as the factor are shown in 
</p>
<p>Figure 3.16. Similar plots were completed for vertical Ra showing nearly identical </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—62 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>results. To reduce surface roughness to the lowest value for the NASA HR-1, the 
</p>
<p>layer height should be set to the lowest values along with using the fine powder. The 
</p>
<p>interaction of the other parameters only had minor contributions and did not impact 
</p>
<p>results enough, so were maintained at nominal values. The fine powder and low layer 
</p>
<p>height also provided low porosity material and desired wall thicknesses.  
</p>
<p> A practical balance of parameters should also be considered as results are 
</p>
<p>used for parts production. While surface roughness was reduced with the lower layer 
</p>
<p>height (0.229 mm) from the nominal (0.254 mm), the build time would increase. The 
</p>
<p>roughness is increased from 15.6 µm at the lower layer height by 1 µm when the layer 
</p>
<p>height is increased to 0.254 mm. This represents a 6% increase in average roughness 
</p>
<p>(Sa), but a build time increase of 17%. The build time was calculated with a simple 
</p>
<p>primitive geometry model changing the layer height, assuming a constant travel 
</p>
<p>speed.  
</p>
<p> 
Figure 3.16. Contour plot for average areal surface roughness with powder size 
</p>
<p>and layer height interaction. The powder size is shown in maximum value (45 
</p>
<p>and 105 µm) and average areal roughness (Sa) units in µm. 
</p>
<p>3.6 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Industrialization of additively manufactured parts requires a detailed 
</p>
<p>characterization of the microstructure and the resulting geometry such as wall 
</p>
<p>thickness, defects such as porosity, and the surface texture for successful use in 
</p>
<p>critical applications. In this study, closed racetrack NASA HR-1 1 mm thin-wall 
</p>
<p>samples were built using the LP-DED process with rotary atomized fine and coarse 
</p>
<p>powder to evaluate surface texture (roughness and waviness), basic geometry, and 
</p>
<p>microstructure. These samples were successfully built using a single-bead deposition 
</p>
<p>wall to a height of 25 mm. A design of experiments (DOE) was setup to evaluate the 
</p>
<p>variation in parameters to determine the correlation and influence of the build 
</p>
<p>parameters and powder particle size distribution (PSD). A total of 35 samples were </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—63 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>successfully deposited with various parameters and were measured using a non-
</p>
<p>contact patterned light projection profilometer in the as-built condition. The samples 
</p>
<p>were also evaluated using optical microscopy and scanning electron microscope 
</p>
<p>(SEM) and sectioned to evaluate thickness, porosity, and roughness. The results are 
</p>
<p>presented and summarized, and the following conclusions are drawn from the NASA 
</p>
<p>HR-1, 1 mm thin-wall samples. 
</p>
<p>• Surface roughness of LP-DED material is derived from excess or partially 
</p>
<p>melted powder captured on the outmost surface of the trailing edge melt 
</p>
<p>pool. The areal average surface roughness (Sa) is thus dominated by peaks 
</p>
<p>of these particles. 
</p>
<p>• Partially or unmelted powder can be solidified in the subsurface and was 
</p>
<p>observed to be a maximum of 2x the diameter of the powder PSD.  
</p>
<p>• Fine powder (10-45 µm) produces on average roughness 23% lower 
</p>
<p>compared to the coarse powder (45-105 µm). 
</p>
<p>• The inner (enclosed) surface shows a higher average surface roughness, 
</p>
<p>texture, and waviness compared to the outer surface due to ricochets, 
</p>
<p>recirculation, and higher volume of powder available to bond to the surface 
</p>
<p>during solidification. This is further exasperated with thin-wall depositions 
</p>
<p>where powder efficiency is reduced and more powder available to bond to 
</p>
<p>the outmost surface during solidification.  
</p>
<p>• The resulting surface roughness from the fine powder indicated a higher 
</p>
<p>sensitivity to the parameter changes where the roughness with the coarse 
</p>
<p>powder showed little change.  
</p>
<p>• The analysis of variance indicated that the powder, layer height, and 
</p>
<p>interaction of these accounted for 90% of the inner surface roughness and 
</p>
<p>95% of the outer surface roughness.  
</p>
<p>• The fine powder produced samples with lower overall porosity and averaged 
</p>
<p>0.03% ±0.01%, while the coarse samples had a higher range and averaged 
</p>
<p>0.13% ±0.07%   
</p>
<p>• A wall thickness of 1.05 to 1.15 mm can be properly maintained with either 
</p>
<p>coarse or fine powder and related to the laser power. 
</p>
<p>• Waviness was minimized using a lower laser powder (&lt;333 W), higher layer 
</p>
<p>height (&lt;0.28 mm) along with increased travel speed (800 mm/min) and 
</p>
<p>increased powder feed rate (24.2 grams/min) to produce a uniform surface 
</p>
<p>with minor waviness. The typically reported menisci of layering were not 
</p>
<p>observed in the NASA HR-1 thin-wall samples deposited with the optimized 
</p>
<p>parameters and 3-nozzle configuration.  
</p>
<p>• Surface roughness can be minimized by using fine powder, laser power of 
</p>
<p>350 W, powder feedrate of 23 g/min, travel speed of 762 mm/min, and a low 
</p>
<p>layer height of 0.229 mm. While the surface roughness is reduced at these 
</p>
<p>parameters a balance should also be evaluated based on process economics. 
</p>
<p>With the decreased layer height, the build time is increased by 17%. The 
</p>
<p>layer height could be increased to 0.254 mm, and the impact on surface 
</p>
<p>roughness would be a 6% increase (+1 µm). 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 3 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3—64 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The LP-DED process is maturing and was demonstrated to successfully deposit 
</p>
<p>freeform thin-wall features. This process can provide an alternative to the laser 
</p>
<p>powder bed fusion (L-PBF) process for large-scale parts that require thin-walls, such 
</p>
<p>as heat exchangers with internal channels or flow passages. While AM has shown 
</p>
<p>surface roughness higher than traditionally manufactured parts, this study showcases 
</p>
<p>a detailed understanding of the surface textures. The understanding of surface texture 
</p>
<p>is critical for increased or debits to flow performance in heat exchangers allowing 
</p>
<p>enhanced heat transfer or increases in pressure drop. This study also demonstrated 
</p>
<p>the repeatability of LP-DED thin-walls and a high-density deposition using the 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 alloy for use in high-pressure hydrogen applications.   
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—65 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4 Chapter 4 – Influence of Angles 
</p>
<p>on Surface Texture and 
</p>
<p>Geometry 
 
</p>
<p>Major portions of this chapter were published in: 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Gradl, P., Cervone, A., Colonna, P., 2023. Influence of build angles on thin-wall 
</p>
<p>geometry and surface texture in laser powder directed energy deposition. Materials 
</p>
<p>&amp; Design 234, 112352. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.matdes.2023.112352 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.1 ABSTRACT 
</p>
<p>The characterization of geometric capabilities and limitations is critical for 
</p>
<p>establishing guidelines for end users of the technology. Within this study, several 
</p>
<p>samples of enclosed vertical tracks were fabricated and characterized using LP-DED, 
</p>
<p>with 1 mm-thick walls and varying inclination angles up to 45° using the NASA HR-
</p>
<p>1 alloy (Fe-Ni-Cr). The wall thickness, melt pool, and surface texture, inclusive of 
</p>
<p>waviness and roughness, were evaluated and results presented. The experimental 
</p>
<p>results indicate that the wall thickness increases exponentially above 30°. The surface 
</p>
<p>texture was shown to be dependent on 1) excess powder adherence, 2) melt pool 
</p>
<p>irregularities causing material droop, and 3) excess material. The experiment revealed 
</p>
<p>that the mean roughness reduces with increasing wall angle for the downskin. The 
</p>
<p>upskin roughness reaches a maximum peak at 20° and slowly reduces as powder 
</p>
<p>adheres within valleys. Both the downskin and upskin surface textures are dominated 
</p>
<p>by irregular waviness generated by the melt pool.  
</p>
<p>4.2 BACKGROUND 
</p>
<p>Research has been conducted to explore the feasibility of creating vertical single-
</p>
<p>bead thin-walls (&lt;1.5 mm) using the LP-DED process, but focused on materials 
</p>
<p>characterization and mechanical testing of vertical or horizontal surfaces 
</p>
<p>[92,94,103,104,118,190,195,235–237]. A gap in prior LP-DED studies is the 
</p>
<p>characterization of thin-wall geometric limitations including build angles, features 
</p>
<p>(holes, channels, slots, etc), thickness variation, surface texture, and the combination 
</p>
<p>of these attributes [5,238–240]. These features, including thin-walls and internal flow 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4 
 </p>
<p></p>
<div class="annotation"><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.matdes.2023.112352">https://doi.org/10.1016/j.matdes.2023.112352</a></div>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—66 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>passages, are common in complex AM parts, particularly in applications such as 
</p>
<p>aerospace or industrial heat exchangers. Thin-wall feature characterization is 
</p>
<p>particularly important due to decreased powder efficiency in the process, which can 
</p>
<p>impact the geometry [120]. The understanding of the DfAM guidelines for thin-wall 
</p>
<p>LP-DED bridge the divide between fundamental research and practical 
</p>
<p>implementation [74].  
</p>
<p>A few studies explored LP-DED of thin angled walls limited to stainless steel 
</p>
<p>(SS) alloys. Kalami and Urbanic conducted LP-DED experiments to produce a SS 
</p>
<p>410 dome structure with 2 mm walls, which focused on developing an algorithm for 
</p>
<p>process planning multi-axis toolpaths and collision avoidance of the deposition head 
</p>
<p>and the part [97]. The research was expanded to study surface roughness (Ra) and 
</p>
<p>concluded that the interfaces between toolpath partitions caused significant changes 
</p>
<p>in roughness [208]. As a result of utilizing partitioned toolpaths and maintaining the 
</p>
<p>deposition head in a normal position, no definitive conclusions could be drawn 
</p>
<p>regarding the impact of changing angles on surface roughness. Kaj et al. studied 2 
</p>
<p>mm walls deposited with LP-DED SS 316L and focused on 5-axis motion control 
</p>
<p>[183]. The research indicated a 32.5° wall was achievable with LP-DED but did not 
</p>
<p>provide any corresponding surface texture or roughness data. Wang et al. deposited 
</p>
<p>1 mm thick angled walls up to 32.9° using SS 17-4PH with collapses occurring above 
</p>
<p>that angle [241]. The research concluded that lowering the layer height (Z-increment) 
</p>
<p>to 0.43 mm resulted in more consistent angles (+/-0.9°), but also collapsed above 31°; 
</p>
<p>no data on the surface texture was included. 
</p>
<p>Surface texture is another key geometric attribute for design since it can impact 
</p>
<p>mechanical properties such as fatigue life, and, in case of internal fluid flows, heat 
</p>
<p>transfer, and friction factors [106]. It is well known that the surface texture for AM 
</p>
<p>produced parts is higher compared to traditional manufacturing techniques [242,243]. 
</p>
<p>Proper filtering of measurements is of paramount importance when analyzing surface 
</p>
<p>texture, encompassing form, waviness, and roughness. When defining roughness as 
</p>
<p>an input for fluid flow analysis, relying solely on roughness data may not provide an 
</p>
<p>accurate representation of the complete surface profile. [127,244]. The combination 
</p>
<p>of height, spatial, hybrid, and functional parameters within comprehensive parameter 
</p>
<p>groups enables a holistic understanding of the surface properties. [245]. Each of these 
</p>
<p>texture parameters aid in describing results from manufacturing processes or end-use 
</p>
<p>function (i.e. wear, lubrication, friction, fatigue, sealing, etc) [245]. Surface texture 
</p>
<p>measurement is important during manufacturing to validate design assumptions and 
</p>
<p>reduce uncertainty. It is also critical to measure and characterize surfaces during 
</p>
<p>manufacturing operations. These experimental data are used to discern the underlying 
</p>
<p>mechanisms behind the observed texture, such as waviness and roughness. This helps 
</p>
<p>guide the total amount of material that needs to be removed during subsequent 
</p>
<p>polishing or machining operations if a certain texture is required.  
</p>
<p>Surface texture is a direct outcome of the AM build process and is influenced by 
</p>
<p>various factors, including the build orientation, wall angles, feedstock, process 
</p>
<p>parameters, wall thickness, and the material being used [68]. It is known for L-PBF 
</p>
<p>that surface texture varies significantly based on the angle and orientation (upskin or 
</p>
<p>downskin) of a surface [126,246]. It has been shown that increasing the angle of 
</p>
<p>downskin surfaces, relative to the build direction, increases the surface roughness due </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—67 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>partially to unmelted powder adherence and dross formation [112,247]. Dross is 
</p>
<p>excess material from overheating that forms at surface as the laser melts the powder 
</p>
<p>and the material subsequently cools [85,248]. These studies all focused on evaluating 
</p>
<p>the upskin and downskin surface texture related to L-PBF, but a gap exists related to 
</p>
<p>this characterization for LP-DED.   
</p>
<p>Prior studies about L-PBF and LP-DED indicated that the observed stair step (or 
</p>
<p>stair case) effect increases the surface roughness [115,236,249]. The stair stepping is 
</p>
<p>the result of slicing the part into defined layers. The part is built in these layers with 
</p>
<p>a determined height increase (Z orientation step up) of the deposition head that is 
</p>
<p>programmed. The step is a resultant of these layers, which can be more pronounced 
</p>
<p>as the surface angle increases relative to the build direction [209,250]. While the 
</p>
<p>literature addresses the impact of the stair step as a form of roughness, it often does 
</p>
<p>not segregate the data to distinguish waviness, creating a gap with respect to the full 
</p>
<p>characterization of the LP-DED process.  
</p>
<p>This chapter summarizes research that used a variety of experimental data to 
</p>
<p>characterize the geometry dependency of various samples fabricated using LP-DED. 
</p>
<p>The study focuses on 1 mm wall thickness samples fabricated at angles up to 45° 
</p>
<p>using hydrogen-resistant NASA HR-1 (Fe-Ni-Cr) alloy. These samples were 
</p>
<p>deposited as freeform “racetracks”, with a straight wall on each side and 180 bends. 
</p>
<p>The purpose of this experiment was to characterize deposition angle dependency for 
</p>
<p>build limitations, upskin and downskin surface texture, wall thickness, and general 
</p>
<p>microstructure. An equation was developed to predict wall thickness. These results 
</p>
<p>are intended to advance the understanding of DfAM for the LP-DED process and to 
</p>
<p>establish attributes for design and research engineers. The experimental results 
</p>
<p>obtained are specifically applicable to the NASA HR-1 alloy. However, it is worth 
</p>
<p>noting that the observed trends may have broader applicability to other alloys, 
</p>
<p>provided that additional data collection is conducted. 
</p>
<p>4.3 METHODOLOGY 
</p>
<p>4.3.1 DED System and Samples 
</p>
<p>Ten NASA HR-1 alloy samples at angles varying every five degrees were 
</p>
<p>deposited on an RPM Innovations (RPMI) 557 LP-DED machine with an argon-inert 
</p>
<p>build chamber. The 557 system incorporated an infrared (IR) continuous wave 
</p>
<p>gaussian profile 3 kW IPG laser, three coaxial nozzle within the deposition head, 5-
</p>
<p>axis motion control, and disc powder feeder with agitation capabilities. The motion 
</p>
<p>control system was a Cartesian gantry-type system with a tilt and rotate trunnion 
</p>
<p>table, although the trunnion table was not used in this experiment. The samples were 
</p>
<p>built using a 2+1 axis approach, meaning each layer was deposited and the deposition 
</p>
<p>head translated in height by +0.254 mm to deposit the next layer. The deposition head 
</p>
<p>remained perpendicular to the build plate for all samples.  
</p>
<p>The samples were deposited as an enclosed racetrack (straight walls and 180° 
</p>
<p>radii) to provide for dimensional stability. The baseline sample was built vertically, 
</p>
<p>with the identifier referencing the build direction (0°); subsequent samples are </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—68 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>identified in reference to the angle offset from the vertical build direction (5°, 10°, 
</p>
<p>…45°). The baseline 0° sample geometry was identical to a prior study in which 
</p>
<p>deposition parameters were varied using the same NASA HR-1 alloy [251]. While 
</p>
<p>the use of 5-axis systems is possible, it is not feasible for every part and the practical 
</p>
<p>application of complex LP-DED parts still uses a 2+1 (layer-wise slicing) and a 
</p>
<p>combination of multi-axis toolpath planning. The samples were single-width passes 
</p>
<p>(1 mm) at 76 mm length (Y-direction), 25 mm height (Z-direction), and 25 mm span 
</p>
<p>between the adjacent wall (X-direction). The samples were built on an A36 mild steel 
</p>
<p>12 mm thick base plate. The layers for each sample were built individually, but 
</p>
<p>simultaneously to allow for proper cooling between passes. All samples were 
</p>
<p>evaluated in the as-built condition with no stress relief or other heat treatments and 
</p>
<p>no surface cleaning or post-processing. The samples were removed from the build 
</p>
<p>plate using a bandsaw and deburred on the bottom edge only. The samples from each 
</p>
<p>angle are shown in Figure 4.1.  
</p>
<p>The upskin is referred to as the surface angled upward relative to the deposition 
</p>
<p>head. This is also the outer surface on all samples. The downskin is the downward 
</p>
<p>facing and the inner surface. The upskin and downskin terminology is consistent with 
</p>
<p>previous L-PBF research [42].  
</p>
<p>The samples were built successfully up to 30° (Figure 4.1). The 35° and 40° 
</p>
<p>specimens featured discontinuities in the radius. The 45° specimen had 
</p>
<p>discontinuities in the radius and failed in the opposing wall. Once the deposition had 
</p>
<p>challenges in a layer, this discontinuity would propagate and become worse with 
</p>
<p>increased height. The straight wall that was successfully built served as the basis for 
</p>
<p>all characterization and analysis. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.1. As-built NASA HR-1 alloy angled specimens removed from the 
</p>
<p>build plate. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—69 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.3.2 Deposition Parameters 
</p>
<p>The deposition parameters used for the experiment are listed in Table 4.1. The 
</p>
<p>optimal parameters were selected based on a prior design of experiments to optimize 
</p>
<p>high density microstructure and reduced build time [77,251,252]. Argon was used as 
</p>
<p>the carrier and shielding gas. The deposition head was fixed normal to the surface 
</p>
<p>and the standoff distance was actively monitored and controlled. The layer height 
</p>
<p>remained constant at 0.254 mm and X-axis translation adjusted based on the angle of 
</p>
<p>the sample. The start and stop points were randomized for each sample.  The trunnion 
</p>
<p>table was locked and not used for these samples. While acknowledging that the use 
</p>
<p>of a trunnion table could potentially increase the maximum build angle, it is important 
</p>
<p>to note that this build approach for thin-wall complex hardware designs may not 
</p>
<p>always be feasible and is not the primary focus of this study.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 4.1. Parameters used for angled racetrack sample deposition. 
</p>
<p>Power 
</p>
<p>(W) 
</p>
<p>Powder Feed rate 
</p>
<p>(grams/min) 
</p>
<p>Travel Speed 
</p>
<p>(mm/min) 
</p>
<p>Layer Height 
</p>
<p>(mm) 
</p>
<p>350 23 762 0.254 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.3.3 NASA HR-1 Alloy 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 is a Fe-Ni-Cr superalloy used in high pressure hydrogen 
</p>
<p>components, such as heat exchangers. It was developed for resistance to hydrogen 
</p>
<p>environment embrittlement (HEE).  NASA HR-1 provides high strength and high 
</p>
<p>ductility in harsh hydrogen environments such as liquid rocket engine nozzles and 
</p>
<p>heat exchangers and it is derived from the A-286 and JBK-75 alloys [253,254]. The 
</p>
<p>powder was rotary atomized by Homogenized Metals Inc. (HMI), with a particle size 
</p>
<p>distribution (PSD) of 55-105 µm meeting +140 mesh at 0% and -325 mesh at 3.2% 
</p>
<p>per ASTM B214 using a Microtrac (Ver 11.1.0.6), and chemical composition 
</p>
<p>provided in Table 2 (HMI powder lot HRA7). A SEM image of the morphology is 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 3. The Oxygen content was 59 ppm and Nitrogen content was 9 ppm. 
</p>
<p>The chemistry was measured using Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP) at HMI. The 
</p>
<p>peak powder size was 69 µm and a Vega3 Tescan Scanning Electron Microscope 
</p>
<p>(SEM) using Backscatter Electron (BSE) showed mostly spherical particles with a 
</p>
<p>few satellites and random distribution of occasional oblong particles. The powder 
</p>
<p>cumulative distribution has 10% of particle sized below 58 µm (D10), 90% below 85 
</p>
<p>µm (D90), and 99% below 105 µm (D99) with a median particle size of 69 µm. Virgin 
</p>
<p>powder was used for all builds. The samples were evaluated in the non-heat treated 
</p>
<p>(as-built) condition.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 4.2. Chemical composition of NASA HR-1 alloy powder (HMI Lot HRA7). 
</p>
<p> Fe Ni Cr Co Mo Ti Al V W 
</p>
<p>Wt. % Bal. 34.03 14.64 3.75 1.84 2.46 0.23 0.3 1.59 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—70 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.2. SEM image of NASA HR-1 (55-105 µm) powder morphology. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.3.4 Surface Metrology and Imaging 
</p>
<p>Surface texture measurements were obtained using a non-contact Keyence VR-
</p>
<p>5200 pattern light projection profilometer. The setup included three telecentric lenses 
</p>
<p>with 80x magnification capability and an overlap of 20%. Each sample was measured 
</p>
<p>on the upskin (outer) surface and then sectioned inside of the radii to allow access to 
</p>
<p>the inner surface. The downskin (inner) surface was then measured. The surface 
</p>
<p>scanning covered a total area of 43 mm by 16 mm shown in Figure 4. Areal surface 
</p>
<p>measurements were reported in addition to directional legacy measurements. The 
</p>
<p>texture measurements are reported along with a brief explanation and mathematical 
</p>
<p>definition [244,245,255] in Table 3.   
</p>

<p>Arithmetic mean 
</p>
<p>directional waviness (Wa) – 
</p>
<p>average height of widely 
</p>
<p>spaced component of the 
</p>
<p>overall profile along a 
</p>
<p>direction such as parallel 
</p>
<p>(vertical) to the build 
</p>
<p>direction or perpendicular 
</p>
<p>(horizontal). Data are filtered 
</p>
<p>using &lt; λc, which removes 
</p>
<p>form and waviness of the 
</p>
<p>surface. 
</p>

</p>
<p>𝐿𝑥
∫ |𝑍(𝑥)| 𝑑𝑥
</p>

</p>

</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>*Sk is approximated and detailed procedures to obtain can be found in [256] and [207]. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.3. Terminology for sample measurement (example of 20° wall 
</p>
<p>sample). </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—73 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>All areal surface measurements are reported according to ISO 25178-2:2021 
</p>
<p>[255,257]. A surface form correction was applied to remove any tilt and curvature 
</p>
<p>from any residual stress distortion of the samples. The data around the edges of the 
</p>
<p>measurement was eliminated using an end effect correction. A reference plane was 
</p>
<p>then established. For the area surface measurements, the primary texture profile was 
</p>
<p>unfiltered (denoted as primary) using form correction only. To extract roughness 
</p>
<p>from the areal surface, a λc spatial frequency cut-off filter of 0.8 mm (i.e., a roughness 
</p>
<p>filter) was applied per ISO 4288-1996. A λc of 2.5 mm was also evaluated but was 
</p>
<p>too large. Measurement uncertainty was obtained by repeating measurements on a 
</p>
<p>single sample five times to determine a 95% confidence interval. 
</p>
<p>When analyzing texture measurements, it is important to consider the end use 
</p>
<p>application to obtain appropriate results [208]. One potential application of the thin-
</p>
<p>wall LP-DED process is the fabrication of heat exchangers with integral channel fluid 
</p>
<p>flow. These heat exchangers are built with the channels running in the vertical build 
</p>
<p>direction (Z). Thus, the directional measurements are reported in the vertical build 
</p>
<p>direction (Figure 4). Five symmetrical vertical lines were placed on the sample image 
</p>
<p>and measurements averaged to report the waviness and roughness. For the directional 
</p>
<p>measurements, form correction was also applied along with λc of 0.8 mm and a λs of 
</p>
<p>2.5 µm to eliminate noise at a 300:1 ratio (λc/ λs) per ISO 4228-1996. This allowed 
</p>
<p>for the segmentation of directional waviness (Wa) and roughness (Ra) effects. 
</p>
<p>Directional measurements are reported according to ASME B46.1 [207]. 
</p>
<p>The surface samples were imaged using a Hitachi S3000H SEM on the upskin 
</p>
<p>and downskin. Each sample was then sectioned along the center axis and mounted 
</p>
<p>and polished according to ASTM E3 using a Presi automatic polisher. The samples 
</p>
<p>were polished using 0.5 µm colloidal silica and optical images obtained. Samples 
</p>
<p>were then etched using etchant #13 (10% Oxalic Acid, Electrolytic) and optical 
</p>
<p>imaging completed again. All optical images were obtained using a Keyence VHX 
</p>
<p>digital microscope. Porosity, thickness, meltpool, and powder particle count 
</p>
<p>measurements were analyzed using Image J (Version 1.53e). Eight thickness 
</p>
<p>measurements were obtained normal to each wall (across) and averaged. The distance 
</p>
<p>between each melt pool (height), perpendicular to wall thickness, was also measured 
</p>
<p>for each wall and averaged. The low angle samples (&lt;10°) included approximately 
</p>
<p>12 melt pool measurements and angles &gt;10° were measured with a minimum of eight 
</p>
<p>melt pools. A 95% confidence interval was calculated and reported for these 
</p>
<p>measurements. Particle count was determined by examining the number of particles 
</p>
<p>in two 1 mm2 areas and averaging. This was obtained from the SEM image at 50x.   
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.4 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
</p>
<p>The measurements were obtained in a sequential order per sample: texture, SEM, 
</p>
<p>optical images. However, they are presented here per type of measurement with the 
</p>
<p>aim of providing a comprehensive understanding of the surface texture and associated 
</p>
<p>geometry. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—74 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.4.1 SEM Image Analysis 
</p>
<p>SEM images are shown in Figure 5. Visually, the adhered powder drastically 
</p>
<p>reduces on the downskin with increasing angle. The opposite is observed with the 
</p>
<p>upskin whereby more powder adheres to the surface. As the angle of incidence 
</p>
<p>increases relative to the deposition nozzle, the powder can bond more directly to the 
</p>
<p>trailing and edges of the melt pool for the upskin. The downskin surface is more 
</p>
<p>protected as the angle increases. An illustration of the vertical and angled powder 
</p>
<p>impingement in the samples is shown in Figure 6. Despite the enclosed racetrack 
</p>
<p>samples, particle impacts are still possible. This is evident from the observations of 
</p>
<p>ricochets and the recirculation of powder, as previously put into evidence [251].  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.4. SEM images of the normal surface for upskin and downskin 
</p>
<p>surfaces (additional images provided in supplemental materials). Scale is the 
</p>
<p>same for all images. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.5. Example of powder impingement for vertical and angled sample. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—75 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Based on the SEM images, the particle count (per mm2) was determined at two 
</p>
<p>locations on the surface and averaged. These data sets are shown in Table 4. The 
</p>
<p>particle count analysis confirms the increase in adherence for the upskin and reduced 
</p>
<p>powder for the downskin, although no inference should be made from this 
</p>
<p>observation related to surface texture without proper filtering for roughness. The 
</p>
<p>increase in angle facilitates a longer particle resonance time due to flow stagnation 
</p>
<p>from the impingement of the deposition nozzle (Figure 6). Additionally, the smaller 
</p>
<p>diameter melt pool (&lt; 1 mm) decreases the powder capture efficiency, leaving 
</p>
<p>approximately 80% of the powder available to adhere to the surface of the solidified 
</p>
<p>material [120,258]. A depiction of the smaller melt pool (1 mm) is shown in the 
</p>
<p>supplemental materials. Due to gravity, the powder will move down the surface of 
</p>
<p>the angled wall and bond to the melt pool as it is cooling as indicated by the increased 
</p>
<p>particle count.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>

</p>
<p>4.4.2 Optical Image Analysis 
</p>
<p>From optical images of the sectioned samples several sets of data were collected. 
</p>
<p>The porosity was measured for each sample in the polished unetched condition (non-
</p>
<p>heat treated) with the highest porosity being 0.14%, as shown in Table 4. This is 
</p>
<p>consistent with prior results on vertical 1 mm thick NASA HR-1 specimens [251] and 
</p>
<p>no trends are apparent based on the angle of the sample. Density was also obtained 
</p>
<p>using the Archimedes method and is provided in the supplemental materials; there 
</p>
<p>were no correlations observed on samples based on the wall angle. The wall thickness 
</p>
<p>was measured for each sample normal to the wall and results provided in Table 4. 
</p>
<p>Figure 7 shows that, as the angle increases, there is a corresponding increase in the 
</p>
<p>normal wall thickness. The wall thickness linearly increases of 7% from 0° to 25° 
</p>
<p>and then increases exponentially from 25° to 45°. The optical images indicate a 
</p>
<p>transition from partially melted and adhered powder at lower angles to a more random </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—76 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>surface profile as the angles increase, more so for the downskin surface (Figure 8). 
</p>
<p>The downskin surface at the high angles shows instances of subsurface unmelted 
</p>
<p>powder. The partially melted and adhered powder is observed regularly on the upskin 
</p>
<p>surface with more instances at the higher angles.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.6. Wall thickness and melt pool visualized on angled wall samples. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—77 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.7. Partially melted and adhered powder shown for the upskin surface 
</p>
<p>and chaotic texturing and subsurface powder shown for the downskin of the 
</p>
<p>45° wall sample. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The increased wall thickness is due to the material droop on the downskin side 
</p>
<p>and excess material deposited on the upskin side. The visualized melt pool can be 
</p>
<p>observed in Figure 7 along the centerline of the sample. The material droop is made 
</p>
<p>evident by the color change of etched samples and below the nominal melt pool. The 
</p>
<p>Marangoni forces moves the material outwards in the melt pool and this material is 
</p>
<p>solidified at the edges causing this chaotic texturing [192,229]. Unmelted subsurface </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—78 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>powder is also observed below the chaotic texturing (Figure 8). Above 30°, gravity 
</p>
<p>has an impact, breaking the surface tension and causing the fluid metal to droop below 
</p>
<p>the nominal melt pool and then solidify. This droop is distinctively different from the 
</p>
<p>dross phenomenon in L-PBF [248] since the melt pool terminates prior to the 
</p>
<p>downskin edge of the specimens. The excess material above the nominal melt pool 
</p>
<p>increases due to the direct impingement of the powder and inert gas and causes the 
</p>
<p>remelted and deposited material to flow upwards, in addition to outwards [259]. 
</p>
<p>It was observed from the experimental dataset, that the wall thickness could be 
</p>
<p>normalized when divided by the cosine (α) function, where α is the angle of the 
</p>
<p>deposited wall. The adjusted wall thickness has been demonstrated to exhibit a high 
</p>
<p>level of consistency, which is corroborated by the measured thickness data (Figure 
</p>
<p>9). Based on the observation that the wall thickness can be adjusted with the angle, 
</p>
<p>this concept was applied to predict the wall thickness based on deposition parameters. 
</p>
<p>Mazzarisi et al. reviewed and evaluated multiple models to predict wall thickness for 
</p>
<p>single-beads based on laser power (P) measured in watts (W), powder feed rate (F) 
</p>
<p>measured in grams/min, and traverse scan speed (V) measured in mm/min [192]. The 
</p>
<p>validation testing has demonstrated a reasonable correlation with the proposed 
</p>
<p>models. Kalami et al. discuss the relationship between the build layer height and its 
</p>
<p>variation based on the angle. However, they do not specifically explore the 
</p>
<p>relationship between the build layer height and the resulting wall thickness. [97]. 
</p>
<p>With the help of this dataset, these approaches were modified resulting in an equation 
</p>
<p>to predict the wall thickness (Wt) measured in mm, which reads 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>𝑊𝑡 =
</p>
<p>𝑃
𝑉𝐹𝑛 𝐾′
</p>
<p>cos ∝
                          
</p>
<p>4.1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where, Wt = thickness in mm; n = Exponent (0.005); K’ = Proportionality 
</p>
<p>constant (2.35); and α = angle of wall in degrees. The exponent and constant were 
</p>
<p>fitted using the experimental data. The model shows a good correlation with the 
</p>
<p>measured thickness and peak error of 3.5% for the current set of NASA HR-1 
</p>
<p>samples.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—79 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.8. Measured and adjusted wall thickness of the angled samples. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The melt pool can be distinguished in the etched optical images and was 
</p>
<p>measured perpendicular to the wall and along the centerline. The vertical distance 
</p>
<p>between the visible melt pools was obtained and averaged. Based on thickness of the 
</p>
<p>sample at 0° and prior research of the NASA HR-1 melt pool with identical 
</p>
<p>parameters [251], a size can be assumed and overlayed for each sample in Figure 7. 
</p>
<p>From this, the number of layers penetrated with subsequent deposition layers can be 
</p>
<p>determined. An average of four deposited layers were subjected to remelting with 
</p>
<p>each subsequent pass when the angles were less than 25°. When the angles exceeded 
</p>
<p>25°, three layers were remelted. The perpendicular melt pool height is shown in 
</p>
<p>Figure 10. The melt pool height was adjusted using the cosine function and shown in 
</p>
<p>the graph. The measurements indicate that as the distance of the melt pool is increased 
</p>
<p>when measured vertically. However, the adjusted step over used to create the desired 
</p>
<p>angle, along with the fixed Z-height of 0.254 mm, demonstrates highly repeatable 
</p>
<p>motion control, a stable melt pool, and a satisfactory solidification process. The 
</p>
<p>adjusted angle wall thickness (Figure 9) also indicated a consistent melt pool. These 
</p>
<p>findings establish the melt pool's robust stability, ruling out arbitrary material 
</p>
<p>deposition related to height or thickness. The adjusted data sets confirm that the 
</p>
<p>downskin droop and upskin excess material are not related to an unstable melt pool, 
</p>
<p>but rather to gravity effects.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—80 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.9. Perpendicular melt pool height and adjusted height of the angled 
</p>
<p>samples. 
</p>
<p>4.4.3 Surface Texture Characterization 
</p>
<p>4.4.3.1 Surface Texture with Primary Filter 
</p>
<p>The initial areal surface texture was measured and evaluated using a form 
</p>
<p>correction factor only. This allowed to analyze the full profile in terms of waviness 
</p>
<p>and roughness. The Sa is shown in Figure 11a: the inner and outer surfaces of the 
</p>
<p>vertical wall (0°) feature a similar average surface texture. The average areal surface 
</p>
<p>texture of the inner surface is slightly higher than the external surface consistent with 
</p>
<p>prior measurements [251]. This is due to the enclosed racetrack walls allowing for 
</p>
<p>powder rebounds and recirculation causing excess adherence. A minor change in the 
</p>
<p>wall inclination of 5° results in a reduced amount of powder adhering to the downskin 
</p>
<p>(inner surface) as it angles away from the deposition nozzle. The average texture of 
</p>
<p>the outer surface increases with the direct impact of the powder from the deposition 
</p>
<p>nozzle. The upskin texture is driven by roughness from the powder but also by 
</p>
<p>variations in the solidification of the melt pool, and it continues to increase linearly 
</p>
<p>up to an angle of 35°, stays near level and then a slight drop occurs at 45°. The 
</p>
<p>downskin texture shows no appreciable differences up to an angle of 30°, while for </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—81 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>larger angles it increases exponentially. This indicates that particle adhesion alone 
</p>
<p>cannot be used to determine texture and that other mechanisms cause this drastic 
</p>
<p>change. The observed downskin texture varies with the angle and aligns with the 
</p>
<p>thickness data and occurrence of material droop.  
</p>
<p> The maximum height of the surface (Sz) in Figure 11b shows that the upskin 
</p>
<p>surface texture decreases with increasing angles. Greater powder adherence is 
</p>
<p>observed, but several particles have the capability to fill in the valleys, thereby 
</p>
<p>contributing to a decrease in the observed effect. This is corroborated by the 
</p>
<p>observation that the maximum peak height (Sp) decreases, while the maximum valley 
</p>
<p>depth (Sv) remains constant. The downskin suggests a general increase of maximum 
</p>
<p>height, where peaks dominate the surface texture. In order characterize this attribute, 
</p>
<p>the skewness and kurtosis parameters were evaluated and are shown in Figure 11c 
</p>
<p>and Figure 11d, respectively. The skewness (Figure 11c) is plotted with the normal 
</p>
<p>wall thickness to identify abnormalities related to symmetry. The plot therefore 
</p>
<p>reveals that the upskin is symmetric about the plane, whereby peaks and valleys are 
</p>
<p>randomly distributed. Up to an angle of 30°, the inner surface is symmetric. Beyond 
</p>
<p>this angle, a predominance of peaks becomes evident, which is coherent with the 
</p>
<p>drastic changes observed in the wall thickness. The powder count analysis 
</p>
<p>demonstrates that powder is less adherent in this case, therefore the asymmetrical 
</p>
<p>peaks are governed by the droop of the material.  
</p>
<p> Figure 11d allows to evaluate the kurtosis for excessively high peaks or 
</p>
<p>valleys. A similar trend is observed in the Sz data of the upskin surface, which 
</p>
<p>demonstrates a general decrease in the number of peaks as the angle increases. The 
</p>
<p>downskin surface shows again a general increase of excessively high peaks with the 
</p>
<p>increase in angle. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—82 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.10. Primary texture of areal surface: a) Sa, average surface texture; 
</p>
<p>b) Sz, Sp, Sv, peak and valley height of surface; c) Ssk, skewness; and d) Sku, 
</p>
<p>Kurtosis. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.4.3.2 Areal Roughness and Particle Count 
</p>
<p>The surface texture and filtered roughness are further examined in relation to the 
</p>
<p>adherence of the powder to determine the factors contributing to the formation of the 
</p>
<p>observed texture. Using no profile filter (i.e., primary), the texture of the upskin 
</p>
<p>linearly increases with increasing particle count (Figure 12a). This could indicate that 
</p>
<p>powder adherence is dominant for the upskin roughness but cannot be determined 
</p>
<p>conclusively without proper filtering. Figure 12a also shows that the texture of the 
</p>
<p>downskin surface initially decreases with reduced particle count. Starting at 30°, the 
</p>
<p>surface texture linearly increases up to 40° and at 45° rises even more steeply. This 
</p>
<p>indicates that the texture of the downskin surface is related to the powder adherence 
</p>
<p>based on particle count.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—83 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The roughness filter, see section 2.4, was applied to the areal surface and plotted 
</p>
<p>as a function of the particle count in Figure 12b. The roughness (less than 0.8 mm 
</p>
<p>wavelength) shows a clear trend for the downskin surface: the average roughness 
</p>
<p>decreases with decreasing powder adherence, and this is also evident in the SEM 
</p>
<p>images. The upskin surface exhibits a similar trend but only up to 20°. Beyond 20° 
</p>
<p>the average roughness is constant, and it is much lower at 45°. Above 20°, the 
</p>
<p>particles continue to bond to the trailing edge of the melt pool as it cools but valley 
</p>
<p>voids are filled. The optical images reveal that the texture extends to a depth of two 
</p>
<p>powder particles, indicating that the excess particles are effectively filling in the 
</p>
<p>valleys of the surface. The core roughness (Sk), whereby the predominant peaks and 
</p>
<p>valleys were removed [256], was also evaluated, and its trend is identical to that of 
</p>
<p>the Sa with the roughness filter. The Sk values ranges from 38 ±0.2 µm to 51 ±0.2 
</p>
<p>µm for the downskin surfaces and from 50 to 58 ±0.2 um for the upskin surfaces. The 
</p>
<p>filtered Sdr was also assessed, because it determines the overall contribution to the 
</p>
<p>texture amplitude (shown in supplementary materials). The trends are identical to 
</p>
<p>those obtained for the Sa and the Sk: the roughness is proportional to the particle 
</p>
<p>count for the downskin surfaces and it reaches a maximum at 20° for the upskin 
</p>
<p>surfaces.  
</p>
<p>This roughness comparison displayed in Figure 12 put into evidence the 
</p>
<p>significance of using appropriate filtering techniques. Significantly different 
</p>
<p>conclusions could be drawn about the mechanism for the texture without properly 
</p>
<p>evaluating all filtering options [260].   
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.11. Roughness compared to particle count from SEM. a) Primary 
</p>
<p>filter and, b) Roughness filter. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.4.3.3 Vertical Directional Roughness and Waviness 
</p>
<p>To better characterize roughness (Ra) and waviness (Wa) in the vertical build 
</p>
<p>direction, the data set was filtered as discussed in section 2.4 and a comparison is 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 13a. The directional average roughness for the upskin is higher than </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—84 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>that of the downskin and driven by the direct particle impact with the melt pool. In 
</p>
<p>contrast to the Sa values (Figure 12), the upskin Ra does not indicate a reliance upon 
</p>
<p>the angle and roughness values are similar at all angles. The upskin Wa does increase 
</p>
<p>at angles above 30° due to chaotic disturbances in the melt pool creating the texture. 
</p>
<p>It can be hypothesized that the increased melt pool disturbance can reduce the 
</p>
<p>presence of partially assimilated surface particles [204]. This is observed in the 
</p>
<p>maximum height roughness (Rz) in Figure 13b, which is driven by the particle count 
</p>
<p>above 25°, and also the waviness portion of the surface texture.  
</p>
<p> The downskin surface indicates similar directional roughness and waviness 
</p>
<p>at angles 0° to 30°. A linear increase in waviness and decrease of roughness is 
</p>
<p>observed starting at 35°. Literature often suggests that the stair-stepping effect due to 
</p>
<p>the build layers creates periodic waviness [116,239], but this is not observed in these 
</p>
<p>samples with a layer thickness of 254 µm. The waviness is driven by chaotic 
</p>
<p>irregularities from the melt pool on the surface due to the gravity slump, or material 
</p>
<p>droop. The directional Ra was also compared with the particle count and showed 
</p>
<p>identical trends to the areal Sa. The maximum height roughness (Rz) is depicted in 
</p>
<p>Figure 13b and shows similar trends to Ra with the peak of 114 µm, which is the 
</p>
<p>upper limit of the PSD for the powder. The Rz peak height of the upskin surface is 
</p>
<p>constant up to the higher values of the angles, where the powder fills in valley voids 
</p>
<p>and thus Rz decreases.  
</p>
<p>Prior literature documented that Rz can be approximated by the upper PSD of 
</p>
<p>the powder for L-PBF [147,261]. The experimental data suggest that, for LP-DED, 
</p>
<p>the Rz peak can be approximated by using the maximum PSD. In the downskin 
</p>
<p>surface, there is a decrease in Rz where the surface is shielded from the direct 
</p>
<p>impingement of larger particles. However, the rebounds and recirculation of particles 
</p>
<p>are driven by the smaller and medium-sized particles (ranging from 55 to 95 µm) due 
</p>
<p>to their mass and velocity differences. The 115 µm diameter particle has 83% larger 
</p>
<p>mass than the 85 µm particle, where the difference between the 55 and 115 µm is 
</p>
<p>337%. The velocity in the powder injection has been shown to vary based on mass 
</p>
<p>and surface area of particles as well as nozzle focal plane, thus the likelihood of 
</p>
<p>rebounds and recirculation is higher with the smaller particles. [220,262]. SEM 
</p>
<p>images confirmed that the size of the particles adhered to the downskin 40° and 45° 
</p>
<p>walls is less than 90 µm. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—85 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.12. a) Directional vertical roughness and waviness. The shaded blue 
</p>
<p>region indicates the downskin surface and shaded red the upskin surface. b) 
</p>
<p>Maximum height (Rz) and particle count. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The relationship between waviness and wall thickness is shown in Figure 14. 
</p>
<p>The waviness is higher on the upskin for most of the angles, although differences are 
</p>
<p>minor at angles below 30°. There is A clear trend of waviness increasing with the 
</p>
<p>wall thickness can be observed and it is due to the surface irregularities from material 
</p>
<p>droop on the downskin. The upskin waviness is driven by the excess material 
</p>
<p>deposition above the nominal melt pool. This results from direct impingement of the 
</p>
<p>particles and remelted material flowing upwards and outward breaking the surface 
</p>
<p>tension [263,264]. This phenomenon has been evaluated in single bead LP-DED 
</p>
<p>walls [265], but this study has demonstrated that it is amplified in the angled walls. 
</p>
<p>Prior studies concluded that waviness was caused by the layer thickness [190,233]. 
</p>
<p>This work has proven that the waviness is an order of magnitude lower that Lu et al 
</p>
<p>and Shim et al and uncorrelated to the layer thickness.   
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—86 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 4.13. Directional waviness and normal wall thickness. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4.5 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>It was shown that the LP-DED process can produce low porosity (&lt;0.14%) 
</p>
<p>angled walls with target thickness of 1 mm and with an inclination angle of up to 45° 
</p>
<p>using the NASA HR-1 (Fe-Ni-Cr) alloy. The geometric aspects of the samples 
</p>
<p>including wall thickness, melt pool, and surface texture were evaluated. The surface 
</p>
<p>texture was filtered to properly segment the waviness and roughness parameters to 
</p>
<p>determine their contribution and the causing process mechanisms. From the 
</p>
<p>experimental observations, optical micrographs, SEM images, and optical surface 
</p>
<p>texture measurements, the following conclusions are drawn: 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• Walls featuring angles of up to 30° are stable, but angles above that 
</p>
<p>threshold pose higher risks of a build failure. In such cases, it may be 
</p>
<p>necessary to incorporate adjacent wall stiffening to ensure structural 
</p>
<p>integrity. 
</p>
<p>• The primary areal surface texture (Sa) exhibits an increase with the rise in 
</p>
<p>wall angle, which can be attributed to surface irregularities arising from the 
</p>
<p>melt pool and material droop. Peaks are dominant for the downskin surface 
</p>
<p>with the increasing angle and reduced for the upskin. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4—87 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• The waviness and subsequent wall thickness increases with the increased 
</p>
<p>wall angle due to the chaotic surface irregularities from material droop on 
</p>
<p>the downskin. The upskin waviness is driven by the excess material 
</p>
<p>deposition above the nominal melt pool. This is due to direct impingement 
</p>
<p>of the particles and remelted material flowing upwards and outwards.  
</p>
<p>• Proper filtering of the texture measurements is required to segregate 
</p>
<p>roughness and waviness. Differing conclusions could be drawn about the 
</p>
<p>mechanisms leading to texture if proper filtering is not considered. The λc 
</p>
<p>of 0.8 mm was an appropriate cut-off filter to extract directional roughness 
</p>
<p>and waviness for these thin-wall samples.  
</p>
<p>• As the angle increases, the filtered average areal and directional surface 
</p>
<p>roughness (Sa, Ra) of the downskin surface significantly decreases. This 
</p>
<p>reduction can be attributed to a decrease in the number of adhered particles 
</p>
<p>on the surface. This trend contradicts the observations documented in the 
</p>
<p>literature regarding surfaces manufactured with the L-PBF process. 
</p>
<p>• The upskin surface exhibits an increase in average areal (Sa) with an 
</p>
<p>increase in particle count. The roughness crests at 20° and remains constant 
</p>
<p>until the adhered powder filled in voids, resulting in a reduction in roughness 
</p>
<p>at higher build angles. 
</p>
<p>• The wall thickness increases exponentially with the angle, reaching an 
</p>
<p>inflection point at 30° where the thickness starts to significantly increase due 
</p>
<p>to melt pool droop. A correlation for the estimation of the wall thickness 
</p>
<p>was developed using the data related to the samples made of NASA HR-1 
</p>
<p>alloy.  
</p>
<p>• The results obtained from single-pass angled walls indicate that, for LP-
</p>
<p>DED, the directional maximum profile height (Rz) can be estimated using 
</p>
<p>the maximum particle size distribution. Only particles with size less than 85 
</p>
<p>µm adhere to the downskin surface at angles above 35° due to higher 
</p>
<p>rebounds and recirculation. 
</p>
<p>• The stair stepping typically observed in the LP-DED was not detected with 
</p>
<p>a layer height of 0.254 mm and remelting of 4-5 previously deposited layers.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The complex geometry of a part being fabricated inherently alters surface texture 
</p>
<p>due to varied angles. This change is influenced by the build parameters and resulting 
</p>
<p>melt pool. Thickness variation and powder adherence for upskin and downskin follow 
</p>
<p>comparable trends based on wall angle, driven by process mechanisms. Surface 
</p>
<p>texture, encompassing roughness and waviness, varies with alloy, parameters, and 
</p>
<p>resulting LP-DED melt pool. Designers must adhere to geometry guidelines and 
</p>
<p>characterize surface texture, which can impact fatigue life, corrosion, heat transfer, 
</p>
<p>and fluid flow performance. Previous views on LP-DED mandated post-machining, 
</p>
<p>but experiments show properly designed LP-DED can approach L-PBF surface 
</p>
<p>finishes. LP-DED manufacturing holds potential for aerospace, power generation, 
</p>
<p>and industrial heat exchangers. 
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—88 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>5 Development and 
</p>
<p>Characterization of 
</p>
<p>Microchannels 
 
</p>
<p>This chapter was partially published in:  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Gradl, P., Cervone, A., Colonna, P., 2022. Integral Channel Nozzles and Heat 
</p>
<p>Exchangers using Additive Manufacturing Directed Energy Deposition NASA HR-1 
</p>
<p>Alloy. 73rd International Astronautical Congress. Paris, France, IAC-
</p>
<p>22,C4,2,x73690. 
</p>
<p>5.1 ABSTRACT 
</p>
<p>This chapter focuses on the process development and characterization of 
</p>
<p>microchannels made from the NASA HR-1 alloy. In Chapters 3 and 4, samples with 
</p>
<p>1 mm walls were produced and characterized, establishing a baseline for the 
</p>
<p>microchannel fabrication. Microchannels of varying internal width/height 
</p>
<p>dimensions (12.7, 10.16, 7.62, 5.08, and 2.54 mm) were successfully deposited down 
</p>
<p>to 2.54 mm without powder entrapment, and 1 mm wall thickness was achieved with 
</p>
<p>high reproducibility. Optical microscopy, scanning electron microscopy, and optical 
</p>
<p>surface texture measurements were employed to destructively section and 
</p>
<p>characterize these channels. The analysis included determining cross-sectional area, 
</p>
<p>surface texture, wall and rib thickness, and the repeatability of these measurements. 
</p>
<p>The study revealed that the areal surface roughness is significantly influenced by the 
</p>
<p>size of the powder feedstock used in microchannel deposition, consistent with 
</p>
<p>Chapter 3 results. While the as-built cross-sectional area exhibited high repeatability, 
</p>
<p>the area was consistently smaller than the as-designed nominal area due to powder 
</p>
<p>adherence and melt pool undulations, as discussed in Chapter 4.  
</p>
<p>5.2 INTRODUCTION 
</p>
<p>For effective utilization of LP DED in heat exchanger manufacturing, it is 
</p>
<p>imperative to demonstrate and characterize various aspects of the geometry. Chapters 
</p>
<p>3 and 4 have discussed the fabrication and characterization of thin-wall geometry 
</p>
<p>samples, establishing a baseline for demonstrating microchannels. To illustrate 
</p>
<p>microchannels and, ultimately, heat exchangers, multiple geometric attributes must 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—89 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>undergo evaluation. These include angles, manufacturing tolerances, wall 
</p>
<p>thicknesses, internal features, and surface texture (refer to Figure 5.1). 
</p>
<p>Characterization of these various aspects is critical for using LP-DED for heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers manufacturing as the heat transfer and associated pressure drop can be 
</p>
<p>tuned to meet the optimal design requirements. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.1. Aspects affecting the LP-DED process that need to be studied to 
</p>
<p>characterize overall geometry limitations inherent in the manufacturing 
</p>
<p>technique. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>While the main emphasis of this dissertation is on microchannel geometry, it 
</p>
<p>recognizes that achieving the final quality of a component involves characterizing its 
</p>
<p>microstructure and resulting mechanical and thermophysical properties. The entirety 
</p>
<p>of the Design for Additive Manufacturing (DfAM) lifecycle is defined by factors such 
</p>
<p>as process inputs and parameters, geometry limitations, microstructure, and material 
</p>
<p>properties, as well as considerations for post-processing, part validation, and 
</p>
<p>application. The application of DfAM best practices is crucial for ensuring successful 
</p>
<p>builds and commissioning parts into service [62]. The LP-DED process was 
</p>
<p>originally designed for applications unrelated to microchannel heat exchangers. 
</p>
<p>Consequently, developmental lessons needed to be gleaned to adapt this process for 
</p>
<p>integral microchannel heat exchangers.  
</p>
<p>5.3 CHANNEL PROCESS DEVELOPMENT AND 
</p>
<p>CHARACTERIZATION 
</p>
<p>5.3.1 Microchannel Deposition 
</p>
<p>The microchannel samples of NASA HR-1 were deposited using an RPM 
</p>
<p>Innovations (RPMI) 557 LP-DED machine equipped with an argon-inert build 
</p>
<p>chamber. This system is identical to the one discussed in Chapters 3 and 4, featuring 
</p>
<p>an infrared (IR) continuous wave Gaussian profile 3 kW IPG laser, a deposition head 
</p>
<p>with three coaxial nozzles, 5-axis motion control, and a disc powder feeder with </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—90 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>agitation capabilities. Deposition parameters remained consistent with those detailed 
</p>
<p>in Table 4. The powder was rotary atomized by Homogenized Metals Inc. (HMI). 
</p>
<p>The coarse powder had a particle size distribution (PSD) of 55-105 µm and mean 
</p>
<p>PSD of 72 µm as measured using a Microtrac (Ver 11.1.0.6). The fine powder has a 
</p>
<p>PSD of 10-45 µm and chemistry shown in Table 6. The chemistry was measured 
</p>
<p>using Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP) at HMI. The chemical composition of coarse 
</p>
<p>is also provided in Table 6 . 
</p>
<p>The coarse powder exhibited an oxygen content of 75 ppm and a nitrogen content 
</p>
<p>of 6 ppm, while the fine powder had an oxygen content of 143 ppm and a nitrogen 
</p>
<p>content of 15 ppm. It is typical that finer powder has elevated oxygen content due to 
</p>
<p>the increased surface area [266]. It is important to note that all powder chemistry 
</p>
<p>adhered to the specifications for the NASA HR-1 alloy [201,205]. Virgin powder was 
</p>
<p>used for all builds. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 5.1. Chemical composition of NASA HR-1 alloy powder. 
</p>
<p>PSD / 
</p>
<p>Lot ID 
</p>

</p>

</p>
