Wt% Bal. 33.87 14.86 3.80 1.84 2.36 0.23 0.30 1.60 
</p>
<p>Fine,  
</p>
<p>HRA15 
Wt% Bal. 34.24 14.78 3.75 1.84 2.41 0.23 0.30 1.61 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>5.3.2 Channel Geometry 
</p>
<p>To evaluate the range of microchannel geometries that could be produced, the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED process was tested on various channel shapes. These included tube-like 
</p>
<p>structures, both round and oval, which are more representative of traditional tube-
</p>
<p>wall rocket nozzles (Figure 5.2). These types of channels offer an advantage in terms 
</p>
<p>of heat transfer as they help cool the rib or land regions [184]. More traditional square 
</p>
<p>and rectangular channels were also built. Finally, Hybrid D-shaped channels were 
</p>
<p>deposited allowing for increased cooling of the ribs and a smooth coldwall for easier 
</p>
<p>secondary processing or fabrication such as a composite overwrap structural jacket 
</p>
<p>[267]. All channels were built with a targeted 1 mm wall thickness using a single-
</p>
<p>bead deposition strategy as shown in Figure 5.3. The graphic shows the order of the 
</p>
<p>channels being built. This process starts with the inner wall (1) and outer wall (2) 
</p>
<p>being deposited first (indicated in yellow). This is followed by deposition of the 
</p>
<p>channel ribs in alternating directions as indicated by numbers 3 – 9 (orange and red) 
</p>
<p>in Figure 5.3. 
</p>
<p>Channels with widths as small as 1.4 mm were successfully demonstrated; 
</p>
<p>however, these narrower channels frequently experienced powder packing, making 
</p>
<p>post-processing difficult. The samples were deposited without openings at the 
</p>
<p>interface with the build plate, preventing free powder flow. Later samples, deposited 
</p>
<p>with a width of 2.54 mm and incorporating powder outlets at the build plate interface, 
</p>
<p>did not encounter issues with packed or trapped powder. This highlights the 
</p>
<p>importance of including openings in the channels to ensure powder flow. Various </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—91 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>channel geometries deposited using coarse powder (55–105 µm) are shown in Figure 
</p>
<p>5.2.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.2. Various types of small channels built with LP-DED 45 – 105 µm 
</p>
<p>powder. From top to bottom: Oval, D-shaped, Rectangular, Round (tube-like). 
</p>
<p>Wall thickness is approximately 1 mm. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.3. Tool path strategy and general order of operations to build single 
</p>
<p>wall microchannels. A) An example of a larger channel (12.7 mm). B) The 
</p>
<p>inner wall and outer walls are first deposited in a layer (yellow) followed by the 
</p>
<p>ribs of the channels as shown in the sequence (orange, red). 
</p>
<p>The design of heat exchangers made of channels obtained with this technique 
</p>
<p>requires the understanding of various manufacturing characteristics, including the 
</p>
<p>expected flow area and surface texture. Therefore, a study was conducted that focused 
</p>
<p>on square channels of different sizes to determine the repeatability of the process, the 
</p>
<p>differences from the design nominal, and the resulting surface texture. Five sets of 
</p>
<p>square channels were produced with internal widths of 12.7, 10.2, 7.62, 5.08, and </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—92 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>2.54 mm. These samples were all built in the vertical build orientation (representative 
</p>
<p>of the same build approach for a heat exchanger) and included powder outlets at the 
</p>
<p>bottom of the channels as shown in Figure 5.4. Two sets of samples for each channel 
</p>
<p>size were deposited, using coarse powder (45 – 105 µm) and fine powder (10 – 45 
</p>
<p>µm). The fine powder square channels of different sizes are shown in Figure 5.4 and 
</p>
<p>coarse powder channels using the same toolpaths are shown in Figure 5.5. The 
</p>
<p>samples were all deposited successfully and did not experience any packed or trapped 
</p>
<p>powder. The same machine toolpath was used for each of the coarse and fine powder 
</p>
<p>samples. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.4. Various sized channel samples shown with fine powder and powder 
</p>
<p>exits. Build direction shown with arrow. 
</p>
<p>5.3.3 Characterization Methods 
</p>
<p>The channel samples underwent sectioning for SEM, optical microscopy, and 
</p>
<p>surface texture analysis, as illustrated in Figure 5.5. These samples were sectioned 
</p>
<p>perpendicular to the build direction, approximately 6 mm from the top of the build. 
</p>
<p>Subsequently, they were mounted in epoxy and polished according to ASTM E3 
</p>
<p>using a Presi automatic polisher. The samples were polished using 0.5 µm colloidal 
</p>
<p>silica and optical images were acquired using a Keyence VHX digital microscope. 
</p>
<p>The images were analyzed using ImageJ to determine the area and perimeter of each 
</p>
<p>channel [268]. Additionally, extra samples were sectioned from the channels and cut </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—93 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>along the axis parallel to the build direction for SEM using a Hitachi S3000H and 
</p>
<p>surface texture imaging.  
</p>
<p>Surface texture measurements were obtained using a non-contact Keyence VR-
</p>
<p>5200 pattern light projection profilometer, equipped with three telecentric lenses 
</p>
<p>offering 80x magnification capability and a 20% overlap. Areal surface 
</p>
<p>measurements were obtained along with directional legacy measurements. All areal 
</p>
<p>surface measurements were reported in accordance with ISO 25178-2:2021 
</p>
<p>standards. In the case of areal surface measurements, the primary texture profile was 
</p>
<p>unfiltered and denoted as "primary," utilizing form correction only. Measurements 
</p>
<p>were taken both on the external surface and internally within the channels. All 
</p>
<p>characterization techniques are identical to those described in Chapter 3, 4, and 6.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.5. Sectioning plan for microchannel samples (coarse powder samples 
</p>
<p>shown). </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—94 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>5.4 MICROCHANNEL CROSS-SECTIONAL AREA 
</p>
<p>AND PERIMETER 
</p>
<p>In this study, the cross-sectional area and comparisons between channels of the 
</p>
<p>same size, derived from the same set of samples, were analyzed and demonstrated to 
</p>
<p>be highly reproducible (Figure 5.6). For each sample size (12.7, 10.16, 7.62, 5.08, 
</p>
<p>2.54 mm), three microchannels were measured. The results indicate an approximately 
</p>
<p>1% difference between channels of the same size. The only outlier in this data is the 
</p>
<p>2.54 mm fine powder channel, exhibiting a 2.7% difference between channels. Some 
</p>
<p>of this error can be attributed to a single (end) channel with additional stock added to 
</p>
<p>the outer radius, as later shown in Figure 5.11. While variations from the designed 
</p>
<p>nominal to as-built channels are noticeable, the reproducibility data underscores that, 
</p>
<p>with established parameters and feedstock, consistent microchannels can be reliably 
</p>
<p>produced. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.6. Reproducibility results of channel cross-sectional area for each of 
</p>
<p>the channel samples. 
</p>
<p>The difference of the measured area from the as-designed area is plotted in 
</p>
<p>Figure 5.7, which also shows the effect of manufacturing with a coarse and fine 
</p>
<p>powder PSD. It can be observed that the measured area is always smaller than the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—95 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>specified area and that the deviation generally increases as the channel size decreases. 
</p>
<p>However, as further explained, this reduction in area appears larger with the smaller 
</p>
<p>channels but is exacerbated due to the relative area of the surface texture in 
</p>
<p>comparison to the overall channel area. One cause of the resulting smaller dimensions 
</p>
<p>of the channels is the slight shrinkage occurring during the deposition process as the 
</p>
<p>samples cool. However, the main cause is the adhered powder and melt pool 
</p>
<p>undulations on the surface.   
</p>
<p>The channels fabricated with fine powder consistently show a larger area (less 
</p>
<p>difference from designed area) compared to identical channel samples fabricated with 
</p>
<p>coarse powder. However, there appears to be more variation in the fine powder 
</p>
<p>samples as the trends is not consistent with decreasing channel size.  The samples 
</p>
<p>produced with fine powder demonstrated more variation in texture and waviness 
</p>
<p>when produced with varying parameters in section 3.4.3. This may be attributing to 
</p>
<p>some of the differences observed in the 5.08 mm sample, but the exact reason is not 
</p>
<p>known.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.7. Difference of cross-sectional area (compared to as-designed area) 
</p>
<p>for each channel sample. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—96 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The observed variation in the cross-sectional area of the LP-DED channels, 
</p>
<p>attributed to powder adherence, melt pool undulations, and process shrinkage, affects 
</p>
<p>the ability to design precisely to a nominal area. The experimental data suggests that 
</p>
<p>an adjusted cross-section can be approximated by using 96% of the designed area to 
</p>
<p>account for these effects. This adjustment, which includes the 4% additional material 
</p>
<p>from these factors, is illustrated in Figure 5.8A for channels of varying widths. Figure 
</p>
<p>5.8B shows the absolute error for the adjusted areas, combined with data from the 
</p>
<p>square microchannel samples in Chapters 6 and 7. A smaller cross-sectional area is 
</p>
<p>observed with decreasing channel width, as surface texture contributes a larger 
</p>
<p>percentage of the overall area, consistent with the trend shown in Figure 5.7. The 
</p>
<p>absolute error across all channel samples is less than 5%.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.8. Adjusted cross-sectional channel areas. A) Measured and adjusted 
</p>
<p>areas for coarse and fine powder samples along with as-designed nominal area, 
</p>
<p>b) Absolute error from adjustments including data from microchannel samples 
</p>
<p>in Chapters 6 and 7. 
</p>
<p> The perimeter of each channel was measured on each sample and is depicted 
</p>
<p>in Figure 5.9. The as-designed channel perimeter assumes a flat surface, but the 
</p>
<p>perimeter of the as-built channels is up to 32% higher, which includes data from the 
</p>
<p>channels characterized in Chapters 6 and 7. This increased perimeter is consistently 
</p>
<p>attributed to the adhered powder and melt pool undulations causing the resulting 
</p>
<p>texture. The perimeter from each channel fabricated with fine powder exhibits less 
</p>
<p>error compared to the identical channels built with coarse powder. However, the 
</p>
<p>perimeter is still higher by more than 16% for each channel size. While the fine 
</p>
<p>powder channels consistently have a lower perimeter, the reduction is limited to 3% 
</p>
<p>for the smaller channels (5.08 and 2.54 mm). </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—97 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.9. A) Measured perimeter for coarse and fine powder samples 
</p>
<p>compared to the as-designed perimeter B) Differences between measured and 
</p>
<p>designed perimeters along with microchannel samples in Chapter 6 and 7. 
</p>
<p>5.5 SURFACE TEXTURE 
</p>
<p>Due to the importance of surface texture from the component and system 
</p>
<p>perspective, the surface texture of channels is critical for heat exchanger design. The 
</p>
<p>study in Chapter 3 evaluated the surface texture of the LP-DED process using 1 mm 
</p>
<p>thin-wall NASA HR-1 alloy oval racetracks while varying the build parameters [251]. 
</p>
<p>Using fine powder reduced the roughness by 23% compared to coarse powder. There 
</p>
<p>was also a discernible difference in surface texture between the inner surface 
</p>
<p>(trapping additional powder) and the outer (external) surface. This distinction was 
</p>
<p>due to the enclosed volume trapping excess powder that recirculated into the melt 
</p>
<p>pool as it solidified. It's important to note that while the samples in Chapters 4 and 5 
</p>
<p>were thin-walled, the spacing between walls was about 25 mm, where heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>designs with microchannels would be less than 10 mm.  
</p>
<p>The primary average areal surface texture, Sa, was measured for each sample on 
</p>
<p>both the inner (internal) and outer (external) surfaces. Sa was chosen to provide a 
</p>
<p>general comparison of these samples, as it is a commonly used parameter for 
</p>
<p>characterizing roughness in AM components due to the complexity of the surface 
</p>
<p>[227]. The Sa data is plotted for the coarse (45 – 105 µm) and fine (15 – 45 µm) 
</p>
<p>powders in Figure 5.10. The samples built with coarse powder consistently exhibit a 
</p>
<p>higher Sa compared to the fine powder samples for each channel size. This increased 
</p>
<p>texture is consistent with the data sets in Chapters 3 and 4, but to a higher degree 
</p>
<p>(45% compared to 23%). The surface roughness across the various sizes of channels 
</p>
<p>is roughly constant for the same powder size, except for the 2.54 mm channel. The 
</p>
<p>2.54 mm coarse powder channel shows an increase with respect to the other channels 
</p>
<p>that averages 17%, while the fine powder 2.54 mm channel increase averages 10%. 
</p>
<p>The difference between the inner and outer surfaces is minor but shows a slightly 
</p>
<p>higher average texture on the outer surface. For the fine powder, the average </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—98 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>difference is about 6% when comparing inner and outer surfaces. This finding is in 
</p>
<p>contrast to what was demonstrated in the oval racetracks shown in Chapter 3 [251]. 
</p>
<p>The channel samples in the current study have an outlet designed at the bottom of 
</p>
<p>each channel (Figure 5.5). This design allows the powder to fully exit and prevents 
</p>
<p>recirculation internally within the channel. The central gas used for inert purge of the 
</p>
<p>deposition and carrier gas for the powder travels through the internal channel at a 
</p>
<p>high velocity, allowing excess powder to flow through the channel instead of 
</p>
<p>stagnating, compared to the samples in Chapter 3  [251]. One observation during the 
</p>
<p>build process with the fine powder was that a portion of it remained suspended in the 
</p>
<p>machine atmosphere. This is likely the finer particle on the lower end of the PSD. 
</p>
<p>This phenomenon may introduce additional particles onto the outer surface, 
</p>
<p>influencing the overall surface roughness. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.10. Average surface texture (Sa) of various sized channel samples. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The difference in texture is also visually apparent from the cross-sectional 
</p>
<p>micrographs presented in Figure 5.11. The coarse powder samples exhibit visual 
</p>
<p>distinctions, particularly noticeable at lower magnifications for the 5.08- and 2.54-
</p>
<p>mm samples. The wall thickness is also observable in the cross sections, with slight 
</p>
<p>thinning as the center rib approaches the outer walls. Slightly raised areas on the outer 
</p>
<p>surfaces, where the ribs tie into the inner and outer walls during deposition, are more 
</p>
<p>pronounced on the fine powder samples. The raised region on the 2.54 mm channel 
</p>
<p>using fine powder averages 118 µm, whereas with coarse powder, it averages 35 µm. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—99 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>This raised region is not a significant concern for heat exchangers, given its parallel 
</p>
<p>orientation to the direction of fluid flow (assuming a vertical build orientation). 
</p>
<p>Additionally, this region may be removed or reduced during post-processing 
</p>
<p>operations. 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.11. Optical micrographs of selected channel samples showing fine and 
</p>
<p>coarse powder for each channel size. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> The SEM images are presented in Figure 5.12 for each internal channel 
</p>
<p>sample with coarse and fine powder. Each image is at the same magnification, and 
</p>
<p>the adhered particles per unit area are similar for each-sized sample built with the 
</p>
<p>coarse and fine powder, respectively. The differences in particle sizes are evident 
</p>
<p>between coarse and fine particles. The coarse particles are around the mean 
</p>
<p>distribution of the PSD, approximately 65–75 µm in diameter. Many of the fine 
</p>
<p>particles are biased toward the upper end of the PSD at 30–35 µm in diameter, but 
</p>
<p>several particles sized 10 µm and below are adhered to the channel surfaces. The </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—100 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>samples fabricated with fine powder also show some bands of powder, indicating 
</p>
<p>some macro waviness, while the coarse powder exhibits a more randomly distributed 
</p>
<p>pattern. 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.12. SEM Images of Internal Channels at the bottom of the channels. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—101 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>5.6 WALL THICKNESS 
</p>
<p>Wall thickness is an important characteristic in heat exchanger design as it 
</p>
<p>impacts heat transfer and the peak temperatures experienced by the walls during 
</p>
<p>operation. The wall thickness must be thin enough for proper cooling but provide 
</p>
<p>adequate thickness to maintain positive structural margins for strength and fatigue 
</p>
<p>life. The rib thickness between the channels is also significant as it influences the 
</p>
<p>cooling of the hotwall (for combustion chambers and nozzles) through conduction. 
</p>
<p>Consistently maintaining a wall thickness of 1.05 to 1.165 mm was achieved for both 
</p>
<p>the fine and coarse powder samples, which aligns with the findings of the prior study 
</p>
<p>in Chapter 3 [251]. Summary data sets that measured both the rib thickness and the 
</p>
<p>outer wall (i.e. simulated hotwall) thickness of the various width channel samples is 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 5.13. Three measurements were collected from each channel rib and 
</p>
<p>outer wall in the cross-sectioned sample and the data was averaged and standard 
</p>
<p>deviation provided in the graph. The measurements were taken in the areas where the 
</p>
<p>wall is constant without the thinning (as shown in Figure 5.11). 
</p>
<p>It is observed that the coarse powder samples are slightly thicker by about 2.6%, 
</p>
<p>driven by the larger particle size distribution (PSD) of the powder causing this 
</p>
<p>difference. There is also a difference observed between the outer wall thickness of 
</p>
<p>coarse and fine powder samples, approximately 3.8%. Another observation from this 
</p>
<p>study is the slight difference between the rib wall thickness and outer wall thickness, 
</p>
<p>averaging 2.5% and 3.7% for coarse and fine powder, respectively. This difference 
</p>
<p>in wall thickness is due to slightly different build parameters. The outer wall is 
</p>
<p>deposited at a constant travel speed, while the rib wall is deposited at a varying travel 
</p>
<p>speed to ensure that the intersection with the outer wall is not overbuilt (acceleration 
</p>
<p>and deceleration leading into the intersection). The thickness was expected to be 
</p>
<p>constant between channels since the build parameters do not change based on the 
</p>
<p>channel size. However, the differences in rib and outer wall thickness, as well as 
</p>
<p>differences between coarse and fine powder samples, are not statistically significant 
</p>
<p>based on a t-test comparison. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—102 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 5.13. Summary of rib and outer wall thickness for channel samples. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>5.7 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS  
</p>
<p>The results presented in this chapter built upon the lessons learned in Chapters 3 
</p>
<p>and 4 to produce heat exchanger channel geometries. Various types of channel 
</p>
<p>geometries were successfully deposited using the LP-DED process and the NASA 
</p>
<p>HR-1 alloy (Fe-Ni-Cr). These geometries included round, oval, square and D-shaped. 
</p>
<p>These channels provide new design opportunities for designers compared to round 
</p>
<p>tubes or square channels for traditionally manufactured tube-wall or channel wall 
</p>
<p>nozzles. Several square channel sizes were successfully deposited with highly 
</p>
<p>reproducible width down to 2.54 mm. Smaller width channels were also fabricated 
</p>
<p>successfully, but could result in higher risk for excess powder being trapped within. 
</p>
<p>The following conclusions can be drawn based on detailed characterization of various 
</p>
<p>square channels.  
</p>
<p>• Repeatable fabrication of microchannels is achievable using established 
</p>
<p>parameters and feedstock. Results indicate a 1% average difference between 
</p>
<p>channels of identical sizes, with a portion of this variation attributed to a 
</p>
<p>singular end channel where additional stock was introduced to the outer 
</p>
<p>radius. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 5—103 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• Cross-sectional channel areas, when measured, consistently deviate from the 
</p>
<p>designed areas, with greater disparities observed as channel size decreases. 
</p>
<p>The variation primarily arises from excess powder adhering to the internal 
</p>
<p>surface, contributing to surface texture, along with shrinkage from 
</p>
<p>deposition. An adjusted area, accounting for the build process's shrinkage, 
</p>
<p>additional texture from powder adherence and melt pool undulations, and 
</p>
<p>heat treatment-induced shrinkage, can be determined by taking 96% of the 
</p>
<p>as-designed area. 
</p>
<p>• The as-built perimeter of channels exhibits up to a 32% increase for coarse 
</p>
<p>powder and as low as 16% for fine powder, attributed to the partial melting 
</p>
<p>and adherence of powder particles. 
</p>
<p>• Coarse powder samples consistently have a higher measured average 
</p>
<p>surface texture (Sa) than fine powder samples across all channel sizes, 
</p>
<p>exhibiting a more pronounced increase compared to the study in Chapter 3 
</p>
<p>(45% vs. 23%). Specifically, the 2.54 mm coarse powder microchannel 
</p>
<p>shows an average increase of 17%, surpassing the fine powder channel's 
</p>
<p>average of 10%. 
</p>
<p>• The difference in surface texture between inner and outer surfaces is minor, 
</p>
<p>with the outer surface exhibiting a slightly higher average texture. 
</p>
<p>Specifically, for fine powder, the average difference is approximately 6% 
</p>
<p>when comparing inner and outer surfaces. This finding contrasts with the 
</p>
<p>observations in oval racetracks presented in Chapter 3. Notably, the channel 
</p>
<p>samples in the current study feature an outlet designed at the bottom of each 
</p>
<p>channel, facilitating the complete exit of powder and preventing internal 
</p>
<p>recirculation. 
</p>
<p>• Wall thickness, ranging from 1.05 to 1.165 mm, remains highly reproducible 
</p>
<p>with minimal variations observed between powder sizes, internal channel 
</p>
<p>rib, and outer wall. Although these differences are not statistically 
</p>
<p>significant, the data aligns with findings in Chapters 3 and 4, demonstrating 
</p>
<p>consistency in wall thickness even with the fabrication of these small 
</p>
<p>microchannels. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>While this study focused exclusively on square channels, it highlights crucial 
</p>
<p>aspects in the development of novel processes for constructing heat exchangers. 
</p>
<p>Future research opportunities lie in conducting additional studies to characterize 
</p>
<p>various channel geometries, including round, oval, and D-shape configurations. 
</p>
<p>Additional materials could also be evaluated to provide more design solutions for 
</p>
<p>various heat exchanger applications.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—104 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6 Post-process Surface 
</p>
<p>Enhancement and 
</p>
<p>Characterization 
 
</p>
<p>Major portions of this chapter were published in: 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Gradl, P., Cervone, A., Colonna, P., 2024. Development and experimental evaluation 
</p>
<p>of surface enhancement methods for laser powder directed energy deposition 
</p>
<p>microchannels for laser powder directed energy deposition microchannels, Virtual 
</p>
<p>Phys. Prototyp. 19:1. e2345389. https://doi.org/10.1080/17452759.2024.2345389. 
</p>
<p>6.1 ABSTRACT 
</p>
<p>This chapter focuses on the development and characterization of surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement methods for microchannels created via the LP-DED process. Various 
</p>
<p>surface finishing techniques, such as abrasive flow machining, chemical milling, 
</p>
<p>chemical-mechanical polishing, electrochemical machining, and thermal energy 
</p>
<p>method were applied to modify the internal surfaces of microchannels made of NASA 
</p>
<p>HR-1 Fe-Ni-Cr alloy. Flow testing to measure the discharge coefficient was 
</p>
<p>performed on microchannel samples whose internal surfaces were processed using 
</p>
<p>the mentioned techniques. After the testing, each sample was characterized using 
</p>
<p>optical microscopy, Scanning Electron Microscopy (SEM), and Computed 
</p>
<p>Tomography. Variations in the surfaces resulted from the powder adherence, melt 
</p>
<p>pool undulations, and polishing mechanisms. The results indicated that removing an 
</p>
<p>average of 70 µm of material, equivalent to the mean powder diameter, is essential to 
</p>
<p>reduce surface roughness and impact the discharge coefficient. Reduction of flow 
</p>
<p>resistance can be accomplished by planarizing both the roughness and waviness peak 
</p>
<p>height and density and a ratio is proposed. This research resulted in thorough surface 
</p>
<p>characterization in relation to hydraulic flow which enables tailored surface 
</p>
<p>adjustments for specific end-use applications. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.2 INTRODUCTION 
</p>
<p>This chapter is focused on the experimental characterization and test results of 
</p>
<p>surface enhancement methods applied to microchannels produced using the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6 
 </p>
<p></p>
<div class="annotation"><a href="https://doi.org/10.1080/17452759.2024.2345389">https://doi.org/10.1080/17452759.2024.2345389</a></div>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—105 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>process. These enhancements included abrasive flow machining, chemical milling, 
</p>
<p>chemical mechanical polishing, pulsed electrochemical machining, and the thermal 
</p>
<p>energy method. These processes were applied to modify the internal surfaces of LP-
</p>
<p>DED microchannels built using a hydrogen-resistant NASA HR-1 alloy. This 
</p>
<p>research addresses a notable knowledge gap, as microchannels created using LP-
</p>
<p>DED have limited data available [5], and research on the improvement of finishing 
</p>
<p>of their internal surfaces is limited. This work involved a comprehensive 
</p>
<p>characterization of the surface texture, using scanning electron microscopy (SEM), 
</p>
<p>optical microscopy, and computed tomography (CT). Hydraulic flow testing was 
</p>
<p>conducted to assess discharge coefficients and provide comparisons between each of 
</p>
<p>the surface enhancement processes. The discharge coefficient was used as a 
</p>
<p>quantitative measure to determine flow resistance in microchannels, considering 
</p>
<p>variations in surface texture. The research objective was to characterize the various 
</p>
<p>surface enhancement methods and their impact on flow resistance, focusing on 
</p>
<p>evaluation rather than optimization for a "smooth" surface. These various surface 
</p>
<p>conditions may, in turn, allow designers to fine-tune these surfaces to meet specific 
</p>
<p>requirements of end-use applications. This potential extends to leveraging additive 
</p>
<p>manufacturing to balance factors including heat transfer, fluid flow friction, 
</p>
<p>corrosion, mechanical fatigue life, and aesthetics. 
</p>
<p>6.3 METHODOLOGY 
</p>
<p>Several channel samples were built using the LP-DED process. Surface 
</p>
<p>enhancements were used to modify the internal perimeter of the channels. These 
</p>
<p>channels samples were flow tested to determine the discharge coefficients of each 
</p>
<p>channel. Following testing the channel samples were sectioned to characterize the 
</p>
<p>internal surface and flow area to anchor the test data.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.1 Fabrication of DED Samples and Processing 
</p>
<p>The channels were designed with a nominal width and height of 2.54 x 2.54 mm 
</p>
<p>square and built using LP-DED. Figure 1 illustrates the LP-DED build and 
</p>
<p>subsequent processing of the channel samples. The length of the square microchannel 
</p>
<p>section was approximately 150 mm. A 25 mm blended transition from the square 
</p>
<p>channel to a round extrusion was designed to allow welding of a universal AN-type 
</p>
<p>fitting as shown in Figure 1D. The channels were built as part of larger sample boxes 
</p>
<p>(Figure 1B) using the NASA HR-1 alloy and then individually sectioned (Figure 1C 
</p>
<p>and 1D). This Fe-Ni-Cr superalloy is resistant to hydrogen environment 
</p>
<p>embrittlement (HEE) in high pressure components [77]. It was developed for high 
</p>
<p>strength and high ductility in harsh hydrogen environments, like those occurring in 
</p>
<p>liquid rocket engine nozzles and heat exchangers. It is derived from the A-286 and 
</p>
<p>JBK-75 alloys [253,254]. The boxes were built with an RPM Innovations (RPMI) 
</p>
<p>557 LP-DED machine equipped with an argon-inert build chamber (Figure 6.1A). 
</p>
<p>The 557 system incorporated an infrared (IR) continuous-wave gaussian profile 3 kW 
</p>
<p>IPG laser, three coaxial nozzles within the deposition head, 5-axis motion control, </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—106 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>and disc powder feeder with agitation capabilities. The samples were built on top of 
</p>
<p>an A36 mild steel 12 mm thick base plate. The laser power was 350 W, with powder 
</p>
<p>feed rate of 23 grams/min, a travel speed of 763 mm/min and a layer height of 0.254 
</p>
<p>mm. Based on previous development work, this set of parameters was optimized to 
</p>
<p>produce crack-free material, resulting in very low porosity [251,269]. 
</p>
<p>The powder was rotary atomized by Homogenized Metals Inc. (HMI), with a 
</p>
<p>particle size distribution (PSD) of 55-105 µm meeting +140 mesh at 0% and -325 
</p>
<p>mesh at 3.8% per ASTM B214 using a Microtrac (Ver 11.1.0.6). The chemical 
</p>
<p>composition is listed in Table 2 (HMI powder lot HRA4). The Oxygen content was 
</p>
<p>61 ppm and the Nitrogen content was 7 ppm. The chemical composition was 
</p>
<p>measured using Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP). The peak powder size was 70 µm 
</p>
<p>and a Vega3 Tescan Scanning Electron Microscope (SEM) using Backscatter 
</p>
<p>Electron (BSE) showed mostly spherical particles with a few satellites and random 
</p>
<p>distribution of occasional oblong particles. Virgin powder was used for all builds. 
</p>
<p>The sample channel boxes were stress-relieved at 1066°C for 90 minutes with a 
</p>
<p>slow furnace cooling, prior to removal from the build plate with a bandsaw (Figure 
</p>
<p>6.1B). The boxes were then homogenized at 1163°C for 6 hours in vacuum and 
</p>
<p>Argon-quenched. A solution anneal was performed at 1066°C for 60 minutes, with 
</p>
<p>an Argon-quench followed by age-hardening at 690°C for 16 hours. The boxes were 
</p>
<p>then furnace cooled to 621°C and held for 16 hours (total aging time of 32 hours) 
</p>
<p>[252,270]. Individual channel samples were then sectioned from the boxes using a 
</p>
<p>water jet (Figure 6.1C). The samples were cleaned and an AS5174-04 stainless steel 
</p>
<p>fitting was laser welded to the inlet end of the samples (Figure 6.1D). The channels 
</p>
<p>were tagged according to the surface enhancement processes. Several samples were 
</p>
<p>used for process development and not tested.  The internal surfaces of the channels 
</p>
<p>were processed according to surface enhancements described in section 2.2 
</p>
<p>Following the polishing of the internal perimeter, a 0.75 mm diameter hole was 
</p>
<p>drilled at two locations and a 3.175 mm diameter tube was welded as shown in Figure 
</p>
<p>6.1E. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—107 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.1. Channel specimen fabrication: A) LP-DED of channel sample 
</p>
<p>boxes, B) complete NASA HR-1 box after stress relief and sectioning from the 
</p>
<p>build plate, C) Panels excised from boxes, D) Individual channel samples with 
</p>
<p>welded inlet port, and E) drilled and welded instrumentation ports. Build 
</p>
<p>Direction (BD) shown with arrow. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 6.1. Chemical composition of NASA HR-1 alloy powder (HMI Lot 
</p>
<p>HRA4). 
</p>
<p> Fe Ni Cr Co Mo Ti Al V W 
</p>
<p>Wt.% Bal. 33.71 14.49 3.75 1.82 2.31 0.24 0.3 1.59 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—108 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.2 Surface Enhancement Processes 
</p>
<p>Several internal surface enhancement processes were selected with the ultimate 
</p>
<p>goal of testing the possibilities of improving the flow friction characteristics of heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers channels. These included abrasive flow machining (AFM), chemical 
</p>
<p>mechanical polishing (CMP), chemical milling (CM), pulsed electrochemical 
</p>
<p>machining (PECM), and thermal energy method (TEM) or thermal deburring. These 
</p>
<p>enhancement processes were selected among the many available 
</p>
<p>[126,133,142,149,271],  based on the ability to modify internal microchannels with a 
</p>
<p>cross section of less than 2.54 x 2.54 mm [51,131,146,272]. The primary goal of this 
</p>
<p>research was not to minimize surface roughness or to allow for the best possible 
</p>
<p>surface polishing of the channels, but to analyze the varying levels of surface 
</p>
<p>polishing that can be obtained to gain a better understanding of how the polishing can 
</p>
<p>be tuned to the requirements in terms of flow resistance. 
</p>
<p>AFM uses a viscous fluid polymer that contains suspended abrasive particles. 
</p>
<p>The slurry is pressurized and pumped through the channels using a single or bi-
</p>
<p>directional flow [273,274], thus providing abrasion, and the removed particulate is 
</p>
<p>carried away with the media. This process is also referred to as extrude-honing or 
</p>
<p>slurry-honing. The slurry type, volume, time, and number of cycles were adjusted to 
</p>
<p>modify the channel surfaces. The pressure was 68.9 bar and the flow was 
</p>
<p>unidirectional and the flow rate ranged from 0.56 L/min to 4.47 L/min. Following the 
</p>
<p>AFM process, the slurry was removed with compressed air and rinsed with a cleaning 
</p>
<p>solution. The channels were then dried with compressed air, ultrasonically cleaned, 
</p>
<p>rinsed with water, and again dried.  
</p>
<p>CM, or chemical polishing, consists of flowing a chemical solution through the 
</p>
<p>channels that attacks the grain structure and dissolves the metal at the surface 
</p>
<p>[127,135]. The dissolved material is flushed out with the hydrofluoric solution. 
</p>
<p>Longer exposure times yield more material removal. The temperature of the solution 
</p>
<p>pumped through the channels varied from 52 to 60°C and the surfaces were exposed 
</p>
<p>to the solution for a period that ranges from 4 to 19 mins. The parts were rinsed 
</p>
<p>following the chemical milling. 
</p>
<p>CMP is a process in which chemical and mechanical forces are applied to remove 
</p>
<p>surface material along preferential directions thus reducing the peaks height [275]. 
</p>
<p>The chemical solution removes most of the material, while abrasive media planarizes 
</p>
<p>the surface. The chemical solution and micro-abrasives were flowed through the 
</p>
<p>channels and any removed particles flushed with the solution. The flow rate of the 
</p>
<p>chemical solution, temperature, time of flow, and weight of the microabrasives was 
</p>
<p>varied for each channel. The flow rate was varied from 0.4 to 2 L/min at temperatures 
</p>
<p>of 50 to 60°C and runs with and without microabrasives (up to 10%). The exposure 
</p>
<p>time was also varied between 10 to 20 mins.  
</p>
<p>PECM obtains the surface improvement by applying a local direct current (DC) 
</p>
<p>pulse using an electrode to dissolve metal [276]. The electrode was inserted inside of 
</p>
<p>the channel and dragged through, thus providing the proper offset gap. A limited 
</p>
<p>portion of the electrode was exposed. The channel was actively flushed with an 
</p>
<p>electrolytic sodium chloride solution.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—109 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>TEM is a process where parts are placed in a sealed chamber, which is 
</p>
<p>pressurized with a mixture of gaseous fuel and oxygen, and ignited [277,278]. The 
</p>
<p>high temperature of the combustion process vaporizes (thin) areas with low thermal 
</p>
<p>mass. The part was fixtured to prevent damage in the 25 cm diameter chamber 
</p>
<p>operating at a combustion pressure of 6 bar with a total processing time of 30 seconds. 
</p>
<p>The surface enhancement processes were outsourced to commercial vendors 
</p>
<p>actively working production. Prior to conducting the flow testing and 
</p>
<p>characterization, each vendor received development samples for trials. This step was 
</p>
<p>necessary because both material composition and geometry can influence the 
</p>
<p>parameters used. Although general parameters were outlined above in Section 2.2, 
</p>
<p>detailed parameters and specific setups for each of the channel samples were deemed 
</p>
<p>proprietary to the vendors. The objective of this research was to introduce variations 
</p>
<p>in the surfaces of the channels rather than achieving an ideally "smooth" surface. To 
</p>
<p>meet this objective, vendors employed distinct parameter sets for each channel to 
</p>
<p>generate the data for this study. The general setup of the surface enhancement 
</p>
<p>processes is illustrated in Figure 6.2. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—110 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.2. Overview of surface enhancement processes used for internal LP-
</p>
<p>DED microchannel samples. 
</p>
<p>6.3.3 Test Configuration and Procedures 
</p>
<p>Testing was conducted at the NASA Marshall Space Flight Center component 
</p>
<p>development facility. A 379-liter GN2 pressurized tank provided deionized (DI) water </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—111 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>to the system depicted in Figure 6.3. A 10 µm filter was placed immediately 
</p>
<p>downstream of the tank after the main valve. A Potter Aero RAA-1/2-301-1/2-5440A 
</p>
<p>flowmeter (3.4 to 36 l/min) was installed in a bypass leg of the system in addition to 
</p>
<p>a 1.143 mm diameter cavitating venturi (Cd = 0.980) to control the mass flow through 
</p>
<p>the channel test articles. The venturi cross-sectional area was sized to be smaller than 
</p>
<p>the smallest cross-section area of the test article. Pressure and temperature 
</p>
<p>measurements were installed at locations indicated on the diagram of Figure 6.3 and 
</p>
<p>on the channel test article. The test article was installed in a blast containment due to 
</p>
<p>the operating pressures. The Sensotech A-105 pressure transducers (0 to 40 bar, 
</p>
<p>±0.1% full scale), Omega E type thermocouples (-200 to 900°C ±1.7°C), flowmeter, 
</p>
<p>and cavitating venturi were all calibrated prior to testing. Instrumentation checks 
</p>
<p>were completed every day of testing.  
</p>
<p>Each channel test article was flushed with DI water at 7 bar for a minimum of 10 
</p>
<p>seconds prior to data collection. The tank was then pressurized at increments of 34, 
</p>
<p>69, 138 Bar and held for 10 seconds at each pressure. The selected pressures were 
</p>
<p>based on the maximum allowable tank pressure, whose value is of the same order of 
</p>
<p>magnitude of the pressure at which high-pressure heat exchangers are operated (e.g., 
</p>
<p>rocket nozzles, components of power and propulsion systems). A Dewetron data 
</p>
<p>acquisition system was used in conjunction with the LabVIEW software for system 
</p>
<p>control. Following pressurization of the test articles at the three set points, all pressure 
</p>
<p>in the system was vented and a new test article installed. A total of 20 channel samples 
</p>
<p>were tested. Each sample was uniquely numbered based on the processing (AB = As-
</p>
<p>built; AFM = abrasive flow machining; CM = chemical milling; CMP = chemical 
</p>
<p>mechanical polishing; PECM = pulsed electrochemical machining; TEM = thermal 
</p>
<p>energy method).     
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.3. Flow test configuration. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—112 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.4 Sectioning and Imaging 
</p>
<p>Following flow testing, the channel samples were sectioned to characterize the 
</p>
<p>channel geometry and surfaces using optical microscopy, optical imaging surface 
</p>
<p>texture, SEM imaging, and computed tomography.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.4.1 Optical Images 
</p>
<p>Each channel sample was cross sectioned at three axial locations perpendicular 
</p>
<p>to the flow (Figure 6.4). The samples were mounted and polished according to the 
</p>
<p>ASTM E3 standard using a Presi automatic polisher. The samples were polished 
</p>
<p>using 0.5 µm colloidal silica and optical images obtained. Samples were then etched 
</p>
<p>using etchant #13 (10% Oxalic Acid, Electrolytic) and optical imaging completed 
</p>
<p>again. All optical images were obtained using a Keyence VHX digital microscope. 
</p>
<p>The Image J image processing software [268] was used for analysis to obtain the 
</p>
<p>cross sectional area and the wetting perimeter. The porosity of each sample was also 
</p>
<p>measured, and the results are provided in the supplementary materials. The results 
</p>
<p>were averaged, and standard deviation reported from the three cross-sections. Each 
</p>
<p>image was also overlayed to compare the processed sample to an as-built sample 
</p>
<p>(identified as AB-2). These overlay images were used to measure the approximate 
</p>
<p>amount of material removed per side, according to the hypothesis that the removal 
</p>
<p>was uniform on all sides. The material removal was measured normal to the wall at 
</p>
<p>20 locations and data averaged. From the images, this hypothesis is only partially 
</p>
<p>verified, since some surface enhancement processes removed more material in the 
</p>
<p>center of the channel wall as opposed to the corners. The perimeter of each channel 
</p>
<p>was measured at the three axial locations and the average is reported.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.4. Sectioning of channels post-flow testing. An example scan of the 
</p>
<p>areal surface texture is provided along the length of the channel.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—113 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.4.2 SEM Imaging 
</p>
<p>The surface perpendicular to the flow was imaged using a Hitachi S3000H 
</p>
<p>Scanning Electron Microscope (SEM) (Figure 6.4). The particle count was 
</p>
<p>determined by examining the number of particles in two 1 mm2 areas and averaging. 
</p>
<p>This was obtained from the SEM image at 50x.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.4.3 Surface Texture Measurements 
</p>
<p>Surface texture measurements were obtained using a non-contact Keyence VR-
</p>
<p>5200 pattern light projection profilometer. The setup included three telecentric lenses 
</p>
<p>with 80x magnification capability and an overlap of 20%. An area approximately 38 
</p>
<p>mm long and 1.6 mm wide was measured for each sample. All areal surface 
</p>
<p>measurements are reported according to ISO 25178-2:2021 [255,257]. The surface 
</p>
<p>data intersecting the corners of the channels were eliminated using an end-effect 
</p>
<p>correction. A surface form correction was applied to remove any tilt and curvature, 
</p>
<p>addressing any residual stress distortion of the samples, and establishing a reference 
</p>
<p>plane. For the areal surface measurements, the primary texture profile was left 
</p>
<p>unfiltered (denoted as primary) using only the form correction. To extract roughness 
</p>
<p>and waviness from the areal surface, a λc spatial frequency cut-off filter of 0.8 mm 
</p>
<p>(i.e., a roughness filter) was applied following the ISO 21920-3 guidelines. To obtain 
</p>
<p>directional roughness and waviness (i.e., Ra and Wa, respectively), a virtual line was 
</p>
<p>created along the center of the channel, and data were extracted to report the results. 
</p>
<p>Various areal data sets such as average texture (Sa), maximum surface height (Sz), 
</p>
<p>core texture depth (Sk), reduced peak height (Spk), and roughness peak density (Spd) 
</p>
<p>were obtained for evaluation based on prior literature [269]. Directional data sets for 
</p>
<p>average roughness (Ra), average waviness (Wa), waviness peak height (Wp), and 
</p>
<p>waviness peak density (WPc) were also evaluated along the direction of flow.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.3.4.4 Computed Tomography Scanning 
</p>
<p>Micro-computed tomography (µCT) scanning was completed on seven samples 
</p>
<p>including AB-2, AFM-2, AFM-15, CM3-, CM-20, PECM-1, and CM-20. These 
</p>
<p>samples were selected to evaluate each of the surface enhancement processes and the 
</p>
<p>range of the calculated discharge coefficient. A fully intact 17 mm length portion of 
</p>
<p>the channel samples were CT-scanned as shown in Figure 6.4. The specimens were 
</p>
<p>scanned on a Zeiss Xradia Versa 620 using 159kV and 23W and exposure of 8.7 
</p>
<p>seconds. A variable exposure scan technique was employed to help reduce noise 
</p>
<p>while simultaneously reducing the overall scan duration. The imaging covered an 
</p>
<p>area of 19 mm2 per scan and the estimated pixel size is 10.03 µm. This resulted in a 
</p>
<p>feature resolution of ~30 µm, which is typically 3-5 times the pixel size [279].  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—114 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.4 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
</p>
<p>6.4.1 Image Analysis 
</p>
<p>6.4.1.1 Optical Images 
</p>
<p>The cross sections of each channel are documented in Figure 6.5. The channel 
</p>
<p>cross section is overlayed with the AB-2 channel colored in gray, while the removed 
</p>
<p>material is depicted in orange. The AB-2 channel sample was used as the baseline 
</p>
<p>since this is unprocessed. These micrographs were obtained at the first pressure port 
</p>
<p>on the test article. The images were used to determine the area, perimeter, and 
</p>
<p>approximate thickness of the removed material, per side. The as-built sample displays 
</p>
<p>pronounced texturing due to excessive powder adherence and disturbances of the 
</p>
<p>melt pool [269]. This texturing increases the wetting perimeter by 32% compared to 
</p>
<p>the as-designed (nominal) channel. Corners are created at the intersection of the melt 
</p>
<p>pool solidification with the upper and lower walls extending to the intersecting ribs 
</p>
<p>(depicted vertically in Figure 6.5). A minor thickness increase is noticeable at the 
</p>
<p>mid-span center of the ribs, accompanied by a gradual thinning of the ribs as they 
</p>
<p>approach the upper and lower walls. This is the region where the laser is triggered off 
</p>
<p>to prevent excessive material deposition at the wall intersections. 
</p>
<p>All samples were crack free and the microstructure of the samples was identical 
</p>
<p>to 1 mm thick walls evaluated in prior studies [251,269]. The grain structure is shown 
</p>
<p>to be fully homogenized. The average porosity across all samples was 0.042% with 
</p>
<p>a maximum porosity of 0.108% on sample CMP-1 due to a single pore in the bulk of 
</p>
<p>the material. The polished and etched sample images are shown in supplemental and 
</p>
<p>a table with the measured porosity for each sample.  
</p>
<p>Varying degrees of material removal were present on all samples, as expected 
</p>
<p>since this was one of the objectives of the experiment. The cross-section micrographs 
</p>
<p>reveal that the walls of the AFM samples exhibit a visually smooth surface. The 
</p>
<p>internal surface increased in comparison to the as-built channel by as much as 2% to 
</p>
<p>12% across the different AFM samples, and the perimeter increased up to 15%. The 
</p>
<p>corners indicate sharp stress risers, revealing that abrasive machining was less 
</p>
<p>effective in the corners. The AFM process selectively removed material from the mid-
</p>
<p>span thickness of the ribs. This resulted in nearly flat ribs with some AFM walls 
</p>
<p>showing concavity. The AFM overlay with the as-built sample confirms the selective 
</p>
<p>material removal in the rib mid-span and no removal in the corners.  
</p>
<p>The internal surfaces of chemically milled samples are larger compared to that 
</p>
<p>of the other samples and the corners are fully rounded. The walls appear jagged at 
</p>
<p>high magnification and much smoother as more material is removed. This is due to 
</p>
<p>the milling solution following the as-built profile of the surface. The area increased 
</p>
<p>by 16% to 36% if compared to the as-built state depending on the CM sample, and 
</p>
<p>the perimeter increased by 12 to 20%. The overlay images show that around the edges 
</p>
<p>and corners the milling is uniform. The processing time for CM is the shortest and 
</p>
<p>the surface changes are directly related to the increase of milling time and flow rate.  
</p>
<p>CMP allows for the removal of the widest range of material and therefore a wider 
</p>
<p>control over the determination of the inner surface, as it allowed to obtain an area </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—115 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>change spanning from 8 to 27%. At high magnification, the CMP samples revealed 
</p>
<p>jagged features on the walls which gradually smoothed out as more material was 
</p>
<p>removed. The perimeters were 11 to 19% larger compared to the nominal perimeter 
</p>
<p>of the channel. There were some remnants of residual powder with material removal 
</p>
<p>uniform on the walls and corners. Visually, there appears to be a smooth wall profile 
</p>
<p>indicating reduced texture. PECM cross sections indicate some removal of material 
</p>
<p>from the walls, but the surfaces still show significant texture. There is no change to 
</p>
<p>the corner geometry and perimeters increased (18 to 24%), indicating some reduction 
</p>
<p>in material. The TEM sample does not exhibit any visual change compared to the as-
</p>
<p>built. The overlay with the as-built sample looks nearly identical and this observation 
</p>
<p>is confirmed by perimeter and area measurements.  
</p>
<p>For all the surface enhancement processes, the increase in measured area is 
</p>
<p>related to the amount of material removed per side. The perimeter length exceeds the 
</p>
<p>design value and is notably influenced by the extent of powder adherence, as 
</p>
<p>demonstrated by both the as-built and the TEM samples. Most processes eliminated 
</p>
<p>the partially adhered powder particles except for TEM. Although a small number of 
</p>
<p>residual particles persisted on the samples, a minimum material removal of 45 µm on 
</p>
<p>each side is required to eliminate over 90% of the particles.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—116 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.5. Polished cross section of channel samples incorporating an overlay 
</p>
<p>of the section of an as-built (AB-2) channel (gray). Orange indicates the 
</p>
<p>material removal from the sample. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—117 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.4.1.2 Scanning Electron Microscope  
</p>
<p>Figure 6.6 shows the SEM images of the inner surface samples, taken normal to 
</p>
<p>the surface. The as-built samples indicate excessive powder adhered to the surface, 
</p>
<p>previously reported to vary based on build angle and geometry [269]. The adhered 
</p>
<p>powder particles were tallied per millimeter of area and averaged. The powder count 
</p>
<p>for the AB-2 and AB-3 samples were similar, and that of TEM-1 was only slightly 
</p>
<p>lower. Since the powder is partially melted into the solidified material, the TEM 
</p>
<p>process does not provide sufficient energy to vaporize the material because the 
</p>
<p>thermal mass is too much. The AFM samples reveal excess powder in the corners, 
</p>
<p>which aligns to observations related to the micrograph of the cross sections. The 
</p>
<p>visual SEM of the surface indicates some periodic texturing and varies with the AFM 
</p>
<p>parameters. The AFM-15 sample reveals a substantial amount of adhered powder that 
</p>
<p>has been partially removed, leaving only a portion still attached. This indicates that 
</p>
<p>not enough material was removed (41 µm). At higher SEM magnification (Figure 
</p>
<p>6.7), visible lines (i.e., scratches) are indicated in the direction of flow due to the 
</p>
<p>abrasives in the slurry [280]. 
</p>
<p>The CM samples are characterized by some jagged edges and irregularities, 
</p>
<p>which are more visible at higher magnification. The surface texture also exhibits 
</p>
<p>directionality, more pronounced in CM-9 and CM-17. The surface texture aligns with 
</p>
<p>the chemical solution flowed, which is indicated explicitly in CM-17, where flow was 
</p>
<p>disrupted by an adhered particle. Several remnant particles and crystals can also be 
</p>
<p>observed along with some evidence of delamination of selected particles. The 
</p>
<p>delamination is likely due to a partially adhered particle where a gap was present, and 
</p>
<p>the chemical solution could flow.  
</p>
<p>The results of the CMP process are similar to those obtained with CM, whereby 
</p>
<p>some surface directionality can be observed and it is due to the flow of the chemical 
</p>
<p>solution. However, the jagged features observed on the surface of the CMP samples 
</p>
<p>are coarser if compared to those visible on the surface of the CM samples and the 
</p>
<p>surface is smooth, in general. The CMP-2 and CMP-6 samples indicate selective 
</p>
<p>etching at grain boundaries, which is superficial, along with annealing twins. Some 
</p>
<p>random micro-pitting is also present on the surface of the CMP samples, indicating 
</p>
<p>that the material was over-exposed to the chemistry during processing. CMP-7 
</p>
<p>displays an uneven surface characterized by round particle and fractures. Some 
</p>
<p>evidence of brittle fractures are observed and cleavage fractures which indicate the 
</p>
<p>polishing media impacted and sheared loosely adhered powder particles. 
</p>
<p>The PECM process sample also illustrates micro-pitting, which is typical of 
</p>
<p>corrosion when the electrolytic residues remain on the surface [281]. The surface 
</p>
<p>characteristic are similar to those of surfaces obtained with L-PBF and   finished with 
</p>
<p>ECP, as documented in the work of An et al. [175]. A limited number of powder 
</p>
<p>particles can be observed on the surface of the PECM samples. The center of the 
</p>
<p>channel exhibits less pitting than the edges near the sidewall due to the shape of the 
</p>
<p>used electrode and to differences in local current. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—118 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.6. SEM 50x magnification images of the surface of each channel. 
</p>
<p>Images are taken normal to the surface. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—119 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.7. SEM 500x magnification images of the surface of each channel. 
</p>
<p>Images are taken normal to the surface. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.4.1.3 Computer Tomography 
</p>
<p>The selected samples that were µ-CT scanned (Figure 6.8) included AB-2, AFM-
</p>
<p>2, AFM-15, CM-3, CM-20, PECM-1, and CMP-6. The resulting images may not be 
</p>
<p>fully representative of each of the corresponding processes since process parameters 
</p>
<p>varied for each. The µ-CT data sets were reconstructed into a mesh with gaussian 
</p>
<p>smoothing applied. The macro visual observations are similar to the optical and SEM 
</p>
<p>images. The as-built samples indicate significant powder particle adherence. It is 
</p>
<p>difficult to distinguish the corners and edge transitions and any potential waviness on 
</p>
<p>the surface is masked by the high density of powder. The AFM-2 and AFM-15 
</p>
<p>samples show random particle adhesion with significant particle accumulation in the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—120 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>corners and waviness along the length. The periodicity of the waviness varies based 
</p>
<p>on the sample.  
</p>
<p>The images of the CM-3 and CM-20 samples show significant rounding of the 
</p>
<p>corners along with some waviness along the length. There are also some indications 
</p>
<p>of non-uniform material such as mid-points of the sidewalls. The PECM-1 sample 
</p>
<p>exhibits fine roughness along the walls, which is smaller than the powder observed 
</p>
<p>characterizing the image of AB-2. There is a high density of powder in the corners 
</p>
<p>and some indication of waviness along the length. The image of CMP-6 shows some 
</p>
<p>indications of powder adherence and waviness along the length with a similar pattern 
</p>
<p>to what can be observed in the image of CM-3.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.8. Micro-CT Scanning images related to various surface enhancement 
</p>
<p>processes. The images are taken normal to the channel cross-section and 
</p>
<p>looking in the same direction as the direction of the flow.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—121 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.5 FLOW TESTING 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>A total of 60 tests were completed with three pressure settings for each of the 20 
</p>
<p>channel samples. The measurement results of the optical and SEM images are listed 
</p>
<p>in Table 6.2 along with the calculated discharge coefficient (Cd). The Cd was used 
</p>
<p>to establish a relative comparison of the various surfaces resulting due to the polishing 
</p>
<p>processes and is calculated according to 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝐶𝑑 =
</p>
<p>�̇�
</p>
<p>𝜌𝐴𝑣
=
</p>
<p>�̇�
</p>
<p>𝐴√2𝜌∆𝑃
 6.1 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where ṁ is mass flow rate (gram/second), A is area (mm2), ρ is density of the 
</p>
<p>water (grams/cm3), and P is pressure (Bar) at the port.  
</p>
<p>The Cd is reported based on the actual measured area of the channels, and the 
</p>
<p>standard deviation is computed from three distinct pressure runs conducted for each 
</p>
<p>channel. The mass flow rate was based on the measured value. The variance between 
</p>
<p>the flow rate calculated using the venturi and that measured by the flow meter was 
</p>
<p>within a margin of less than 2% across all tests. The cavitation of the venturi was 
</p>
<p>verified based on the exit pressure to inlet pressure ratio (&lt;0.80) for each test run. 
</p>
<p>The water mass flow rates remained uniform for each test, approximately measuring 
</p>
<p>85 ±0.0086 g/sec, 118 ±0.0075 g/sec, and 167 ±0.0078 g/sec corresponding to the 
</p>
<p>respective tank pressures. The pressure and temperature data were taken from a 10 
</p>
<p>second period (100 samples/sec) and averaged. The change in area is calculated from 
</p>
<p>the average of six measurements (AB-Avg) from the AB-2 and AB-3 channels.  
</p>

</p>
<p>CMP-7 11.63 ± 0.13 6.82 ± 0.039 9.3% 58 ± 12.9 8 ± 1 0.56 ± 0.007 26.7% 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The discharge coefficient is plotted against measured cross-sectional area, 
</p>
<p>measured perimeter, and average areal texture and depicted in Figure 6.9. The Cd is 
</p>
<p>calculated using the measured area. At Cd below 0.6, the differences due to the as-
</p>
<p>designed (6.452 mm2) to actual as-built area were minor. However, above a Cd of 0.7 
</p>
<p>when the nominal as-designed area is used to calculate Cd, it overpredicts its value 
</p>
<p>since it is smaller than actual area (Supplemental). This is an important aspect related 
</p>
<p>to additively manufactured surfaces since the actual as-built areas can be smaller due 
</p>
<p>to powder adherence, melt pool irregularities, dross formation. The area of the CM 
</p>
<p>samples increased by 36% resulting in a 120% change of the Cd. The as-built samples 
</p>
<p>and the TEM samples featured the highest resistance, as expected, and the area value 
</p>
<p>is smaller than the as-designed value by 4%. The obvious trend is the increase in cross 
</p>
<p>sectional area due to the various processes resulting in larger Cd. However, for 
</p>
<p>several channels with similar area, there were differences in the Cd. AFM-2, AFM-
</p>
<p>17, CMP-1, CMP -2, and CMP-7 all featured similar areas, but the Cd varies by more 
</p>
<p>than 78%. Alternatively, comparable Cd values of around 0.9 were measured for 
</p>
<p>AFM-2 and CM-17, despite a notable disparity in their cross-sectional areas, which 
</p>
<p>differ by 19.7%. Similar cross-sectional areas were measured for AB-2, AB-3, TEM-
</p>
<p>1, and AFM-15, while AFM-15 is characterized by a higher Cd value. These 
</p>
<p>variations can be attributed to differences in surface texture, perimeter, and the 
</p>
<p>resulting resistance within the channel. 
</p>
<p>As the perimeter increases, the Cd generally decreases linearly. The larger 
</p>
<p>perimeter, in the as-built samples, is caused by the partially adhered powder creating 
</p>
<p>boundary layer turbulence and increased resistance [251]. The as-built perimeter is 
</p>
<p>30% larger than the as-designed nominal perimeter. Figure 6.9B also depicts AFM-2 
</p>
<p>and AFM-7 among the smallest perimeters. This is due to the reduction of the 
</p>
<p>sidewall texture, removing the mid-span rib material from the convex shape to flat. 
</p>
<p>Additionally, there is powder remaining in the corners of several AFM samples 
</p>
<p>creating a radius. This indicates the excess powder in the corners of the AFM samples 
</p>
<p>did not have a significant impact on the Cd. There are a few outliers in perimeter plot, 
</p>
<p>namely CM-16, CM-17, CM-20, and CMP-6. These outliers have lower average </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—123 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>surface texture among the data set but their peculiar behavior in the perimeter plot 
</p>
<p>cannot be solely explained by the average Sa. There is an increase of Cd when the 
</p>
<p>texture increases, as expected. There is a linear rise in Cd up to 0.6 based on a 
</p>
<p>decrease of the surface texture. According to this data set, the surface texture below 
</p>
<p>14 µm plateaus although there is an increase in Cd.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.9. Summary of Cd and comparison to: A) measured channel cross-
</p>
<p>sectional area, B) measured perimeter, and C) measured average areal texture, 
</p>
<p>Sa. The data is colored based on the surface enhancement process. 
</p>
<p>Sa represents an average value and does not provide a comprehensive 
</p>
<p>characterization of the surface and its flow resistance. To gain a deeper insight into 
</p>
<p>how surface texture impacts Cd, the data is evaluated based on the magnitude of the 
</p>
<p>peaks and valleys and the density of these disturbances. While the maximum height 
</p>
<p>of the surface, denoted as Sz, can serve as a measure, it may not always accurately 
</p>
<p>depict the true surface characteristics due to the presence of outliers, such as 
</p>
<p>extremely elevated peaks or deep valleys. Sz, which sums the maximum peak height 
</p>
<p>(Sp) and maximum valley depth (Sv), was compared to the Cd and no correlations 
</p>
<p>were observed (Supplemental). This implies two key points: 1) the existence of </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—124 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>anomalous localized peaks does not affect Cd, and 2) Sz is an inadequate measure to 
</p>
<p>understand the surface texture mechanism influencing Cd. Instead, core roughness 
</p>
<p>depth (Sk) is utilized as a substitute for Sz, representing the average peak-to-valley 
</p>
<p>distance. The surface texture parameters discussed in this section are provided in 
</p>
<p>Table 3. 
</p>
<p>Figure 6.10 shows that Sk reveals that as the peak-to-valley depth diminishes, 
</p>
<p>Cd rises and then stabilizes around a depth of approximately 40 µm. The peak height, 
</p>
<p>referred to as reduced peak height (Spk), as indicated in Figure 6.10, illustrates that 
</p>
<p>higher peaks contribute to greater resistance. For the samples featuring  the highest 
</p>
<p>Cd (CM-16 and CM-20), Spk constitutes roughly 8% of the surface, whereas it 
</p>
<p>accounts for 13% in samples with lower Cd. The remaining portion of the surface 
</p>
<p>comprises core roughness depth. Conversely, reduced valley depth (Svk) was 
</p>
<p>assessed but no correlation was observed with Cd.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 6.3. Summary of Surface Texture Measurements. 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
