</p>
<p>CMP-6 12.2 231.8 38.7 14.5 8.4 493.4 22.8 12 5.4 
</p>
<p>CMP-7 17.5 506.2 52.6 22.0 16.4 399.2 36.6 12 11.7 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—125 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.10. Discharge coefficient in relation to core roughness and reduced 
</p>
<p>peak height. 
</p>
<p> The roughness is due to powder adherence and melt pool irregularities. To 
</p>
<p>better illustrate this relation, Figure 6.11 reports the average texture as a function of 
</p>
<p>the average material removal per side in. The value of Cd is displayed as colored dots 
</p>
<p>whose shade is related to a scale. The value of Cd is consistently higher than 0.75 
</p>
<p>whenever and amount of sidewall material equivalent to the average powder size (70 
</p>
<p>µm) was removed. This led to a reduction in surface texture to values below 14 µm 
</p>
<p>and is independent of the surface enhancement process. The results of these 
</p>
<p>experiments revealed a strong variation of Cd if only the minimum powder size (55 
</p>
<p>µm) was removed, and limited discrete particles remained. According to particle 
</p>
<p>count analysis, a material removal of 45 µm eliminated 90% of the adhered powder. 
</p>
<p>However, this still resulted in higher Sa values due to remaining melt pool surface 
</p>
<p>irregularities, which has an adverse impact on Cd. Particle density alone does not 
</p>
<p>correlate with the Cd associated with the applied surface enhancement treatment. 
</p>
<p>While material removal does play a role, its value alone cannot be used to predict the 
</p>
<p>value of Cd. Certain processes, such as AFM, could achieve lower resistance with 
</p>
<p>significantly less material removal if compared to that achievable with CM. For 
</p>
<p>certain applications, the aim might be to minimize material removal while 
</p>
<p>simultaneously achieving the lowest possible surface texture. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—126 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.11. Average areal texture compared to material removal per side. 
</p>
<p>The average texture (Sa), as depicted in Figure 6.9C, levels out as the discharge 
</p>
<p>coefficients measured for the samples exceed a Cd of 0.7. Sa, being an average value, 
</p>
<p>does not fully characterize the surface and cannot be directly related to variations of 
</p>
<p>Cd. To characterize the variations of Cd in relation to surface features, the surfaces 
</p>
<p>were categorized based on their roughness peaks and on the density of these peaks. 
</p>
<p>Reduced peak height (Spk) and density of peaks (Spd) are plotted in Figure 6.12A 
</p>
<p>and B, respectively, alongside the channel cross-sectional area. The Spk represents 
</p>
<p>the mean height of peaks which are present above the core roughness based on an 
</p>
<p>areal material ratio curve (~10% of surface) [282]. This representation allowed for a 
</p>
<p>clear correlation between the surface treatment of a sample and the associated 
</p>
<p>measured Cd. 
</p>
<p>In samples whose associated Cd was less than 0.7, roughness is primarily caused 
</p>
<p>by partially adhered powder, accounting for more than 70% of the surface texture. 
</p>
<p>Directional measurements, such as profile (Pa) and roughness (Ra), were also 
</p>
<p>examined but did not provide sufficient resolution in the surface characterization to 
</p>
<p>correlate with measured Cd. This is an important result, since Ra is typically reported 
</p>
<p>but is simply an average directional (single line) value. Since the peaks are one of the 
</p>
<p>primary contributors to flow resistance, the Ra is insufficient to fully characterize the 
</p>
<p>surface in case accurate Cd predictions are the final objective. 
</p>
<p>In addition to the peak height (Spk), the density of the peaks (Spd) also 
</p>
<p>contributes to flow resistance, as illustrated in Figure 6.12B. Spd is traditionally used 
</p>
<p>for bearing surface evaluation and would indicate a larger contact surface area. For </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—127 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>surface enhancements and flow characterization, higher Spd values imply that there 
</p>
<p>are more peaks per unit area but that they have been smoothed away. The samples 
</p>
<p>featuring the lowest Cd were planarized, thus they the peaks were smoothed and this 
</p>
<p>is correlated with higher Spd values.   
</p>
<p>Waviness was also evaluated using a cutoff filter of 0.8 mm. Waviness is visually 
</p>
<p>observed in all samples (Figure 6.8). However, samples whose internal surfaces are 
</p>
<p>characterized by identical waviness value still generated flows with significantly 
</p>
<p>different Cd. To better explain this phenomenon, the waviness peaks (Wp) were 
</p>
<p>evaluated and their values suggest that lowered peaks cause an increase of Cd, as seen 
</p>
<p>in Figure 6.12C. The waviness valleys (Wv) were also evaluated and cannot be 
</p>
<p>correlated to Cd. To further validate result of the waviness peaks, the periodic 
</p>
<p>waviness peak density (Wpc) was analyzed and it was observed that it features 
</p>
<p>different values for samples associated with higher Cd. Wpc is defined as the number 
</p>
<p>of peaks per the unit length. In this case the sampling length was 38 mm. As the 
</p>
<p>density of the waviness decreases, the Cd increases. The CM samples featured the 
</p>
<p>lowest Wpc, which was approximately 50% lower than that of the as-built samples. 
</p>
<p>It is observed that the waviness has a stronger impact on samples associated with Cd 
</p>
<p>greater than 0.7. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.12. Channel cross sectional area compared to A) Reduced peak 
</p>
<p>height, Spk, B) density of peaks, Spd, C) waviness peak height, Wp, and D) 
</p>
<p>Density of waviness, WPc. 
</p>
<p>These observations indicate that an effective surface enhancement process 
</p>
<p>should remove the partially and fully adhered powder particles (roughness peaks and 
</p>
<p>their density) and smooth the waviness peaks and density thereof to reduce resistance 
</p>
<p>within the channel. It is important to note that, as the peak waviness decreases, the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—128 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>average waviness decreases, but then valleys eventually become the primary 
</p>
<p>contributor to the flow resistance. Achieving full planarization of a surface 
</p>
<p>necessitates reducing both peaks and valleys related to waviness, requiring a larger 
</p>
<p>amount of material removal.  
</p>
<p>The impact of the combined texture effects is corroborated by the analysis results 
</p>
<p>depicted in Figure 6.13, namely the comparison between a ratio of roughness 
</p>
<p>peak/density (Spk/Spd) with waviness peak/density (Wp/WPc). Cd increases 
</p>
<p>significantly with lower values of roughness and density. As the roughness ratio 
</p>
<p>approaches 0.04, a trend can be observed: the surface waviness begins to exert a more 
</p>
<p>pronounced influence on the increase of Cd. However, to reduce the waviness 
</p>
<p>necessitates more material removal compared to the roughness and an increase in 
</p>
<p>surface area for planarization. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 6.13. Ratio of roughness peaks to density (Spk/Spd) compared to the 
</p>
<p>ratio of waviness peak to density (Wp/WPc). Note Y-axis is Log scale.  
</p>
<p>The experimental data underscored that an increase in area causes an increase of 
</p>
<p>Cd, as expected. According to this experiment, approximately 65% of Cd variation 
</p>
<p>can be attributed to changes in area. The wetted perimeter also contributes to flow 
</p>
<p>resistance (8%). The ideal perimeter should match the mathematical definition, in 
</p>
<p>theory, but in application varies along with the area. For instance, in a square channel, 
</p>
<p>each sidewall is fully planarized or flat. The surface texture accounts for 
</p>
<p>approximately 25% of the variation in Cd. The surface roughness due to the adhered 
</p>
<p>powder and waviness resulting from irregular melt pools and build layering affect not </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—129 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>only the perimeter, but also the overall 3D surface in the direction of the flow. This 
</p>
<p>is evident since in several channels that had the same perimeter and channel area, the 
</p>
<p>Cd varied based on the resulting texture due to the various surface enhancements.  
</p>
<p>The goal of this testing and characterization was to demonstrate differences in 
</p>
<p>surfaces resulting from each of the surface enhancement processes. The performance 
</p>
<p>from each of the different microchannels with surface enhancements, along with a 
</p>
<p>comparison to as-built baseline samples was summarized in Table 2. Chemical 
</p>
<p>milling and CMP demonstrated the potential to reduce or eliminate the powder 
</p>
<p>adherence and minimize waviness. However, these processes also removed the most 
</p>
<p>material. Sample CM-17 featured some of the lowest peak and density values for 
</p>
<p>roughness and waviness and with a slightly larger area would have likely achieved a 
</p>
<p>performance in terms of Cd equal to that provided by the samples associated with the 
</p>
<p>highest Cd. The evaluated surface enhancement technologies could also be improved 
</p>
<p>by adopting optimized process parameters. One goal may be to minimize material 
</p>
<p>removal while achieving a reduction of roughness and waviness peaks and density. 
</p>
<p>The material removal must be balanced with the processing economics, as increased 
</p>
<p>material removal increases the cost.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>6.6 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>This work demonstrated that various surface enhancements can be applied to 
</p>
<p>internal square microchannel samples produced using LP-DED with 1 mm thick 
</p>
<p>walls. The samples featured a 2.54 x 2.54 mm square cross section and were built 
</p>
<p>using the NASA HR-1 (Fe-Ni-Cr) alloy. Several techniques were demonstrated 
</p>
<p>including abrasive flow machining (AFM), chemical milling (CM), chemical 
</p>
<p>mechanical polishing (CMP), electrochemical machining (PECM), and thermal 
</p>
<p>energy method (TEM) to modify the internal surfaces. Flow testing was performed 
</p>
<p>to characterize the flow resistance of the resulting channel surfaces and the samples 
</p>
<p>were then destructively evaluated using SEM, µ-CT, optical microscopy, and optical 
</p>
<p>surface texture. The goal of this study was to demonstrate how variations in the LP-
</p>
<p>DED surfaces can be obtained, and to understand the underlying causes for 
</p>
<p>corresponding changes in the resulting flow resistance. This data could then be used 
</p>
<p>to provide requirements for additively manufactured components, such as heat 
</p>
<p>exchanges.  
</p>
<p>The following conclusions can be drawn: 
</p>
<p>• The various surface enhancements result in different surface characteristics 
</p>
<p>deriving from the mechanics of the process. These include: remnant powder, 
</p>
<p>jagged edges and crystals, scratches, pitting or selective grain boundary 
</p>
<p>attack.  
</p>
<p>• The CM and CMP processes removed the largest amount of material per 
</p>
<p>side (up to 213 µm) resulting in a larger cross-sectional area (36%) 
</p>
<p>compared to the as-built samples.   </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 6—130 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• The as-built perimeter was 32% longer than nominal perimeter (the design 
</p>
<p>perimeter). This is due to the adhered powder particles and surface 
</p>
<p>irregularities resulting from the melt pool and build layering. 
</p>
<p>• A 121% increase in Cd could be achieved using chemical milling with a 
</p>
<p>36% increase in area. 
</p>
<p>• 65% of the change in flow resistance is attributed to the area change; 8% 
</p>
<p>depends on the perimeter and 25% on the surface texture.  
</p>
<p>• To increase the Cd, the surface enhancement process should eliminate 
</p>
<p>adhered powder (roughness peaks and their density) and planarize peaks and 
</p>
<p>density related to waviness. This implies that an effective surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement process necessitates reducing both peaks and density thereof 
</p>
<p>related to surface roughness and waviness, requiring increased material 
</p>
<p>removal. 
</p>
<p>• The material removal should be at least equal to the average powder 
</p>
<p>diameter (70 µm) to achieve reductions in flow resistance. While material 
</p>
<p>removal equal to the minimum powder diameter (45 µm) results in 90% of 
</p>
<p>powder elimination, it also results in significant variations of Cd.    
</p>
<p>• The ratio of roughness and waviness peak and density (Spk/Spd and 
</p>
<p>Wp/WPc) is proposed as a relevant surface characterization parameter. 
</p>
<p>Using this parameter, it is possible to observe a transition where the effect 
</p>
<p>of surface roughness due to peaks is reduced, and a reduction of the surface 
</p>
<p>waviness is required to increase the Cd. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The application of surface enhancements to surface geometries obtained with the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED process demonstrated that a surface could be modified based on a set of 
</p>
<p>requirements. This data along with future testing can tailor a surface for fluid friction 
</p>
<p>factors, fatigue life, corrosion, heat transfer, or even aesthetics. This is particularly 
</p>
<p>important in heat exchangers, where each of these attributes are balanced during the 
</p>
<p>design process. A combination of the LP-DED process and surface enhancements can 
</p>
<p>be used to fabricate large scale components for aerospace, power generation, and 
</p>
<p>industrial heat exchangers. Future research might combine processes, such as AFM 
</p>
<p>and CM/CMP, using the advantages of the unique surfaces from each. This study 
</p>
<p>engaged commercial vendors for the development of surface enhancements. It is 
</p>
<p>anticipated that these processes could be effectively and economically utilized for 
</p>
<p>future applications requiring surface variations to meet specific requirements. 
</p>
<p>  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—131 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7 Friction Factor of Channels with 
</p>
<p>Improved Surface Finishing 
 
</p>
<p>Major portions of this chapter were published in: 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Gradl, P., Cervone, A., Colonna, P., 2025. Enhancement of friction factors for 
</p>
<p>microchannels fabricated using laser powder directed energy deposition. Materials 
</p>
<p>&amp; Design 251, 113673. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.matdes.2025.113673 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7.1 ABSTRACT 
</p>
<p>Designing high-performance and aerospace-grade heat exchangers requires 
</p>
<p>detailed characterization of the as-manufactured geometry, including cross-sectional 
</p>
<p>area and surface texture, to reduce uncertainties in performance prediction and issues 
</p>
<p>regarding subsequent system integration. This chapter presents experimental testing 
</p>
<p>and analysis of 600 mm length channels fabricated using the LP-DED process. 
</p>
<p>Surface finishing is often required for heat exchangers built using AM to meet the 
</p>
<p>desired pressure drop specifications. Moreover, the highly textured as-built surfaces 
</p>
<p>result in a measured pressure drop higher than what is predicted by current 
</p>
<p>correlations. Various surface enhancement techniques were applied to the internal 
</p>
<p>surfaces of the channels to tailor flow dynamics and induce variations in pressure 
</p>
<p>drop. The study focused on evaluating the pressure drop and friction factor variations 
</p>
<p>depending on the type of surface and its conditions. A novel method for calculating 
</p>
<p>the hydraulic diameter, accounting for surface texture variations, is proposed and 
</p>
<p>used to correlate and analyze all the collected experimental data. 
</p>
<p>Surface enhancements, including abrasive flow machining (AFM), chemical 
</p>
<p>milling (CM), and chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), were compared to the as-
</p>
<p>built surface. Based on experimental testing, channels processed with surface 
</p>
<p>enhancements provide a tenfold reduction in differential pressure compared to the as-
</p>
<p>built channels. After testing, the samples were destructively sectioned to obtain 
</p>
<p>geometric and detailed surface texture information. This characterization helped to 
</p>
<p>inform a new prediction method for determining equivalent sand grain roughness, 
</p>
<p>thus reducing the uncertainty of predicted friction factors. The new correlation allows 
</p>
<p>to estimate friction factor and pressure drop with a deviation from the experimental 
</p>
<p>data that is within 20% of their value. The identification of the mechanisms at the 
</p>
<p>basis of the formation of surface texture allowed to categorize distinct aspects: 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—132 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>roughness peaks, peak smoothing/reduction, minimized roughness, and combined 
</p>
<p>waviness and valley reduction. These aspects of surface texture can be associated to 
</p>
<p>specific friction factor ranges. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7.2 INTRODUCTION 
</p>
<p>Critical design features of high-performance, aerospace-grade heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>include the geometry of internal channels and their surface finish. These 
</p>
<p>characteristics are fundamental for the transfer of thermal energy between fluids and 
</p>
<p>the flow friction, which ultimately determine whether the component and the overall 
</p>
<p>system function properly. Designing these channels is often an iterative process that 
</p>
<p>aims to minimize the associated pressure loss, maximize heat transfer, and maximize 
</p>
<p>the service life of the component. Optimizing pressure drop, heat transfer, and fatigue 
</p>
<p>life involves compromising between contrasting requirements. Surface texture 
</p>
<p>significantly affects pressure losses and heat transfer. In this respect, Chapters 5 and 
</p>
<p>6 document research on LP-DED microchannels and the related first-order flow 
</p>
<p>resistance in terms of discharge coefficient, while this chapter focuses on the study 
</p>
<p>of friction factors, their relationship with sand grain roughness and the derivation of 
</p>
<p>a correlation, the definition of hydraulic diameter suitable for this case, and the 
</p>
<p>correlations for the prediction of pressure losses in square microchannels. 
</p>
<p> The Moody diagram, established decades ago, provides nondimensional 
</p>
<p>values of the friction factor as a function of the Reynolds number and relative pipe 
</p>
<p>roughness [283]. Moody developed this diagram valid for laminar and turbulent flows 
</p>
<p>using experimental data from Darcy-Weisbach and Nikuradse [284,285]. Relative 
</p>
<p>pipe roughness relates the height of surface roughness to the hydraulic diameter, 
</p>
<p>however it cannot provide a merit parameter that is adequate for the complexity of 
</p>
<p>additively manufactured surfaces and its relationship to the original sand grain 
</p>
<p>roughness characterizing the experimental data of Darcy-Weisbach and Nikuradse. 
</p>
<p>The proportionality relationship between relative roughness and friction factor is well 
</p>
<p>known: as roughness increases, the friction factor also increases, and this is 
</p>
<p>mathematically expressed by the Colebrook-White correlation [286,287]. Early 
</p>
<p>experiments by Darcy, Nikuradse, and Colebrook were limited to values of relative 
</p>
<p>roughness lower than 5% [288]. Researchers like Kandlikar et al. expanded the 
</p>
<p>measurement range to values of relative roughness up to 14% [289]. Additive 
</p>
<p>manufacturing (AM) offers several advantages if adopted for the fabrication of 
</p>
<p>special heat exchangers, especially in terms of complexity and miniaturization of 
</p>
<p>channels, therefore it is becoming more prominent. However, traditional correlations 
</p>
<p>for the modeling of the fluid dynamic effect of surface texture perform very poorly 
</p>
<p>in case of surfaces obtained with AM, resulting in actual values of the friction factor 
</p>
<p>that are higher than those predicted by correlations based on prior experimental data 
</p>
<p>[290]. McClain et al. [291] highlighted the necessity of accurately characterizing and 
</p>
<p>measuring surface texture and equivalent sand grain roughness for accurate 
</p>
<p>predictions because the roughness to hydraulic diameter ratio is high, and outside the 
</p>
<p>range of validity of current correlations.   </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—133 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Several authors have investigated flow friction associated with simple- and 
</p>
<p>complex-geometry channels made with L-PBF and as-built surfaces. Richermoz et 
</p>
<p>al. [292] experimented on circular and round channels made of Alloy 600 and 
</p>
<p>manufactured with L-PBF.  The channels featured alternating 90° serpentine bends. 
</p>
<p>They measured a 37% increase in friction factor over traditionally manufactured 
</p>
<p>channels. However, they noted that uncertainty in hydraulic diameter affected the 
</p>
<p>results, which were measured using CT and basic directional roughness 
</p>
<p>measurements. Zhou et al. [169] investigated changes  of hydraulic diameter in L-
</p>
<p>PBF-made channels, depending on build angle, dross formation, and warping. They 
</p>
<p>proposed a linear model adjustment but did not relate such adjustment to physical 
</p>
<p>geometry. These differences highlight the importance of perimeter biasing due to 
</p>
<p>internal channel texture as this affects the hydraulic diameter. 
</p>
<p>Snyder et al. [293] reported that varying L-PBF parameters reduced surface 
</p>
<p>roughness by 50%, resulting in a proportional reduction of the friction factor. Kirsch 
</p>
<p>et al. [294] found a three-fold increase in friction factor with a 30% difference in 
</p>
<p>hydraulic diameter at Reynolds numbers from 3,000 to 10,000. Hartsfield et al. [295] 
</p>
<p>concluded that sand grain roughness does not correlate with profilometer roughness 
</p>
<p>measurements of L-PBF surfaces. These researchers therefore proposed to 
</p>
<p>approximate the value of sand grain roughness as 50% of the mean powder diameter. 
</p>
<p>Zhu et al. [167] estimated that values of relative roughness can be estimated by 
</p>
<p>multiplying the average areal surface texture (Sa) from each surface by a factor of 
</p>
<p>two. This work considered solely L-PBF-made horizontal round tubes with diameters 
</p>
<p>greater than 6 mm and as-built surfaces. Jamshidinia et al. [113] put into evidence 
</p>
<p>that surface roughness resulting from L-PBF is related to process heat input, 
</p>
<p>emphasizing the importance of build parameters. However, the friction factor 
</p>
<p>correlations they obtained cannot be broadly applied to channels produced with 
</p>
<p>different AM processes. 
</p>
<p>Most documented research on flow friction associated with additively 
</p>
<p>manufactured channels focused on channels with as-built surfaces, thus no post-
</p>
<p>processing of the surfaces was considered. Favero et al. [165] performed friction 
</p>
<p>measurements on oval copper channels made with L-PBF and post-processed with 
</p>
<p>chemical milling. They concluded that pressure drop could be reduced by an order of 
</p>
<p>magnitude with a six-fold reduction in average roughness. The developed 
</p>
<p>correlations are based on a hydraulic diameter calculated based on area and perimeter, 
</p>
<p>but no sand grain roughness was considered. Kaur and Singh [168] emphasized the 
</p>
<p>need for thorough research on the localized influence of surface texture.  
</p>
<p>The build mechanics of the LP-DED is fundamentally different from that of L-
</p>
<p>PBF. Results regarding the flow friction of channels made with L-PBF cannot be 
</p>
<p>applied broadly and friction factors associated to channels made with LP-DED are 
</p>
<p>expected to vary due to differences in powder feedstock, process parameters, and 
</p>
<p>environmental process factors like purges and surrounding powder. The derivation of 
</p>
<p>friction factor correlations is further complicated if surfaces are treated with 
</p>
<p>enhancement.  
</p>
<p>Comprehensive experimental data and correlations valid for additively 
</p>
<p>manufactured microchannels with similar geometry and different surface texture are 
</p>
<p>not available yet. Full characterization of surface topography is crucial to understand </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—134 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>the underlying mechanisms causing the texture. Moreover, such surface enhancement 
</p>
<p>methods enable the possibility to improve the internal channel finish and ensuing 
</p>
<p>flow friction through post-processing. Designers of additively manufacture heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers require options to prescribe surface finishing to tune the flow 
</p>
<p>characteristics to meet design objectives, rather than accepting as the only option 
</p>
<p>what results from as-built surfaces. This chapter summarizes therefore an 
</p>
<p>experimental study to determine the pressure drop associated with square channels 
</p>
<p>fabricated with LP-DED and processed with various surface enhancements, resulting 
</p>
<p>in a wide range of surface textures and topographies. The experimental data allowed 
</p>
<p>to establish 1) a new definition of hydraulic diameter which eliminates perimeter bias 
</p>
<p>due to surface texture, 2) an equivalent sand grain roughness, and 3) new correlations 
</p>
<p>that considerably improve friction factor predictions for such channels. The surface 
</p>
<p>texture characteristics are evaluated in detail, providing roughness and waviness 
</p>
<p>attributes which can be directly related to the friction factor. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7.3 METHODOLOGY 
</p>
<p>Channel samples were fabricated specifically for differential pressure drop 
</p>
<p>testing. The experimental campaign took place at the same facility utilized for the 
</p>
<p>discharge coefficient testing detailed in Chapter 6. The test sections of the square 
</p>
<p>microchannels described in this chapter were approximately 615 mm in length. 
</p>
<p>Numerous channels were fabricated and tested in their as-built condition, while 
</p>
<p>several others underwent surface enhancements before testing. After the testing 
</p>
<p>phase, the channel samples were sectioned to characterize the internal surface and 
</p>
<p>flow area to validate assumptions for the analysis and correlations.  
</p>
<p>7.3.1 Fabrication of DED Samples and Processing 
</p>
<p>The 600 mm length test channels were designed with a nominal width and height 
</p>
<p>of 2.54 x 2.54 mm and built using LP-DED. They are shown in Figure 7.1. The square 
</p>
<p>test section included a length of 615 mm with a 25 mm blended transition segment 
</p>
<p>from the square channel to a round extrusion on both ends (Figure 7.1C). This design 
</p>
<p>facilitated the welding of a universal AN-type fitting onto either end as an interface 
</p>
<p>with the rest of the facility. The channels were constructed with the same RPMI 557 
</p>
<p>machine, utilizing an A36 mild steel base plate. The boxes were built using consistent 
</p>
<p>parameters, including a laser power of 350 W, powder feed rate of 23 grams/min, 
</p>
<p>travel speed of 763 mm/min, and a layer height of 0.254 mm. All these parameters 
</p>
<p>are the same as those utilized for the channels described in Chapters 5 and 6. 
</p>
<p>A coarse powder was used for most of the samples, with some samples fabricated 
</p>
<p>using fine powder feedstock. The coarse powder used in the process was rotary 
</p>
<p>atomized by Homogenized Metals Inc. (HMI), featuring a particle size distribution 
</p>
<p>(PSD) ranging from 45 to 105 µm. The specific lot number of the powder was 
</p>
<p>HRA11, with an average size of 73 µm. Notably, the oxygen content measured 68 
</p>
<p>ppm, and the nitrogen content was 12 ppm. The fine powder featured a PSD of 10-</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—135 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>45 µm and average size of 32 µm. The oxygen content was 145 ppm and nitrogen 
</p>
<p>was 10 ppm. The higher oxygen content in the fine powder is attributed to the larger 
</p>
<p>area per volume of the powder. The composition, as determined using ICP, is detailed 
</p>
<p>in Table 7.1. Virgin powder was employed for all builds. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 7.1. Chemical composition of the NASA HR-1 powder utilized to build 
</p>
<p>the channel samples (HMI Lot HRA11/Coarse and HRA9/Fine). 
</p>
<p>Size  Fe Ni Cr Co Mo Ti Al V W 
</p>
<p>Coarse Wt.% Bal. 33.98 14.74 3.78 1.84 2.36 0.24 0.3 1.6 
</p>
<p>Fine Wt.% Bal. 33.91 14.66 3.79 1.83 2.41 0.24 0.3 1.6 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The channels were initially fabricated as larger boxes, as illustrated in Figure 7.1, 
</p>
<p>and then underwent a series of heat treatments, including stress relief, 
</p>
<p>homogenization, solution annealing, and double aging. These heat treatment 
</p>
<p>operations were identical to those described in Section 6.3.1. Following the heat 
</p>
<p>treatment, the boxes were removed from the build plate using a bandsaw. 
</p>
<p>Subsequently, the boxes were individually sectioned using water jet cutting and the 
</p>
<p>ends of the channels were machined in preparation for the welding of the ports. The 
</p>
<p>channels were cleaned, and subsequently an AS5174-04 Inconel 625 fitting was laser-
</p>
<p>welded to each of the inlets and outlets of the samples (Figure 7.1C). The channel 
</p>
<p>that was tested is indicated by the yellow lines, and stock regions were fabricated to 
</p>
<p>either side of the channel to allow it to be sectioned. Each sample was then 
</p>
<p>individually tagged and provided to the vendors for surface enhancement processing 
</p>
<p>(Figure 7.1D).  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—136 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.1. Channel sample fabrication. A) LP-DED build of boxes with 
</p>
<p>multiple channels, B) Completed boxes following deposition, C) Laser-welded 
</p>
<p>fittings; the square channel width is indicated by yellow dotted lines, D) 
</p>
<p>Channel sets with welded inlet and outlet fittings. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—137 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The internal surfaces of the channels underwent surface enhancements identical 
</p>
<p>to those described in Section 6.3.2, except for the PECM and TEM processes, which 
</p>
<p>were not applied. Following the polishing of the internal perimeter, a 0.75 mm 
</p>
<p>diameter hole was drilled at six locations for the pressure transducer port tubes 
</p>
<p>(sequentially tagged as PT03 through PT08), and a 3.175 mm diameter tube was laser 
</p>
<p>welded, as depicted in Figure 7.2. PT01 and PT02 were used for the measurement of 
</p>
<p>the tank pressure prior to the test article. The distances between the tubes are shown 
</p>
<p>in Figure 7.2C. It should be noted that the distance between PT6 and PT7 for some 
</p>
<p>tubes was reduced to ~76 mm (increasing the distance of PT5-PT6 to ~178 mm) due 
</p>
<p>to a mismeasurement during drilling. Further details of the inlet and outlet ports are 
</p>
<p>also shown in Figure 7.2A and B. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.2. Channel fabrication of inlet/outlet ports and instrumentation. A) 
</p>
<p>Inlet port welding, B) Outlet port welding, C) Welding of the instrumentation 
</p>
<p>tubes for pressure transducers. Build direction denoted as BD. 
</p>
<p> In addition to the baseline as-built samples and those whose surfaces were 
</p>
<p>enhanced, additional as-built samples were fabricated with a round channel and a 
</p>
<p>larger area of the square channel. The intended use of the round channels was to 
</p>
<p>provide an appropriate hydraulic diameter conversion from the round to square 
</p>
<p>channels. The channels with larger area were conceived to investigate the comparison 
</p>
<p>between channels with surfaces in the as--built condition and channels with modified 
</p>
<p>surfaces but with an equivalent cross--sectional area. The round (tube) channels were 
</p>
<p>designed with a diameter of 2.87 mm to match the cross-sectional area of the square 
</p>
<p>channels. As discussed in section 7.4.4, it was later discovered that the tubes shrank 
</p>
<p>more than anticipated, and the actual diameter was ~2.7 mm instead of 2.87 mm. The 
</p>
<p>larger square channels were designed to be 2.95 mm in height and width.  
</p>
<p>7.3.2 Surface Enhancements of 600 mm Length Channels 
</p>
<p>The objective of the surface enhancements of the internal surfaces was similar to 
</p>
<p>that driving the tests addressed in Chapter 6 [296]. The objective was therefore to 
</p>
<p>obtain a range of surface conditions (i.e., variations) of the internal channels for each </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—138 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>of the utilized surface enhancement processes. The same processing parameters as 
</p>
<p>those discussed in 6.3.2 were used to achieve the desired surface modifications. The 
</p>
<p>only difference was that the AFM, CM, and CMP processes could be performed 
</p>
<p>bidirectionally since ports were located on each end. The configuration of the 
</p>
<p>channels is shown in Table 7.2 and the corresponding channel identifiers in Table 
</p>
<p>7.3. The samples processed with the surface enhancements (AFM, CM, CMP) were 
</p>
<p>all built with coarse powder (45-105 µm).  
</p>
<p> 
</p>

<p>7.3.3 Test Configuration and Procedures 
</p>
<p>The facility available at NASA Marshall Space Flight Center (MSFC) and 
</p>
<p>reported in Chapter 6 was used for the experimental campaign aimed at obtaining 
</p>
<p>friction factors. The schematic of the test setup is shown in Figure 7.3. The 379-liter 
</p>
<p>GN2 pressurized tank was filled with deionized (DI) water and a 10 µm filter was 
</p>
<p>incorporated downstream of the tank after the main valve. A Potter Aero RAA-1/2-
</p>
<p>301-1/2-5440A flowmeter (3.4 to 36 l/min) was installed in a bypass leg of the system 
</p>
<p>in addition to a 1.016 mm diameter cavitating venturi (Cd = 0.980) to control the 
</p>
<p>mass flow through the channel test articles. The venturi cross-sectional area was sized 
</p>
<p>to be smaller than the smallest cross-section area of the test article. Pressure 
</p>
<p>transducers and thermocouples were installed at locations indicated on the diagram 
</p>
<p>of Figure 7.3. The test article was installed in a blast containment due to the operating 
</p>
<p>pressures.  
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.3. Schematic of the experimental setup realized to test 600 mm length 
</p>
<p>channels. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—140 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The test articles were bolted into a fixture within a groove to hold the channel 
</p>
<p>sample as shown in Figure 7.4. This is due to the concern that the pressurization of 
</p>
<p>the water would cause some distortion of the rather long test articles. In addition, 
</p>
<p>some bowing of the channels was observed following fabrication. Images of the test 
</p>
<p>facility and the fixture installed in a safeguard blast chamber are shown in Figure 7.5. 
</p>
<p>Each channel test article was flushed with deionized (DI) water at 7 bar for a 
</p>
<p>minimum of 10 seconds prior to data collection. The tank was then pressurized at 
</p>
<p>increments of 34, 69, 138 Bar and held for 10 seconds at each pressure with water 
</p>
<p>flowing through both the flow meter and a cavitating venturi (1.106 mm dia. throat).  
</p>
<p>The cavitation of the venturi was verified based on the exit to inlet pressure ratio 
</p>
<p>(&lt;0.80) for each test run. A second set of data was collected at identical tank pressures 
</p>
<p>bypassing the venturi allowing for the flow rate to be maximized for each channel. 
</p>
<p>The pressures for the experiments were selected based on the maximum allowable 
</p>
<p>tank pressure and on the criterion that they should be similar to typical heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>operating pressures. A Dewetron data acquisition system was used in conjunction 
</p>
<p>with the LabVIEW software for system control.  Six sets of data were collected per 
</p>
<p>channel sample.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.4. Holding fixture for channels with a groove (not shown) and bands 
</p>
<p>to secure the test article. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—141 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.5. Test facility with channel sample and holding fixture installed in 
</p>
<p>blast chamber. 
</p>
<p>Information about the instruments whose locations are shown in Figure 7.3 is 
</p>
<p>detailed in Table 7.4. The pressure transducer (PT), thermocouples (TC), flowmeter, 
</p>
<p>and cavitating venturi were all calibrated prior to testing. Instrumentation checks 
</p>
<p>were completed every day of testing. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—142 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Table 7.4. Instrumentation log for the testing of 600 mm length channels. 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>After testing was completed, all recorded data were tabulated for each of tested 
</p>
<p>channel and each tank pressure set point. Figure 7.6 shows an example of the 
</p>
<p>recording of the data related to channel AB-B1.  At each pressure set point, the 
</p>
<p>pressure was kept constant, and data acquired for approximately 10 seconds. The time 
</p>
<p>periods determining the data to be averaged are highlighted in Figure 7.6 as colored 
</p>
<p>vertical bands. The finite pressure differential is obtained by subtracting values of 
</p>
<p>absolute pressures at the selected ports. Mass flow was measured using the flow 
</p>
<p>meter. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.6. Example of raw data set for channel AB-B1 with cavitating venturi 
</p>
<p>[runs AB-B1-4 (gray), AB-B1-5 (blue), AB-B1-6 (red) ]. 
</p>
<p> The necessary fluid properties were obtained for each data set and run. The 
</p>
<p>density of water (g/cc) was calculated with a reference model [297] using the average 
</p>
<p>temperature at the inlet and outlet of the channel and the average channel pressure to </p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>thermodynamic state was obtained as 
</p>

</p>
<p>7.1 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>7.3.4 Post-test Channel Measurements  
</p>
<p>7.3.4.1 Port Distance Measurements 
</p>
<p>After the testing was complete, the channels were visually inspected and 
</p>
<p>measured to obtain the as-produced geometry. The distance between each of the ports 
</p>
<p>was measured using two different sets of digital calipers while the channels were 
</p>
<p>rigidly clamped to prevent any bowing. The port distances measuring less than 152 
</p>
<p>mm were measured using Mitutoyo 500-160-30 (±0.02 mm) vernier calipers. For 
</p>
<p>distances greater than this value, a Mitutoyo 500-506-10 (±0.05 mm) caliper with 
</p>
<p>higher range was used. The procedure to obtain the center of the tube port was to 
</p>
<p>measure between each port on the outside of the instrumentation tubes and subtract 
</p>
<p>the value of the diameter of the tube. Figure 7.7 shows photographs to clarify how 
</p>
<p>these measurements were performed. All the resulting measurements are tabulated in 
</p>
<p>Table 7.5. The nomenclature of full, mid, and short distances was representative of 
</p>
<p>the distances between pressure transducers PT3-PT8, PT4-PT8, and PT5-PT6, 
</p>
<p>respectively. The maximum error determined between these measurements was 
</p>
<p>determined to be 0.043% for the full length and 0.1% for the mid length.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—144 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.7. Caliper measurements to determine exact distance between ports 
</p>
<p>for analysis. 
</p>
<p>7.3.4.2 Sectioning of the Channels 
</p>
<p>Following visual inspections and port distance measurements, the channel 
</p>
<p>samples were sectioned to determine their cross-sectional area, perimeter, surface, 
</p>
<p>and microstructure details of the surface. Three sections, perpendicular to the flow, 
</p>
<p>were taken (Figure 7.8): the first at the midpoint between PT3 and PT4, the second 
</p>
<p>offset from the center between PT5 and PT6, and the third at the midpoint between 
</p>
<p>PT7 and PT8. These sections, selected along the length of the channel, allow to 
</p>
<p>evaluate any axial length variations resulting from surface enhancement processing. 
</p>
<p>The samples were tagged according to the channel number, with -1, -2, and -3 suffixes 
</p>
<p>for traceability during microscopic imaging. Due to the time required for preparation 
</p>
<p>and measurement, a single section normal to the bottom surface and parallel to the 
</p>
<p>flow was taken at the axial midpoint of the channel for SEM imaging and surface 
</p>
<p>texture measurements.   </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—145 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.8. Location of the sectioning of the 600 mm length channels. The 
</p>
<p>direction of the flow is the same as the direction of the build. 
</p>
<p>7.3.4.3 Optical Images, SEM, and Surface Texture Characterization 
</p>
<p>The samples from the sections (-1, -2, and -3) were mounted, polished, and 
</p>
<p>etched using identical procedures as those described in Section 6.3.4.1. All optical 
</p>
<p>images were obtained using a Keyence VHX digital microscope. The images were 
</p>
<p>processed using the software Image J (Version 1.53e) [268]. The values obtained for 
</p>
<p>the area and the perimeter were averaged for the subsequent analysis and to obtain 
</p>
<p>their standard deviation. Additional measurements were taken on the round channels 
</p>
<p>to determine the diameter (Appendix Figure 12.4). The diameter of the round tubes 
</p>
<p>was measured in 10 circumferential locations and averaged.  
</p>
<p>The surface texture was obtained using a non-contact Keyence VR-5200 pattern 
</p>
<p>light projection profilometer. An 80x magnification was selected and an overlap of 
</p>
<p>20%. The scanned internal area of interest was approximately 38 mm long and 1.6 
</p>
<p>mm wide and reported according to ISO 25178-2:2021. The procedures were 
</p>
<p>identical to those described in section 6.3.4.3. An end-correction factor was applied 
</p>
<p>in addition to a form correction to remove any tilt and curvature. The unfiltered 
</p>
<p>primary surface texture (denoted as primary [P]) of the samples was evaluated in 
</p>
<p>addition to the filtered roughness (denoted as [R]) and waviness using a λc spatial 
</p>
<p>frequency cut-off filter of 0.8 mm per ISO 21920-3 guidelines. Directional profile, 
</p>
<p>roughness, and waviness parameters (i.e., Px, Rx, and Wx, respectively) were also 
</p>
<p>extracted from the data using a line along the center of the sample, equidistant from 
</p>
<p>the edges. This directional roughness and waviness are in the direction of the flow, 
</p>
<p>which also corresponds to the direction of the build. Several surface texture 
</p>
<p>parameters are evaluated to characterize the samples including Sa, Sq, Sz, Sk, Spk, 
</p>
<p>Spd, Pa, Ra, Rk, Wa, WP, and WPc. These are defined and discussed in detail in 
</p>
<p>Sections 4.3.4 and 6.3.4.3. 
</p>
<p> Additional surface texture characterization was obtained on selected 
</p>
<p>samples using a Keyence VKX-1100 laser scanning confocal microscope. The field 
</p>
<p>of view for this microscope was much smaller and required more scanning time and 
</p>
<p>an overlap of 5% was used in the X-direction. The scan area was approximately 1.45 
</p>
<p>mm width by 1.9 mm in length. These samples were scanned with a 20x 
</p>
<p>magnification lens with a height repeatability of 50 nm and width repeatability of 120 
</p>
<p>nm in laser confocal mode. The same texture parameters were extracted using 
</p>
<p>identical filtering. The main purpose of measuring these data was to obtain some </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—146 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>surface contour maps to provide visual comparisons to supplement the SEM and 
</p>
<p>optical images.  
</p>
<p>7.4 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7.4.1 Channel Geometry Characterization 
</p>
<p>The physical appearance and measurements resulting from the surface 
</p>
<p>enhancements and as-built channels were similar to those of the channel samples 
</p>
<p>presented in Chapter 6 [296]. The SEM images revealed identical artifacts from the 
</p>
<p>processing of the channels as those shown in section 6.4.1.2. The SEM images of all 
</p>
<p>the square channels are compiled in Figure 7.9. The as-built (AB) samples indicate 
</p>
<p>that adhered particles are randomly distributed on all the samples. The difference 
</p>
<p>between samples fabricated with fine powder (AB-53 and AB-54-L) and the other 
</p>
<p>AB samples fabricated with coarse powder is clearly visible. The fine powder covers 
</p>
<p>nearly the entire surface of the sample, while the coarse powder particles cover about 
</p>
<p>30% of the channel surface and are randomly distributed.  
</p>
<p>The SEM images for the AFM samples indicate that a high number of particles 
</p>
<p>can be identified also after processing. Several of the samples such as AFM-A1, 
</p>
<p>AFM-A2, AFM-B1, AFM-B2 appear to be unchanged from the AB baseline samples. 
</p>
<p>In the other AFM samples, many powder particles are still present, but the peaks of 
</p>
<p>the particles were removed. The remaining powder particles are still present on the 
</p>
<p>material or smeared. On a limited number of AFM samples such as AFM-A4, AFM-
</p>
<p>A6, AFM-B4, AFM-B5, material is observed to be removed from the central portion 
</p>
<p>or biased towards an edge. Powder particles are still present along the edges or on the 
</p>
<p>side. This indicates that the process may not be completely uniform and was not as 
</p>
<p>effective on the longer samples compared to the AFM samples reported in Section 
</p>
<p>6.4.1. 
</p>
<p>The CM samples, shown in Figure 7.9, indicate that most of the powder particles 
</p>
<p>were removed. Some random particles are still present and there are indications of 
</p>
<p>etching of the grains. Some indications of periodic waviness or texture can be 
</p>
<p>observed, particularly on the CM-A6, CM-B5, and CM-B6 samples. Crystals can also 
</p>
<p>be observed on several samples, similarly to what was observed for the short samples 
</p>
<p>discussed in Chapter 6. CMP was effective at removing almost all particles. There is 
</p>
<p>some evidence of macro texturing and significant etching of the grain boundaries for 
</p>
<p>CMP and CM samples.  
</p>
<p> Figure 7.10 displays the polished cross-sectional micrographs obtained from 
</p>
<p>the #2 mid-span samples (between PT05 and PT06). These are zoomed in to show 
</p>
<p>the inner wall and resulting surface from the enhancement processes. Figure 7.11 
</p>
<p>shows the chemically etched samples. The images reveal the entire wall thickness 
</p>
<p>and grain structure. The images of the cross-sections of the surfaces, each treated with 
</p>
<p>a different enhancement method, show similar trends as those belonging to the short 
</p>
<p>samples whose investigation reported in Chapter 6. The images of the AB samples 
</p>
<p>indicate high texturing around the perimeter. The coarse particles are apparent. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—147 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Texturizing can be noticed on the images of the samples built with fine powder (AB-
</p>
<p>53 and AB-54-L), but greatly reduced and similar to one that is also notable on the 
</p>
<p>surfaces of the channel samples treated Section 5.5. Samples AB-53, AB-57, AB-54-
</p>
<p>L were built without any adjacent channels, and support ribs along the midspan, 
</p>
<p>observed in Figure 7.11. This caused the samples to feature slightly round corners 
</p>
<p>compared to the corners of the other AB channels. Images show that most of the 
</p>
<p>AFM-processed samples exhibit some partially adhered powder. The images of CM-
</p>
<p>processed samples indicate rounding of the corners and material removal from the 
</p>
<p>entire inner perimeter. The cross-sectional area of the CM-processed samples is 
</p>
<p>noticeably larger. Sample CM-B2 is the only one for which some loose powder is 
</p>
<p>visible, and it may be a result of the sample preparation and mounting; the powder 
</p>
<p>did not appear to be attached.  
</p>
<p> Figure 7.11  illustrates that the CMP-processed samples now have smaller 
</p>
<p>radii compared to their previously more squared corners. The walls appear to be 
</p>
<p>visually smooth. The image of sample CMP-A4 shows a potential powder particle in 
</p>
<p>the upper left corner but it is not attached and likely related to polishing.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—148 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.9. SEM images of the inner surfaces of the 600 mm length square 
</p>
<p>channel samples.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—149 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>

</p>

<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>

</p>
<p>Chapter 7—150 
</p>
<p> 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>averaged. The full perimeter was measured for each sample, and it includes all 
</p>
<p>adhered powder and variations from the deposition process. The standard deviation 
</p>
<p>is also provided. The difference between the measured values of the area and 
</p>
<p>perimeter and the nominal (as-designed) values is also presented, together with the 
</p>

</p>
<p>determined from the measurements taken different calipers. The standard deviation 
</p>
<p>of the measurements features a maximum coefficient of variation of 0.043% for the 
</p>
<p>full-length measurements and 0.1% for the mid distance measurements.  
</p>

</p>
<p>The data of Table 7.5 are plotted in Figure 7.12 to elucidate their dependency on 
</p>
<p>the channel type and surface processing methods. The values of the cross-sectional 
</p>
<p>areas of the as-built channels are consistently smaller than the nominal design values 
</p>
<p>due to powder adherence and shrinkage during processing (Figure 7.12A). This was 
</p>
<p>expected and it was observed also for the similar data discussed in Chapters 5 and 6. 
</p>
<p>The value of cross-sectional area of the AFM-processed channels are similar and 
</p>
<p>close to the nominal design values. Despite the slurry flow rate and pressure were 
</p>
<p>varied, there appears to be a limit to the amount of material that can be removed. 
</p>
<p>Increasing the flow rate and pressure of processes that are abrasive may be necessary 
</p>
<p>to enlarge the channel area. CM-processed channels cause significantly larger values 
</p>
<p>of the cross-sectional area compared to the as-built value and the nominal design 
</p>
<p>value, as previously observed. The CMP samples indicate consistent areas and 
</p>
<p>perimeters despite processing parameter variations. 
</p>
<p>Figure 7.12C reports the values of measured perimeters depending on the post-
</p>
<p>processing treatment. As expected, the perimeter value of as-built samples is higher 
</p>
<p>than the design value (which is an ideal value formed by perfectly straight segments), 
</p>
<p>which is 10.16 mm for most samples. The AB-54-L sample was designed with a 
</p>
<p>nominal perimeter of 11.79 mm. The perimeter value of as-built round samples (9.017 
</p>
<p>mm) similarly deviate from the nominal design values. AFM samples feature a higher 
</p>
<p>perimeter value, similar to the value of as-built samples, due to the presence of 
</p>
<p>residual powder particles. The larger value of the area of CM samples results from 
</p>
<p>the higher amount of removed material, which effectively eliminates adhered powder 
</p>
<p>and even some of the underlying surface. CMP processing effectively removes 
</p>
<p>particles while keeping to a minimum material removal, allowing for more precisely 
</p>
<p>controlling the value of the area and perimeter. The data sets of Figure 7.12C indicate 
</p>
<p>that the CMP process can effectively reduce and planarize the perimeter with minimal 
</p>
<p>area increase. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—153 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.12. Measurements characterizing the inner surface of channels. A) 
</p>
<p>Cross-sectional area, B) Area Coefficient of Variation, C) Perimeter, D) 
</p>
<p>Perimeter Coefficient of Variation. [FP = Fine Powder].  
</p>
<p>Figure 7.12B shows values of the coefficient of variation (CV) for the area of 
</p>
<p>each channel type, while Figure 7.12D displays the value of CV for the perimeter. 
</p>
<p>The CV, calculated as the standard deviation divided by the mean, indicates the 
</p>
<p>variance between cross-sections along the length of the channel. The areas and 
</p>
<p>perimeters are based on the average of three cross-sections, with the means presented 
</p>
<p>in Figure 7.12A and Figure 7.12C, respectively. This CV is a merit parameter 
</p>
<p>measuring the capability of surface enhancement processes in producing consistent 
</p>
<p>uniform area and perimeter along the channel length. A high CV suggests significant 
</p>
<p>variation.  
</p>
<p>Regardless of the surface enhancement process, the value of CV for the area 
</p>
<p>along the channel lengths remains within 2.5%. This variation accounts for both the 
</p>
<p>effects of surface processing and measurement error, indicating consistent processing 
</p>
<p>conditions. The area of the AFM samples vary by 2.2% along their length, despite 
</p>
<p>having nearly identical absolute cross-sectional areas. 
</p>
<p>The perimeter CV range is higher than the cross-sectional area CV range, as 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 7.12D. No clear trends link perimeter CV to area CV; variations 
</p>
<p>appear as random error (Refer to Appendix Figure 12.1). As-built samples with 
</p>
<p>randomly adhered powder show consistent area and perimeter variation along the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—154 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>length, in line with the results from section 6.5. AFM processed samples exhibit the 
</p>
<p>highest CV due to processing inconsistencies, with some powder remaining adhered 
</p>
<p>to the walls while other areas are cleared, leading to a higher perimeter variation 
</p>
<p>(5.6%). CM samples demonstrate consistent perimeter measurements along the 
</p>
<p>channel length, with a CV within 3.3%. The CMP process shows excellent 
</p>
<p>consistency, maintaining perimeter variations within 2.2% along the channel length. 
</p>
<p>7.4.2 Surface Texture Characterization 
</p>
<p>All samples were scanned to obtain quantitative information on the surface 
</p>
<p>texture at the mid-point of the channels as indicated in Figure 7.8. The scans provided 
</p>
<p>the amplitude of the primary and roughness areal texture, spatial, functional, hybrid, 
</p>
<p>and material ratio parameters. Additionally, the 2D profile, roughness, and waviness 
</p>
<p>parameters were obtained along the centerline of the channel. The primary [P] and 
</p>
<p>roughness [R] filtering to obtain these parameters is discussed in Section 7.3.4.3 and 
</p>
<p>6.3.4.3. Table 7.6 lists a summary of the selected parameters.  
</p>
<p>The 95% confidence interval (C.I.) of the values was obtained by repeating four 
</p>
<p>times the measurements characterizing a single as-built sample. Reporting values 
</p>
<p>related to the as-built sample introduces bias in the confidence interval for parameters 
</p>
<p>associated with single-point high peaks and due to the powder particles, such as Sp. 
</p>
<p>The confidence interval characterizing samples with enhanced surface texture is 
</p>
<p>likely much higher (&gt;95%). Additionally, directional measurements (Wa, Ra, Pa) are 
</p>
<p>affected by much larger deviation, as slight changes in line placement can result in 
</p>
<p>significant variations of peak height or valley lengths. In contrast, areal 
</p>
<p>measurements are affected by lower deviation since all texture artifacts of the 
</p>
<p>measured area comprise the calculated value (i.e, Sa, Sq, Sp, etc). As noted, the Sp 
</p>
<p>value is based on a single peak value across the entire surface therefore bound to be 
</p>
<p>biased towards higher values and a higher standard deviation. This issue underscores 
</p>
<p>the importance of using a merit parameter such as reduced peak height (Spk) and core 
</p>
<p>roughness (Sk). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>

</p>
<p>filtered roughness texture.

<p>Samples treated with each of the post-processing techniques were selected 
</p>
<p>among all those available and were mapped on a VK-1100 microscope. Figure 7.13 
</p>
<p>displays the 3D height maps overlayed on the laser scanned and optical images. The 
</p>
<p>scan windows measured ~1935 µm in the X direction and ~1450 µm in the Y-
</p>
<p>direction. The textures are consistent with those of the cross-section SEM images of 
</p>
<p>the samples discussed in Chapter 6. The SEM images (Figure 7.9) do not adequately 
</p>
<p>provide depth data due to the limited depth of field, therefore the overlay optical and 
</p>
<p>laser scan images provide additional information (Figure 7.13). The images of the 
</p>
<p>square and round as-built samples indicate a random distribution of adhered powder 
</p>
<p>particles and appear visually similar. This suggests that images of these samples, </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—156 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>being built with coarse powder, can be used as an appropriate reference to determine 
</p>
<p>the effective diameter of the square and of the round channels.  
</p>
<p>The images of AFM samples show higher peaks along with a few powder 
</p>
<p>particles on the surface. Some of the powder particles are reduced or smeared, as 
</p>
<p>indicated by the elongated peaks along the flow direction (X). The images of CM 
</p>
<p>samples display lower peak heights across the surface, but some pitting can be 
</p>
<p>observed (cool colors). This pitting could be attack of the surface from the chemical 
</p>
<p>or pull-out of powder particles from the surface. A jagged surface is apparent in the 
</p>
<p>optical images and height mapping overlay. The images of the selected CMP sample 
</p>
<p>also reveals reduced peak heights with some preferential direction for material 
</p>
<p>removal. Increased surface height in the scanned window can be observed, and some 
</p>
<p>material is selectively attached to the grain boundaries.  
</p>

<p>7.4.3 Discharge Coefficient 
</p>
<p>The discharge coefficient was calculated according to its definition, namely  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝐶𝑑 =
</p>
<p>�̇�
</p>
<p>𝜌�̇�
=
</p>
<p>�̇�
</p>
<p>𝐴√2𝜌∆𝑃
 7.4 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where ṁ is mass flow rate in kg/sec, A is area in mm2, ρ is density of water in 
</p>
<p>grams/cm3, and ΔP is the differential pressure between PT08–PT03 in bar. The 
</p>
<p>discharge coefficient was evaluated for each channel sample at each test pressure. 
</p>
<p>The area is based on the average of three measurements (shown in Figure 7.8) and 
</p>
<p>listed in Table 7.5.  
</p>
<p>Figure 7.15 shows the functional relationship between Cd and the cross-sectional 
</p>
<p>area, perimeter and the average primary texture. The trends are nearly identical to 
</p>
<p>those that can be observed in Figure 6.9. The estimated Cd depends on the tests due 
</p>
<p>to the significantly longer length of the channels compared to those treated in Section 
</p>
<p>6.5. The effect of surface enhancement processes on flow resistance is similar, with 
</p>
<p>CM and CMP causing the lowest resistance (highest Cd). The Cd increases with the 
</p>
<p>cross-sectional area, as expected. The large square as-built channel (AB-54-L) 
</p>
<p>features an area that is nearly identical to that of channels CM-B3 and CM-B4 and its 
</p>
<p>average Cd is 38% lower. The Cd of the large as-built AB-54-L channel is higher </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—158 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>therefore the flow resistance is lower than that of the as-built channels featuring 
</p>
<p>smaller cross-sectional area, as expected. A slight difference in flow resistance can 
</p>
<p>be observed between AB channels with the larger and smaller cross-sectional area, 
</p>
<p>which suggest that other factors, such as the texture, have a higher influence on the 
</p>
<p>flow resistance.  
</p>
<p>Figure 7.15 also shows that the value of Cd increases with decreasing perimeter 
</p>
<p>lengths down to Cd=0.3. For a range of perimeter values, it remains constant and 
</p>
<p>equal to approximately 0.3, then increases again starting from Cd=0.4. This is 
</p>
<p>expected since the powder adherence and surface undulations cause the value of the 
</p>
<p>perimeter to increase at lower Cd values. The decrease of the perimeter value for Cd 
</p>
<p>in the range from 0.2 to 0.3 is due to the full removal or partial removal of powder 
</p>
<p>particles with sizes lower than the average particle size (73 µm). The larger perimeter 
</p>
<p>values associated with values of Cd higher than 0.4 is mostly due to the increase in 
</p>
<p>the channel cross-sectional area resulting from material removal. 
</p>
<p>Figure 7.15C displays how the flow resistance decreases exponentially with 
</p>
<p>decreasing values of the average areal texture (primary), Sa.  The Sa strongly 
</p>
<p>decreases down to ~Cd=0.32, while for larger values of Cd it decreases very slightly 
</p>
<p>for values of from Cd=0.32 up to 0.5.  
 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.15. Relationship of the estimated Cd with A) Measured cross-sectional 
</p>
<p>area, B) Measured perimeter, and C) Measured average primary texture. The 
</p>
<p>color of the symbols is associated to the surface enhancement process as listed 
</p>
<p>in the legend. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—159 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>In Chapter 6 it is discussed that other surface texture parameters deemed 
</p>
<p>necessary for flow characterization include the core texture depth (Sk) and reduced 
</p>
<p>peak height (Spk). These values allow to better to discern the average distance 
</p>
<p>between peaks and valleys, thus filtering out the maximum heights of peaks and 
</p>
<p>valleys. Figure 7.16 shows how Sk and Spk vary with Cd. The functional dependence 
</p>
<p>is identical to that of the data visualized in Figure 6.10: as the core peak-to-valley 
</p>
<p>depth reduces, the Cd increases. The value of Sk becomes approximately constant for 
</p>
<p>heights of peaks or valleys of ~50 µm, and it weakly decreases for values of Cd up to 
</p>
<p>0.5. The trend of Spk is similar, although not as pronounced. Overall Sk and Spk 
</p>
<p>decrease exponentially with Cd.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.16. Sk and Spk as a function of Cd. The color of the symbols is 
</p>
<p>associated to the surface enhancement process as listed in the legend. 
</p>
<p>It can be concluded, in analogy with the considerations reported in Section 6.6, 
</p>
<p>that flow resistance depends for the most from roughness-related peaks and their 
</p>
<p>density, as well as from the planarization of waviness and their density. Figure 7.17 
</p>
<p>presents the summary of the relations between Cd and Spk, Spd, Wp, and WPc and 
</p>
<p>the cross-sectional area. These trends are similar to those reported in Figure 6.12 for 
</p>
<p>shorter channels. Given that material is removed from the surface due to the 
</p>
<p>enhancement treatment, the resulting channel cross-sectional area is larger and Spk 
</p>
<p>is lower. Compared to the Spk of the as-built channels (AB-xx), the Spk of channels 
</p>
<p>whose surfaces are treated is lower by 79%, and the density of these peaks (Spd) is 
</p>
<p>lower by 74%. Similar results hold for waviness, with Wp and WPc being lower by 
</p>
<p>76%. The calculated Reynolds number (discussed in section 7.4.4) indicates that the </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—160 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>channels operate in the turbulent regime. Higher micro peaks, macro waviness, and 
</p>
<p>their density distribution along the channel length increase boundary layer turbulence 
</p>
<p>and fluid dynamic losses. Effective removal of these features is crucial for reducing 
</p>
<p>flow resistance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.17. Flow resistance dependence for roughness and waviness of all the 
</p>
<p>tested channels: A) Spk, B) Spd density, C) Wp, and D) WPc density.  
</p>
<p>To allow to better estimate the amount of material removal required to obtain a 
</p>
<p>specified flow resistance, Figure 7.18 provides the visualization of the relationship 
</p>
<p>between material removal (MR) per side and Sk, Spk, and Wp. To obtain Figure 6.11, 
</p>
<p>MR was measured at 20 locations for each channel. MR per side is calculated based 
</p>
<p>on the simplifying assumption that 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
 𝑀𝑅 =
</p>
<p>√𝐴Ch  −  √𝐴AB
</p>
<p>2
 
</p>
<p>7.5 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where ACh is the average cross-sectional area of the channel calculated measured 
</p>
<p>at three locations and AAB is the value of the average as-built area. The results 
</p>
<p>discussed in Section 6.5 show that the average powder size correlated to the material 
</p>
<p>removal necessary to effectively increase the Cd. The results in Figure 7.18 suggest 
</p>
<p>that removing powder of the minimum size (diameter of 45 µm) is required to reduce 
</p>
<p>the flow resistance by approximately 40%, but to reduce flow resistance by &gt;75% the 
</p>
<p>powder of maximum size (105 µm) must be removed. The removal of large-size 
</p>
<p>powder eliminates all the adhered powder particles and decreases flow friction within 
</p>
<p>the boundary layer.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—161 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.18. Relationship between material removal per side based on: A) Core 
</p>
<p>Depth, Sk, B) Reduced Peak Height, Spk, and C) Waviness peak height, Wp.  
</p>
<p>  
</p>
<p>7.4.4 Differential Pressure and Friction Factor 
</p>
<p>In order to calculate the Colebrook-White friction factor using existing methods, 
</p>
<p>Reynolds number, pressure difference and hydraulic diameter (Dh) [298] must be 
</p>
<p>determined. According to conventional methods, all these calculations assume a 
</p>
<p>circular tube and methods to convert these quantitates for the case of non-circular 
</p>
<p>geometry are documented in several publications [299–301]. The conventional 
</p>
<p>definition of hydraulic diameter for a square channel is  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝐷ℎ =
</p>
<p>4𝐴
</p>
<p>𝑃
 
</p>
<p>7.6 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—162 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where A is the area in mm2 and P is the wetted perimeter in mm. These values 
</p>
<p>were measured as part of the detailed characterization of the channel cross sectional 
</p>
<p>areas and perimeters documented in Section 7.4.1. The hydraulic diameter was 
</p>
<p>calculated according to equation 7.6 for all channels and values are reported as black 
</p>
<p>triangles on the plot of Figure 7.19. This figure shows that values of Dh calculated 
</p>
<p>with equation 7.6 depart significantly from the expected linear relation between area 
</p>
<p>and perimeter for samples featuring small values of the area and comparatively large 
</p>
<p>value of perimeter. The channels with as-built surfaces feature a measured perimeter 
</p>
<p>approximately 30% higher than that of a nominally designed channel with smooth 
</p>
<p>walls. The plot of Figure 7.19 shows that the deviation from linearity is significant 
</p>
<p>for the points referring to as-built and AFM channels with cross-sectional areas less 
</p>
<p>than 6.8 mm2. In those cases, the value of the perimeter is much larger than the 
</p>
<p>nominal value due to the surface texture resulting from the adhered powder and 
</p>
<p>waviness, yielding a value of the Dh which deviates from the one given by the linear 
</p>
<p>trend by around 18%.  
</p>
<p>Other issues affect the deviation from the linear dependence of Dh. First, the 
</p>
<p>perimeter exhibits a higher coefficient of variation (CV) compared to the CV of the 
</p>
<p>cross-sectional area (see Figure 7.12), introducing additional uncertainty in the Dh  
</p>
<p>values. Second, using Dh with large perimeter values could further bias the estimation 
</p>
<p>of the friction factor. Since sand grain roughness (relating to surface texture in section 
</p>
<p>7.4.5) is already incorporated into the friction factor correlation, accounting for the 
</p>
<p>increased perimeter due to high roughness would effectively result in "double 
</p>
<p>bookkeeping" of the texture values. 
</p>
<p> Given the unsatisfactory ability of the conventional definition of hydraulic 
</p>
<p>diameter of correlating the cross-sectional area and the perimeter of the additively 
</p>
<p>manufactured channels, an alternative definition Dh evaluated for a square cross-
</p>
<p>section channel is 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝐷ℎ1 = √𝐴meas 
</p>
<p>7.7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where Ameas is the measured cross-sectional area, whose values for the considered 
</p>
<p>channels are listed in Table 7.5. The justification for this definition is that the Dh for 
</p>
<p>a square channel is simply the length of a side [289], as previously mentioned. Values 
</p>
<p>of Dh1 are shown as white circles in Figure 7.19 together with their correlating 
</p>
<p>function displayed as a dotted line. The values of Dh, associated with an area larger 
</p>
<p>than 6.6 mm2, form a linear function and the values of Dh1 appear to be offset from 
</p>
<p>this. This observation leads to a definition of hydraulic diameter valid for square 
</p>
<p>channels that includes a texture attribute, namely 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝐷ℎ2 = √𝐴𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠 − 2𝑆𝑘 
</p>
<p>7.8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>where Sk is the core texture height. This definition of hydraulic diameter 
</p>
<p>incorporating a correction term twice the surface texture attribute is similar to 
</p>
<p>definitions for determining a representative cross-sectional area in AM mechanical </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—163 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>testing [302,303] and also adopted in constricted flow theory presented by Taylor et 
</p>
<p>al. [288,304]. The introduction of a texture correction term eliminates the bias 
</p>
<p>resulting from the case of a highly texture perimeter: channels with high texturing 
</p>
<p>(those with a cross-sectional area lower than 6.6 mm2) feature values of Dh that are 
</p>
<p>17% lower, with a value of the cross-sectional area which is 4.5% lower.  For this 
</p>
<p>reason, the definition of hydraulic diameter provided by Equation 7.8 is adopted for 
</p>
<p>all the calculations treated in this chapter. The ratio of length (PT08-PT03) to 
</p>
<p>hydraulic diameter is approximately 179:1.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>  
Figure 7.19. Hydraulic Diameter based on various definitions. Dh2 is defined by 
</p>
<p>equation 7.6, Dh1 is defined by equation 7.7, and Dh is defined by equation 7.8. 
</p>
<p>The Dh2 symbols are colored according to the value of the Cd.   
</p>
<p>After an appropriate hydraulic diameter is calculated for the square channels, the 
</p>
<p>Reynolds number is determined. The Reynolds number is defined as 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝑅𝑒 =
</p>
<p>𝐷h2𝑣𝜌
</p>
<p>𝜇
, 7.9 
</p>
<p>  
</p>
<p>where Dh2 is calculated according to equation 7.8, 𝑣 is computed from equation 
</p>
<p>7.3, ρ is the fluid density, and 𝜇 is defined according to equation 7.1.  
</p>
<p>The values of Re for all test runs range from 17,011 to 122,818, thus the flow is 
</p>
<p>fully turbulent. Figure 7.20 provides a plot of the values of the Reynolds number as </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—164 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>a function of the mass flow rate with the symbols colored according to the velocity 
</p>
<p>range. Values referring to samples treated with surface enhancements (polished) are 
</p>
<p>plotted along with those referring to the as-built samples obtained with coarse powder 
</p>
<p>and fine powder.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.20. Reynolds number as a function of mass flow rate obtained from 
</p>
<p>measurements on enhanced and as-built channels. The data is colored by 
</p>
<p>velocity for samples processed with surface enhancements (polished) and as-
</p>
<p>built samples. The Reynolds number is calculated with the hydraulic diameter 
</p>
<p>defined by Equation 7.6.  
</p>

</p>

<p>The same set of data used to obtain Figure 7.21 was used to obtain Figure 7.22, 
</p>
<p>but in this plot the relation between pressure drop and mass flow rate is also displayed 
</p>
<p>as a function of core surface texture, average directional waviness, and average 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

<p>The validity of these friction factor definitions were experimentally evaluated 
</p>
<p>with a general focus on round pipes [310,311]. One of the objectives of the 
</p>
<p>experiments presented here is to characterize and determine an explicit friction factor 
</p>

</p>

<p>All the expressions for the friction factor were compared regarding predictions
