<p>In equation 7.17, ∆𝑃 is the pressure difference between two measuring ports, 𝑣 
</p>
<p>is given by Equation 7.3, ρ is the fluid density, gc is the gravitational constant (9.81 
</p>
<p>m/sec2), and L is the length between the locations of the considered ports. Figure 7.23 
</p>
<p>depicts how the Darcy friction factor, determined by correlating all the measured the 
</p>
<p>differential pressure data, varies with the mass flow rate. As expected, for low values 
</p>
<p>of the mass flow rate fD increases for a given of mass flow rate. fD is larger for the AB 
</p>
<p>channels and the AFM channels, for which a high density of powder particles are 
</p>
<p>present on the surface. Figure 7.23 shows the constant mass flow rate tests conducted 
</p>
<p>at 0.069, 0.095, and 0.13 kg/s, along with data sets where the flow rate is maximized. 
</p>
<p>The friction factor remains relatively constant regardless of the flow rate. The values 
</p>
<p>of fD  for selected channels is represented by a horizontal line, indicating minimal 
</p>
<p>variation in friction factor with changes in flow rate (refer to Appendix, Figure 12.3). 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—169 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.23. Darcy friction factor as a function of mass flow rate. The dotted 
</p>
<p>horizontal lines represent the same channel across the six different test runs. 
</p>
<p>The points are colored according to the measured value of the pressure 
</p>
<p>difference between pressure ports. 
</p>
<p>The values of the Darcy friction factor obtained by correlating experimental 
</p>
<p>results was evaluated against the values of friction factor calculated with the 
</p>
<p>expressions based on the surface texture (Re, KS, and Dh2) in relation to the discharge 
</p>
<p>coefficient (Figure 7.24). These data are derived using the full-length channel value 
</p>
<p>of the differential pressure (PT08-PT03) for each test condition. None of the friction 
</p>
<p>factor correlations obtained with Equations 7.10 through 7.16 match the values of 
</p>
<p>Darcy friction factor derived from measurements. The values obtained with 
</p>
<p>Equations 7.10 to 7.16 show similar trends across the range of Cd with a maximum 
</p>
<p>deviation between predictions of 10%. The value calculated with the Colebrook-
</p>
<p>White correlation falls within the median range of the values calculated with the other 
</p>
<p>correlations. The plot also shows that there is discrepancy between values calculated 
</p>
<p>with all the friction factor correlations and the Darcy friction factor, with the value 
</p>
<p>calculated with surface-based friction factor correlations being lower for values of 
</p>
<p>Cd &lt; 0.27 and higher for Cd  &gt; 0.3. Based on these considerations, the Moody-derived 
</p>
<p>friction factor expression (fM) was selected for further analysis. At lower flow 
</p>
<p>resistances, values calculated with fM feature the least variance from those calculated 
</p>
<p>with the fD expression.  At higher flow resistances, values calculated with the fM </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—170 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>correlation are close to the mean of values computed with all other friction factor 
</p>
<p>expressions.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.24. Comparison of the friction factor determined experimentally from 
</p>
<p>differential pressure measurements along with friction factors calculated from 
</p>
<p>various correlations (Equations 7.10–7.16). 
</p>
<p>The friction factor correlations 7.10 to 7.16 depend on the surface roughness and 
</p>
<p>channel hydraulic diameter (KS, Dh2) along with Re. KS is not representative of 
</p>
<p>surface roughness, or of surface texture, which can be directly measured. A possible 
</p>
<p>assumption for the evaluation of surface roughness is that it is defined as the average 
</p>
<p>directional roughness, Ra, since this is the most commonly measured. However, this 
</p>
<p>parameter is insufficient to fully characterize texture, and other surface texture 
</p>
<p>attributes are discussed in Section 7.4.5. Adams et al. [312] proposed a constant value 
</p>
<p>of 5.863 for the KS/Ra ratio. Similarly, Forster recommended a value of 7 for 𝐾𝑠/Ra 
</p>
<p>[313], while Koch and Smith suggested a value of 6 [314]. The value of 5.863 was 
</p>
<p>initially used to convert Ra to a KS for the data presented in Figure 7.24. However, 
</p>
<p>other authors have argued that a single KS parameter is insufficient [305,315]. This 
</p>
<p>deficiency is evident in Figure 7.24 where variations in surface texture led to changes 
</p>
<p>in flow resistance and deviations in the friction factor correlations compared to the fD 
</p>
<p>correlation. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—171 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The correct characterization of the roughness of a surface is crucial for 
</p>
<p>determining differential pressure along fully turbulent flow through a channel 
</p>
<p>because the viscous sublayer and roughness sublayer are disturbed and deflected by 
</p>
<p>the surface texture. The height and spacing of the roughness and waviness alters the 
</p>
<p>contribution to the drag and pressure losses in addition go thickening the boundary 
</p>
<p>layer further constricting the flow. Figure 7.25 shows that similar values of 
</p>
<p>differential pressure may result from largely different Reynolds numbers and velocity 
</p>
<p>values. This observation is consistent with that of Kadivar et al. [305] that the 
</p>
<p>pressure drop is unaffected by fluid viscosity and velocity, if the peaks of the surface 
</p>
<p>texture penetrate the roughness sublayer. The height of the roughness sublayer can 
</p>
<p>be as much as 2 to 5 times the diameter of the sand grain roughness, KS [316]. As 
</p>
<p>presented in Chapter 6, the surface texture that results in the KS is complex and 
</p>
<p>comprises both micro roughness and macro waviness, therefore these surfaces must 
</p>
<p>be fully characterized, and new correlations determined. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.25. Reynolds number as a function of the differential pressure. The 
</p>
<p>data points are colored by the mean velocity. 
</p>
<p>7.4.5 Sand Grain Roughness 
</p>
<p>The definition of sand grain roughness is related to the experiments of Darcy and 
</p>
<p>Nikuradse: sand was bonded to internal pipes using an adhesive or lacquer in order </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—172 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>to obtain a prescribed level of internal surface roughness [284,285]. The exact cross-
</p>
<p>sectional surface texture from these experiments is not known, but they are commonly 
</p>
<p>represented as shown in Figure 7.26 (left). The surface roughness was obtained by 
</p>
<p>measuring the diameter of a sand grain or evaluated by means of contact-type 
</p>
<p>profilometers. Capillary action between the lacquer and sand grains applied to the 
</p>
<p>pipe surface likely created small radii and captured the sand grains, so using the sand 
</p>
<p>grain diameter is also an incorrect hypothesis. While sand grain roughness may 
</p>
<p>appear similar to the roughness of a surface resulting from additive manufacturing, 
</p>
<p>as in the case of a surface generated with the LP-DED process with its adhered 
</p>
<p>powder particles, experimental data sets [305] indicate that the two surfaces are quite 
</p>
<p>different, as evident from the images of Figure 7.26 (right). The microscopic shape 
</p>
<p>of the surface resulting from the LP-DED process is made of both smooth and sharp 
</p>
<p>irregularities due to waviness and roughness, and a combination of both. The 
</p>
<p>resulting flow from these irregularities can result in trapped eddies at sharp transitions 
</p>
<p>or pockets, vortex shedding, and crests that protrude into the boundary layer [317].  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.26. Left: schematic representation of sand grains attached to the pipe 
</p>
<p>surface with and without glueing lacquer as in the experiments of Darcy and 
</p>
<p>Nikuradse [284,285]. Microscopic image of the surface texture resulting from 
</p>
<p>LP-DED manufacturing, showing both roughness and waviness. 
</p>
<p>The reason for the deviation between values of the friction factor computed with 
</p>
<p>predictive equations and those based on measured differential pressures is further 
</p>
<p>elucidated with the help of Figure 7.27, indicating the percent deviation in the friction 
</p>
<p>factor (Δf) at three fixed flow rates. As the differential pressure increases due to the 
</p>
<p>velocity rise from the increased mass flow, the experimental friction factor remains 
</p>
<p>nearly constant across each channel. The maximum coefficient of variation (CV) of 
</p>
<p>fD at the three fixed flow rates is 5.8%. The average CV across all channel samples is 
</p>
<p>1.6%, with a median CV of 1.1%. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—173 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
 
</p>
<p>Figure 7.27. Percent deviation between the experimentally determined fD and 
</p>
<p>explicit fM plotted with differential pressure. The points indicate the fixed mass 
</p>
<p>flow rates when the venturi was installed. 
</p>
<p> To determine the appropriate conversion from the measured surface texture 
</p>
<p>to sand grain roughness, an equation is solved iteratively for the sand grain roughness 
</p>
<p>factor (KF) as shown in Equation 7.18. This method uses the fD from Equation 7.17, 
</p>
<p>which is based on the measured pressure drop, as a known variable.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
𝑓D ≈ 𝑓M1 = 1.14 + 2𝑙𝑜𝑔10 (
</p>
<p>𝐷h2
</p>
<p>𝑆𝑇 ∙ 𝐾F
</p>
<p>)
−2
</p>
<p> 
7.18 
</p>
<p>  
</p>
<p>where ST is the measured surface texture/roughness (i.e., Ra, Pp, Sa, Sk), and KF 
</p>
<p>is the sand grain roughness factor. An iterative optimization was run for each data set 
</p>
<p>that modified the KF until the difference between fM1, the new friction factor, and fD 
</p>
<p>was within 0.1%. The ST ∙ 𝐾F value is then equal to KS.  
</p>
<p> To incorporate 𝐾𝑠 into friction factor correlations, Stimpson suggested using 
</p>
<p>a parameter to normalize the value of the sand grain roughness with the hydraulic 
</p>
<p>diameter (Ks/Dh) which allows for a comparison to the mean directional roughness 
</p>
<p>(Ra/Dh) [108]. However, Stimpson only considered L-PBF as-built channels with 
</p>
<p>rough texture variations, which did not include any partially or fully polished channel </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—174 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>data. Wildgoose used a similar method to normalize additional data sets using Ks/Dh, 
</p>
<p>but again with as-built L-PBF samples and no polishing [300]. Wildgoose omitted 
</p>
<p>Ra/Dh values below 0.007, which is the ratio of interest for this current experiment 
</p>
<p>using surface enhancements. Stimpson and Wildgoose employed micro-CT scanning 
</p>
<p>to obtain the roughness parameters. While micro-CT could measure features as small 
</p>
<p>as 30 µm, the uncertainty estimated for Ra was 4 µm.   
</p>
<p> Figure 7.28 presents the experimental data for various surface enhancement 
</p>
<p>methods, showing the sand grain roughness (KS/Dh2) and surface roughness/texture 
</p>
<p>(ST/Dh2) normalized with the hydraulic diameter. In each plot, KS/Dh2 remains 
</p>
<p>constant, as it is directly calculated to equate fM1 with fD in Equation 7.18. The 
</p>
<p>normalized surface texture measurements (Ra/Dh2, Pp/Dh2, Sa/Dh2, Sk/Dh2) vary, 
</p>
<p>filtering different surface features like roughness, waviness, and extreme peaks and 
</p>
<p>valleys. Optimization based on mean filtered roughness (Ra) shows the least error 
</p>
<p>with a linear fit, which is unexpected given its poor correlation to flow resistance as 
</p>
<p>described in Section 6.5. The primary profile (Pp), a directional measurement 
</p>
<p>including full texture, shows some linearity, but the slope plateaus at Pp/Dh2 of 0.05. 
</p>
<p>The average areal texture (Sa) and core texture depth (Sk) exhibit some linearity but 
</p>
<p>with greater scatter.  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—175 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.28. Sand grain roughness (KS/Dh2) as a function of the surface 
</p>
<p>roughness/texture parameters (ST/Dh2) normalized by hydraulic diameter. The 
</p>
<p>points are colored according to the measured roughness or texture values 
</p>
<p>indicated for Ra, Pp, Sa, and Sk. 
</p>
<p> The correct prediction of the friction factor is essential for the design of heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger channels; therefore the value of KS must be determined, which in turn 
</p>
<p>requires the value of KF. Figure 7.29 displays a plot of the values of KF as a function 
</p>
<p>of the measured surface texture/roughness parameters. Regardless of the surface 
</p>
<p>texture/roughness parameter, the absolute KS value can be computed with the value 
</p>
<p>of fD. Based on the various surface texture/roughness parameters the range of KS is 
</p>
<p>4.8 to 167 ±0.26 µm, corresponding to the enhanced samples and as-built, 
</p>
<p>respectively. Ra and Sa, defined as average values, fail to capture the peak-to-valley 
</p>
<p>range of the entire surface profile, leading to a broader range for KF. Averaging all 
</p>
<p>Ra values, as shown in Figure 7.29, yields a KF value of 4.8. In comparison, the range 
</p>
<p>suggested in section 7.4.4 is 5.863 to 7. Other researchers suggested a KF of 0.2 to 
</p>
<p>0.4 for ground or machined surfaces [318], which aligns with Ra in the 3 to 5 µm </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—176 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>range. Based on these observations and as made evident by Figure 7.29, the value of 
</p>
<p>KF is directly related to the roughness/texture and it cannot be assumed as a single 
</p>
<p>constant.  
</p>
<p>The value of KF for the Pp is much lower and its average value is 0.66. This is 
</p>
<p>expected since it represents all profile features including roughness and waviness and 
</p>
<p>is unfiltered. As Pp approaches as a value of 110 µm, indicative of as-built surfaces, 
</p>
<p>the KF approaches a value of 1. This observation provides some evidence that Pp 
</p>
<p>could be used as a first-attempt approximation of the value of KS when the surface is 
</p>
<p>left in the as-built condition. The KF related to Sa is lower than for Ra because the 
</p>
<p>measured texture across the entire surface, with higher peaks and valleys, is captured. 
</p>
<p>The average value of KF is 0.84 related to Sk and indicates a linear trend with the 
</p>
<p>absolute Sk. As-built roughness channels display a plateau in both Sa and Sk, but the 
</p>
<p>friction factor remains relatively unchanged, suggesting the peak roughness sublayer 
</p>
<p>may have been reached. 
</p>
<p>  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—177 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.29. Values of sand grain roughness factor based on Equation 7.18 as a 
</p>
<p>function of the measured surface texture/roughness values. The data sets are 
</p>
<p>colored according to the value of  fD.  
</p>
<p> Table 7.7 provides the linear equations in terms of the KS/Dh2 ratio for the 
</p>
<p>computation of the texture parameters resulting from the fitting of the experimental 
</p>
<p>data. A linear fit of the KS/Dh2 to the Ra/Dh2 parameter indicates a coefficient of 
</p>
<p>determination of 95%. The primary profile (Pp) texture parameter revealed the 
</p>
<p>highest proportional variance at 85.7%. The coefficient of determination for the Sa 
</p>
<p>texture parameter was 88.2% and Sk at 90.1%.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>

</div>

</p>

</p>
