<p>7.4.6 Friction Factor Correlation and Mechanism 
</p>
<p>This section summarizes the data used to calculate the friction factor based on a 
</p>
<p>new correlation (Equation 7.11 and Table 7.7) and hydraulic diameter expression 
</p>
<p>(Equation 7.8). The surface profile characteristics and distinct texture types are 
</p>
<p>presented in relation to their correlation with the experimental friction factor. 
</p>
<p>Using the linear fit of Ra from Table 7.7, a correlation for fM was developed and 
</p>
<p>results are presented in Figure 7.30. The values calculated with the new correlation 
</p>
<p>(reported as circles), show improved accuracy by over 20% compared to the prior 
</p>
<p>correlation (shown by triangles), which used a constant value of KS. This new 
</p>
<p>correlation also expands the applicable range of surface textures, as previous studies 
</p>
<p>only considered fully rough, as-built tubes or channels. The new correlation becomes 
</p>
<p>less accurate when the friction factor falls below 0.03, as surface roughness and 
</p>
<p>waviness decrease. At this transition, calculating Dh (indicated by squares on Figure 
</p>
<p>7.30) using the traditional definition of hydraulic diameter (4A/P) may be more 
</p>
<p>appropriate with the new linear fit model for KS/Dh2. Several datasets at f &lt;0.3, not 
</p>
<p>shown in Figure 7.30, produced negative values for KS  when using Dh2. Switching 
</p>
<p>to Dh corrects these negative values, but the predicted f may still be lower than the 
</p>
<p>experimental values, as shown by the shift between Dh2 and Dh predictions. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—179 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.30. Correlation of fM using the KS/Dh2 and Ra/Dh2 linear fit method in 
</p>
<p>relation to the fD. The points are colored according to the prediction error 
</p>
<p>from the idealized fit. 
</p>
<p>Previous chapters defined the surface texture is the result of randomly distributed 
</p>
<p>adhered powder particles contributing to roughness, as well as of melt pool 
</p>
<p>undulations and of build layers causing periodic and random macro waviness. The 
</p>
<p>orientation of these surface features (treated in Chapter 4) relative to the flow 
</p>
<p>direction affect the value of KS. The channels utilized in the experiments described 
</p>
<p>here were fabricated by depositing layers while moving the deposition nozzle in the 
</p>
<p>vertical direction, therefore surface roughness is caused by randomly distributed 
</p>
<p>particles and directionality is inconsequential. The direction in which the waviness is 
</p>
<p>measured should be based on the direction of the flow and not the direction of the 
</p>
<p>build. In this experiment, with the deposition in the vertical direction the macro-
</p>
<p>waves are perpendicular to the flow direction.  
</p>
<p>To establish the mathematical relationship between the surface texture 
</p>
<p>parameters and the friction factor, as well as the pressure drop, the experimental data 
</p>
<p>were limited to those related to channels having a mean flow areas of 6.64 mm2 
</p>
<p>±10%. Figure 7.31 presents a chart showing values of Darcy friction factor as a 
</p>
<p>function of the measured maximum roughness peak height and maximum waviness 
</p>
<p>height. The plot indicates that the roughness peaks, primarily due to the adhered 
</p>
<p>powder particles, are the main cause of the variation of the friction factor. Adhered </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—180 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>particles increase flow resistance by destroying the laminar sublayer within the 
</p>
<p>boundary layer through the formation of eddies. However, Figure 7.31 also indicates 
</p>
<p>the combined effect of the roughness peak height and maximum waviness height to 
</p>
<p>achieve a friction factor lower than 0.05. As the powder-induced peaks are 
</p>
<p>eliminated, the valleys resulting from the waviness cause flow recirculation that 
</p>
<p>results in local velocity gradients. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.31. Maximum Peak Height (Rp) compared with maximum waviness 
</p>
<p>height (Wz) for channel areas = 6.64 mm2 ±10%, ṁ = 0.13 kg/sec. The data sets 
</p>
<p>are colored according to the fD. 
</p>
<p>To assess the contributions of the valleys, a 3D functional texture parameter 
</p>
<p>called void volume was evaluated for all channels. The void volume represents the 
</p>
<p>space contained within surface texture that can trap fluid, defined as the volume 
</p>
<p>between all peaks and valleys relative to an idealized flat plane fitted at the highest 
</p>
<p>points of the surface (refer to Figure 7.14). This plane can then be offset in the Z-
</p>
<p>orientation (moving -Z towards the bottom of surface) to evaluate the peaks, core 
</p>
<p>texture, and valleys. The void volume is normalized based on the volume per unit 
</p>
<p>area, with units µm3/µm2.  
</p>
<p>Figure 7.32 displays the comparison of the roughness maximum profile peak 
</p>
<p>height (Rp) with the dale void volume (Vvv). The Vvv of the as-built samples have 
</p>
<p>a range of 2.7 to 4.6 µm3/µm2 due to heightened peaks and extreme valleys, often 
</p>
<p>caused by adjacent particles; this is 61% of the total range shown on the plot. As 
</p>
<p>surface enhancements reduce the roughness peaks and corresponding void volume, </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—181 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>flow constriction and surface friction also decrease. The data suggest that an ideal 
</p>
<p>void volume below ~2.3 µm3/µm2 is necessary to achieve a friction factor under 0.04. 
</p>
<p>To approach a friction factor of 0.03, both peak removal nearing planarization and 
</p>
<p>valley reduction are essential. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.32. Maximum peak height (Rp) compared to the dale void volume 
</p>
<p>(Vvv) indicating the remaining valleys; channel areas = 6.64 mm2 ±10%, ṁ = 
</p>
<p>0.13 kg/sec. The data sets are colored according to the fD. 
</p>
<p>To reduce the friction factor, several surface texture attributes must be properly 
</p>
<p>controlled during the channel processing, as indicated by the observed data. Figure 
</p>
<p>7.33 shows the comparison of the primary profile peak height (Pp) with the fD. The 
</p>
<p>primary profile incorporates both roughness and waviness surface attributes that 
</p>
<p>affect the friction factor based on both roughness height and length scales. One of the 
</p>
<p>values (CMP-B8) was identified as an outlier due to an erroneous Pp measurement 
</p>
<p>and is not shown. The variation in the surface profile correlates to four distinct texture 
</p>
<p>types and their corresponding reduction in the experimental friction factor: 1) 
</p>
<p>roughness peaks, 2) peak smoothing/reduction, 3) minimized roughness and 
</p>
<p>combined waviness and 4) valley reduction. These regions were selected arbitrarily 
</p>
<p>based on the data set groupings observed in Figure 7.33 and their corresponding fD. 
</p>
<p>The region labeled ‘Roughness peaks’ is characterized by adhered powder 
</p>
<p>particles that cause the highest friction factor. These peaks must be completely 
</p>
<p>removed to reduce the friction factor. They significantly disrupt the roughness 
</p>
<p>sublayer, increasing drag due to turbulent eddy structures  [319], and causing flow 
</p>
<p>recirculation in deep valleys. As a result, the flow area becomes constricted, with fD  </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—182 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>exceeding 0.07, likely due to the roughness sublayer extending into the outer layer. 
</p>
<p>Perry et al. suggest that eddies can match the roughness height, extending into the 
</p>
<p>core flow and further restricting the flow area [317]. The as-built channels and many 
</p>
<p>AFM processed channels include roughness peak features. The Pp must be reduced 
</p>
<p>by 36% by eliminating the powder induced roughness peaks before peak smoothing 
</p>
<p>is present and the friction factor is reduced by 25%. The region labeled 'Peak 
</p>
<p>Smoothing/Reduction' on Figure 7.33 indicates partially removed, rounded, and 
</p>
<p>sometimes smeared peaks on the surface, resulting in an fD of 0.06 ±0.01. SEM 
</p>
<p>images in section 7.4.1 reveal remnants of adhered powder particles on the surface of 
</p>
<p>the AFM-processed channels. The surface exhibits smeared or reduced peaks, where 
</p>
<p>partial particles blend into the subsurface. These features correspond to a ‘peak 
</p>
<p>smoothing and reduction’ (as indicated in Figure 7.33), but residual particles are still 
</p>
<p>present. 
</p>
<p>To further reduce surface friction, roughness peaks must be fully eliminated and 
</p>
<p>macro waviness reduced, as indicated in the 'Minimized Roughness/Combined 
</p>
<p>Waviness' region in Figure 7.33. Achieving surface planarization requires the 
</p>
<p>complete elimination of all powder particles resulting in a reduction of the peak 
</p>
<p>profile by 30% to obtain a friction factor below 0.05. The depth of the roughness and 
</p>
<p>waviness peaks and resulting flow micro-eddies, can exacerbate the flow constriction. 
</p>
<p>These eddies are influenced by the height and spacing of the roughness and waviness 
</p>
<p>peaks. Section 6.5 ascertained the combination of peaks and density contribution to 
</p>
<p>flow resistance. This aligns with Coleman's research on discrete geometric ribs 
</p>
<p>causing variations in flow disturbances based on the distance between ribs [320]. 
</p>
<p>Therefore, the complete removal of powder particles and the reduction of the spatial 
</p>
<p>waviness peaks are crucial. 
</p>
<p>Minimizing the surface peaks and reducing macro waviness leaves minor valleys 
</p>
<p>present on the surface. At this surface condition, the minor peaks and associated 
</p>
<p>valleys likely become submerged in the sublayer flow, exerting minimal influence on 
</p>
<p>skin friction [318]. The primary profile shows an average reduction of 27% when 
</p>
<p>transitioning from the ‘minimized roughness/waviness’ to the ‘valley reduction’ 
</p>
<p>region. Figure 7.33 shows the primary profile reduction as an exponential curve, 
</p>
<p>correlating with the friction factor reduction, where minimal material removal from 
</p>
<p>waviness causes only slight changes in the friction factor. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—183 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.33. Surface attributes affecting reduction of the friction factor.  The 
</p>
<p>color of the symbols is associated to the surface enhancement process as listed 
</p>
<p>in the legend. Right: The cross-section microscopic images are representative 
</p>
<p>of the surfaces for each of the enhancement processes. 
</p>
<p>Modifying the surface through material removal is essential to achieve a planar 
</p>
<p>surface to minimize flow friction.  Heat exchangers are designed to meet performance 
</p>
<p>targets based on mass flow rate, pressure drop, and heat transfer coefficient. This 
</p>
<p>research focuses on specifying an as-built geometry, where surface alterations ensure 
</p>
<p>the intended geometry and flow conditions are met. To achieve this, the required 
</p>
<p>material removal must align with the heat exchanger's technical specifications and 
</p>
<p>surface conditions (as shown in Figure 7.33). Technical merit should also include 
</p>
<p>processing economics; excessive material removal leads to wasted powder feedstock, 
</p>
<p>longer LP-DED build times, and extended post-processing, while insufficient 
</p>
<p>removal results in high differential pressure, potentially missing performance targets. 
</p>
<p>Figure 7.34 illustrates the value of material removal per side as a function of the 
</p>
<p>Dale void volume measurement. The CM process exhibits the highest material 
</p>
<p>removal (over 90 µm), leading to the largest cross-sectional area but failing to achieve 
</p>
<p>the lowest fD. While CM is effective at removing powder particles and reducing 
</p>
<p>partial waviness peaks, it does not fully planarize the surface by removing valleys. In 
</p>
<p>contrast, CMP effectively reduces waviness peaks through abrasive media polishing, 
</p>
<p>which also minimizes valleys, as shown by the reduction in Vvv (Figure 7.34). 
</p>
<p>Removing 60 µm of material per side, combined with planarization, results in a 
</p>
<p>friction factor of 0.03. Targeting material removal of 84 µm (a 40% increase) reduces 
</p>
<p>the fD by an additional 7%, bringing it down to 0.028. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—184 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The AFM process reduces surface friction with minimal material removal (less 
</p>
<p>than 25 µm), but results in a fD exceeding 0.05. Further reduction of the friction factor 
</p>
<p>may be possible with increased slurry pressure, flow rate, resulting in more 
</p>
<p>aggressive abrasive milling. While CM minimizes tooling and overall costs compared 
</p>
<p>to AFM and CMP, all processes can be used to modify surfaces and adjust the friction 
</p>
<p>factor based on end-use requirements. Future work may explore a combination of 
</p>
<p>techniques to minimize material removal while effectively reducing surface 
</p>
<p>roughness and waviness. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 7.34. Material removal per side and dale void volume compared to the 
</p>
<p>Darcy friction factor. The symbol markers indicate the surface enhancement 
</p>
<p>process and data sets are colored according to the fD. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>7.5 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>This chapter presented the results of flow testing for 600 mm length channels, 
</p>
<p>representative of LP-DED heat exchanger square channels. The effects of three 
</p>
<p>surface enhancement methods—abrasive flow machining, chemical milling, and 
</p>
<p>chemical mechanical polishing— on the fluid dynamic performance of heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger channels were compared. As-built channels were used to determine the 
</p>
<p>performance benchmark. All the built and tested channels feature square a cross 
</p>
<p>section measuring 2.54 x 2.54 mm. The channels were fabricated from the NASA </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—185 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>HR-1 (Fe-Ni-Cr) alloy because the application is the cooling system of rocket 
</p>
<p>nozzles, however results are applicable to heat exchangers realized with the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process in general.  
</p>
<p>Flow testing was conducted at various mass flow rates to characterize the 
</p>
<p>pressure drop for each channel. Following experimental testing, the sample channels 
</p>
<p>underwent destructive evaluation, whereby inner surfaces were characterized using 
</p>
<p>SEM, optical microscopy, and optical surface texture analysis. Based on this detailed 
</p>
<p>study, a friction factor correlation was developed. The correlation includes 
</p>
<p>texture-dependent parameters which in turn relate to equivalent sand grain roughness. 
</p>
<p>The sand grain roughness, originally based on the experiments of Darcy and 
</p>
<p>Nikuradse, is commonly used for friction factor correlations. However, surfaces 
</p>
<p>produced by the LP-DED process are more complex and cannot be characterized with 
</p>
<p>sand grains values. 
</p>
<p>The following conclusions can be drawn: 
</p>
<p>• Surface enhancements significantly reduced boundary layer turbulence by 
</p>
<p>eliminating adhered powder and planarizing the surface, resulting in a 
</p>
<p>tenfold reduction in pressure drop. 
</p>
<p>• The surface enhancement techniques [abrasive flow machining (AFM), 
</p>
<p>chemical milling (CM), and chemical mechanical polishing (CMP)] produce 
</p>
<p>distinct surface attributes (peaks and valleys associated with roughness and 
</p>
<p>waviness) due to differences in the way material is removed, thus surface 
</p>
<p>flow drag is different.  
</p>
<p>• The surface texture was varied across the channels using enhancements 
</p>
<p>applied to the as-built surfaces. The filtered Ra ranged from 3.6 to 19.3 µm, 
</p>
<p>primary Sa ranged from 11.3 to 31.6 µm, primary Sk ranging from 33.9 to 
</p>
<p>99.8 µm, and Pp from 31.4 to 152.3 µm.  
</p>
<p>• A new definition of hydraulic diameter, dependent on the value of the 
</p>
<p>characterized cross-sectional area and core surface texture (Sk), has been 
</p>
<p>proposed and validated with experimental data. This definition reduces the 
</p>
<p>error associated with traditional definitions of hydraulic diameter, which 
</p>
<p>depend solely on cross-sectional area and perimeter. This new expression of 
</p>
<p>the hydraulic diameter allows to overcome the large error due to the strong 
</p>
<p>biases occurring in the case of highly textured surfaces. For example, the 
</p>
<p>value of the actual perimeter of the cross-section of as-built channels is 
</p>
<p>widely different from its design value, which assumes a perfect geometry, 
</p>
<p>free from any irregularity.  
</p>
<p>• None of the friction factor correlations documented in the literature provide 
</p>
<p>values that match those computed with the Darcy friction factor obtained 
</p>
<p>from measured differential pressures, including the Colebrook-White 
</p>
<p>correlation.  A new friction factor correlation was obtained by modifying 
</p>
<p>the sand grain roughness expression and that of the hydraulic diameter so as 
</p>
<p>to take into account microscopic surface features. The predictive capabilities 
</p>
<p>of the new correlation has been validated. 
</p>
<p>• The variation in the surface profile correlates to four distinct types of texture 
</p>
<p>and relates to the reduction in the experimental friction factor: 1) roughness </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 7 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 7—186 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>peaks, 2) peak smoothing/reduction, 3) minimized roughness and combined 
</p>
<p>waviness and 4) valley reduction. 
</p>
<p>• A method was developed to predict sand grain roughness based on variations 
</p>
<p>in the surface texture and associated areal and directionally measured values. 
</p>
<p>• The proposed method for characterizing equivalent sand grain roughness 
</p>
<p>calculates friction factor values with less than 20% deviation from the 
</p>
<p>experimental data, whereas values calculated using other correlations in the 
</p>
<p>literature deviate by approximately 50%. 
</p>
<p>• The primary profile, which considers surface roughness and waviness, 
</p>
<p>provides a reasonable first-order approximation of equivalent sand grain 
</p>
<p>roughness for as-built surfaces. 
</p>
<p>• The average directional roughness normalized by hydraulic diameter 
</p>
<p>(Ra/Dh) provides the best fit for determining the equivalent sand grain 
</p>
<p>roughness from experimental data. 
</p>
<p>• Optimal material removal is essential to eliminate roughness peaks and 
</p>
<p>planarize waviness. Abrasive methods effectively achieve planarization, 
</p>
<p>whereas chemical milling does not allow for waviness to be completely 
</p>
<p>eliminated. A minimum removal thickness of 60 µm per side is necessary to 
</p>
<p>achieve a friction factor below 0.03. Removing 84 µm of material (40% 
</p>
<p>more than the minimum) resulted in a further 7% reduction in friction factor. 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—187 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8 Summary and Recommendations 
</p>
<p>8.1 MAIN FINDINGS AND CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The research documented in this thesis concerns the laser powder directed energy 
</p>
<p>deposition (LP-DED) process for fabricating large-scale compact heat exchangers. 
</p>
<p>With an upper limit for the build volume that is over 17 times larger than the largest 
</p>
<p>possible with powder bed fusion machines (as of 2024), LP-DED enables the 
</p>
<p>fabrication of large and highly complex parts, enhancing performance, part 
</p>
<p>consolidation, and the ability to process novel alloys. This investigation encompassed 
</p>
<p>comprehensive characterization and experimental testing of LP-DED-fabricated 
</p>
<p>microchannels, as well as post-process surface enhancements, focusing on critical 
</p>
<p>surface texture and its impact on fluid flow. The findings are essential to bring to 
</p>
<p>widespread adoption of a manufacturing solution that addresses challenges related to 
</p>
<p>consistent geometry, wall thickness, and high surface texture, establishing LP-DED 
</p>
<p>as a viable technology for current and future heat exchanger fabrication. To 
</p>
<p>summarize the main results, each key research question is addressed separately in the 
</p>
<p>following. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>RQ 1: How is the surface texture of heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>microchannels affected by the LP-DED fabrication process? 
 
</p>
<p>The surface texture of LP-DED walls is influenced by the build process 
</p>
<p>parameters and feedstock including input power, travel speed, layer height, powder 
</p>
<p>feed rate, and powder size distribution. Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 address this research 
</p>
<p>question by treating experiments and the destructive evaluations of samples which 
</p>
<p>enabled the full characterization of the relationship between process parameters, 
</p>
<p>geometric constraints, and the mechanisms causing the surface texture. The focus was 
</p>
<p>on the reduction of the as-built surface texture, which is five to seven times rougher 
</p>
<p>than that of traditionally machined surfaces. Fine powder was shown to reduce 
</p>
<p>surface texture but resulted in larger variations of surface texture and bulk density 
</p>
<p>compared to coarse powder. Geometrical studies revealed that wall angles exceeding 
</p>
<p>30° require stiffening or supports to prevent failure, with surface texture largely 
</p>
<p>influenced by powder adherence and melt pool irregularities. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The characterization of the surface texture resulting from LP-DED requires the 
</p>
<p>identification and measurement of key parameters. These include both those deriving 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8 
 </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—188 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>from 3D areal surface measurements and 2D directional (legacy) measurements, 
</p>
<p>filtered according to the necessary surface topography and ISO standards (ISO 4288-
</p>
<p>1996 and ISO 25178-2:2021) which provide information about primary texture, 
</p>
<p>roughness, and waviness. The study evaluated: average texture (Sa), core roughness 
</p>
<p>(Sk), peak height and valley depth (Spk, Svk), skewness (Ssk), kurtosis (Sku), and 
</p>
<p>directional parameters including average, maximum height, and peak roughness (Ra, 
</p>
<p>Rz, Rp, Rpk), primary, average, and maximum profile (Pp, Pa, Pz), and average and 
</p>
<p>peak waviness (Wa, Wp). The assessment and analysis of these parameters led to the 
</p>
<p>conclusion that while many standard height (amplitude) parameters were relevant for 
</p>
<p>comparing deposition processes, a more detailed characterization, combining 
</p>
<p>amplitude and functional parameters (Sk, Vvv), and material ratio (Spk, Spd), is 
</p>
<p>necessary to fully evaluate surface topography and correlate it with flow 
</p>
<p>performance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The build process is significantly influenced by several key parameters. The 
</p>
<p>study reported in Chapter 3 focused on the production of boxes with 1 mm wall 
</p>
<p>thickness. The as-built inner and outer surfaces were characterized by varying 
</p>
<p>machine parameters and powder sizes. Critical machine parameters included motion 
</p>
<p>control, laser and optics, and powder feed and deposition. All build parameters were 
</p>
<p>evaluated using a design of experiments (DOE) to understand contributions to surface 
</p>
<p>texture and interactions. Key build parameters that were identified were input power, 
</p>
<p>travel speed, layer height, powder feed rate, and powder size distribution. Fine 
</p>
<p>powder (10-45 µm) and coarse powder (45-105 µm) feedstock made through rotary 
</p>
<p>atomization were used. Destructive characterization of samples revealed that 3D areal 
</p>
<p>and 2D profile surface texture correlate with twice the measured powder diameter or 
</p>
<p>with the diameter of partially melted powder at the melt pool's trailing edge.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Several adjustments can be made to the LP-DED technique to enhance the build 
</p>
<p>process and improve surface texture. Surface roughness stems from excess or 
</p>
<p>partially melted powder adhered to the trailing edge of the melt pool in addition to 
</p>
<p>melt pool irregularities. Fine powder (10-45 µm) reduces average surface texture by 
</p>
<p>23% compared to coarse powder (45-105 µm) but results in a higher degree of 
</p>
<p>variability of the texture. Inner surfaces exhibit higher roughness due to ricochets and 
</p>
<p>recirculation, particularly in thin-wall depositions where powder efficiency is 
</p>
<p>reduced to 10-20% (i.e., only 10-20% of the blown powder is captured in the melt 
</p>
<p>pool). Analysis of variance indicated that powder size, layer height, and their 
</p>
<p>interaction accounts for 90% of inner surface roughness and 95% of outer surface 
</p>
<p>roughness. Fine powder also resulted in lower overall porosity (0.03% ±0.01%) 
</p>
<p>compared to coarse powder (0.13% ±0.07%). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>To minimize waviness and achieve uniform surfaces, optimal parameters include 
</p>
<p>a lower laser power (&lt;333 W), higher layer height (&lt;0.28 mm), increased travel speed 
</p>
<p>(800 mm/min), and increased powder feed rate (24.2 g/min). Specific optimal 
</p>
<p>parameters for minimizing surface roughness are fine powder, laser power of 350 W, 
</p>
<p>powder feed rate of 23 g/min, travel speed of 762 mm/min, and a low layer height of 
</p>
<p>0.229 mm. Although these settings reduce surface roughness, process economics </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—189 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>must also be considered. When the layer height is decreased by 10%, the build time 
</p>
<p>increases by 17%, while the roughness only increases by 6% (+1 µm). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4 describes a study devoted to examining how varying geometries, 
</p>
<p>particularly angles and channel sizes, affect surface texture in case coarse NASA HR-
</p>
<p>1 powder is employed. Several enclosed vertical tracks with a target wall thickness 
</p>
<p>of 1 mm and inclination angles up to 45° were fabricated and characterized. 
</p>
<p>Evaluations focused on wall thickness, as-built microstructure (resulting from the 
</p>
<p>melt pool), and surface texture, including waviness and roughness. Walls with angles 
</p>
<p>up to 30° built successfully, but greater values of the angle may result in build failure, 
</p>
<p>potentially requiring the stiffening of the adjacent wall for structural integrity. It was 
</p>
<p>showed that wall thickness increases exponentially for values of the inclination angle 
</p>
<p>above a 30°. Surface texture is primarily influenced by excess powder adherence, 
</p>
<p>melt pool irregularities causing material droop, and excess material, which becomes 
</p>
<p>excessive at angles above 30°. For downskin surfaces, the mean roughness decreases 
</p>
<p>with increasing wall angle, while upskin roughness peaks at 20° and then gradually 
</p>
<p>reduces as powder adheres within valleys. Both downskin and upskin textures are 
</p>
<p>dominated by irregular waviness caused by the melt pool. The samples featured 
</p>
<p>crack-free, low-porosity (&lt;0.14%) material at all considered angles. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Primary areal surface texture (Sa) increases with wall angle due to surface 
</p>
<p>irregularities caused by the melt pool and material droop, with peaks more dominant 
</p>
<p>on the downskin surface. Waviness and wall thickness also increases with wall angle 
</p>
<p>due to chaotic surface irregularities due to material droop on the downskin. Upskin 
</p>
<p>waviness results from excess material deposition above the nominal melt pool, driven 
</p>
<p>by direct particle impingement and remelted material flow. Proper filtering of texture 
</p>
<p>measurements was essential to segregate roughness and waviness, as improper 
</p>
<p>filtering could lead to incorrect conclusions about texture mechanisms. A λc of 0.8 
</p>
<p>mm is an appropriate cut-off filter for these thin-wall samples. As the angle increases, 
</p>
<p>the filtered average areal and directional surface roughness (Sa, Ra) of the downskin 
</p>
<p>surface significantly decreases, contrary to what was observed in relation to L-PBF 
</p>
<p>processes. The upskin surface roughness increases with particle count, peaking at 20° 
</p>
<p>and then decreases as adhered powder fills voids at higher angles. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>A correlation for estimating wall thickness was developed using data obtained 
</p>
<p>from measurements performed on angled samples. Results from single-pass angled 
</p>
<p>walls indicated that the directional maximum profile height (Rz) could be estimated 
</p>
<p>using the maximum particle size distribution, with only particles smaller than 85 µm 
</p>
<p>adhering to the downskin surface at angles above 35° due to higher rebounds and 
</p>
<p>recirculation. The stair-stepping typically observed in surfaces resulting from LP-
</p>
<p>DED was not detected with a layer height of 0.254 mm and remelting of 4-5 
</p>
<p>previously deposited layers. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—190 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>RQ 2: What are the geometrical relationships and 
</p>
<p>sensitivities affecting fluid flow performance if heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger channels are manufactured with the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process? 
 
</p>
<p>The geometrical relationships and sensitivities affecting fluid flow performance 
</p>
<p>include the cross-sectional area, perimeter, and surface texture characteristics 
</p>
<p>including roughness and waviness. Surface characteristics result from roughness 
</p>
<p>peaks and valleys, waviness peaks and valleys, and the frequency, or density, of these 
</p>
<p>features. Fluid flow performance was evaluated experimentally by varying the flow 
</p>
<p>rate and measuring the flow resistance (discharge coefficient) and pressure drop 
</p>
<p>along the channel length.  Various surface finishing techniques, such as abrasive flow 
</p>
<p>machining (AFM), chemical milling (CM), chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), 
</p>
<p>electrochemical machining (PECM), and thermal energy method (TEM) were applied 
</p>
<p>to modify the internal surfaces. The microchannel samples used for these experiments 
</p>
<p>were fabricated from the NASA HR-1 Fe-Ni-Cr alloy. The friction factor estimated 
</p>
<p>from samples featuring as-built surfaces is 0.097±0.00028, while surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement techniques reduce it to 0.023±0.00033. Destructive evaluation of 
</p>
<p>channels following testing provides the opportunity to derive critical insights into 
</p>
<p>surface texture and geometry if tools like optical microscopy, SEM, and CT scanning 
</p>
<p>are used for detailed analysis. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Surface texture parameters, including areal and directional characteristics, are 
</p>
<p>instrumental in identifying peaks, valleys, and their densities as they relate to 
</p>
<p>microchannels. Directional measurements are sensitive to the location of the profile 
</p>
<p>line along the sample length, which can result in variations in peaks or valleys. In 
</p>
<p>contrast, areal measurements are affected by less error because the entire surface is 
</p>
<p>involved in the measurement. Notably, Sp indicates high variation since it is based 
</p>
<p>on a single peak value across the surface. This highlights the importance of using 
</p>
<p>reduced peak height (Spk) and core roughness (Sk) to avoid bias toward the value of 
</p>
<p>a single peak height. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Microchannels produced through LP-DED can be used in their as-fabricated 
</p>
<p>condition, but they may not meet the pressure drop requirements of many heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers depending on their applications. Surface texture variations, such as 
</p>
<p>powder adherence, melt pool undulations, and polishing mechanisms, significantly 
</p>
<p>affect fluid flow performance. The experimental flow test results presented in Chapter 
</p>
<p>6 and 7 indicate that as-built surfaces cause high flow resistance due to powder 
</p>
<p>adherence, which diminishes with material removal. Adhered powder particles 
</p>
<p>increase flow resistance and disrupt the flow boundary sublayer causing the 
</p>
<p>formation of micro-scale eddies. The discharge coefficient analysis put into evidence 
</p>
<p>that 65% of the flow resistance variations are due to area alterations, 8% to perimeter 
</p>
<p>changes, and 25% to surface texture.  
</p>
<p> </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—191 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Surface enhancement processes aimed at improving the discharge coefficient 
</p>
<p>must reduce surface roughness, waviness peaks, and their frequency. A minimum 
</p>
<p>surface material removal of 50 µm is required to reduce the mean friction factor by 
</p>
<p>more than 30% while removing 70 µm, equivalent to the mean powder diameter, can 
</p>
<p>reduce the mean friction factor by more than 57%. Post-processing steps such as 
</p>
<p>eliminating adhered powder and planarizing surface peaks and valleys are critical to 
</p>
<p>reducing friction factors and flow resistance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The differential pressure along the length of LP-DED microchannels is caused 
</p>
<p>mainly by roughness peaks, but other surface characteristics play an important role 
</p>
<p>in the reduction of the friction factor below 0.05. Therefore, peak 
</p>
<p>smoothing/reduction, roughness minimization, and combined waviness/valley 
</p>
<p>reduction are critical to reducing the friction factor. Effective heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>microchannels rely on accurate predictions, and detailed characterization validates 
</p>
<p>the assumptions. The ratios between roughness and waviness peaks and densities 
</p>
<p>(Spk/Spd and Wp/WPc) are proposed therefore as one of the key parameters for 
</p>
<p>surface characterization as they are directly correlated to flow resistance. 
</p>
<p>Additionally, values of the dale void volume (Vvv) can be used to detect where fluid 
</p>
<p>could be trapped inside the surface valleys. Moreover, Vvv correlates with the 
</p>
<p>reduction of friction factor. The analysis of Vvv over the surface can be used to 
</p>
<p>identify the location of the transition from roughness-dominated to waviness-
</p>
<p>dominated surface effects, thus it can guide the design of material removal processes 
</p>
<p>aimed at optimizing flow resistance. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>RQ 3: What improvements can be made to control the 
</p>
<p>surface texture of thin-wall LP-DED internal microchannels? 
 
</p>
<p>The ability of controlling the surface texture of thin-wall internal microchannels 
</p>
<p>allows to effectively design flow passages complying with a specified friction factor. 
</p>
<p>These surface improvement processes must be controlled in terms of the detailed 
</p>
<p>characteristics of the surface texture and repeatability. It is of paramount importance 
</p>
<p>that associated predictive models for the estimation of the relevant surface parameters 
</p>
<p>are available.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Several post-processing techniques were evaluated using 600 mm-long sample 
</p>
<p>channels, namely abrasive flow machining (AFM), chemical milling (CM), and 
</p>
<p>chemical mechanical polishing (CMP). These techniques reduce texture by 
</p>
<p>eliminating partially and fully adhered powder, minimizing waviness, and achieving 
</p>
<p>planarization. Detailed characterization of resulting surfaces, in relation to process 
</p>
<p>parameters, revealed different characteristics, including remnant powder, jagged 
</p>
<p>edges, crystals, scratches, pitting, and selective grain boundary attack. Material 
</p>
<p>removal varied among the considered enhancement techniques, with CM and CMP 
</p>
<p>being the most effective in this regard, allowing the elimination of a layer of up to 
</p>
<p>355 µm per side. The increased cross-sectional area considerably reduces the pressure 
</p>
<p>drop over the length of the channel, which is also due to the decrease of boundary </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—192 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>layer turbulence. However, material removal should be minimized to avoid the cost 
</p>
<p>of excessive processing time. Maximum material removal does not result in the 
</p>
<p>lowest friction factor. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>It was further demonstrated that surface enhancements produce distinct surface 
</p>
<p>attributes, affecting surface-induced drag differently in relation to the variations in 
</p>
<p>material removal mechanisms (i.e., chemical and abrasive), which in turn result in 
</p>
<p>different surface characteristics in terms of peaks and valleys. Channel surface 
</p>
<p>conditions varied widely if comparing as-built surface to enhanced surfaces, with 
</p>
<p>filtered Ra ranging from 3.6 to 19.3 µm, primary Sa from 11.3 to 31.6 µm, primary 
</p>
<p>Sk ranging from 33.9 to 99.8 µm, and Pp from 31.4 to 152.3 µm. These variations in 
</p>
<p>texture result in values of the friction factor ranging from 0.097 to 0.023. These large 
</p>
<p>variations indicate that, while the techniques can be effective, the aspect of 
</p>
<p>repeatability needs to be carefully addressed to ensure uniform results. 
</p>
<p>The coefficient of variation was used to evaluate the consistency of surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement results along the length of the microchannels using data from three 
</p>
<p>distinct cross-sections. Variation in perimeter and area was attributed to random 
</p>
<p>error, with a maximum cross-sectional area error of 2.5%, the same for all considered 
</p>
<p>post-processing techniques. Perimeter error varied more than cross-sectional area, 
</p>
<p>driven by randomly adhered powder on the internal channel walls. AFM processed 
</p>
<p>channels featured the highest perimeter variation, namely 6.2%; CM samples 
</p>
<p>exhibited consistent perimeter variation, lower than 2.8%; the perimeter variation of 
</p>
<p>CMP samples was within 1.9%. These values indicate significant improvement of 
</p>
<p>surface texture, as the as-built samples featured a perimeter value that was 26% 
</p>
<p>greater than the nominal value (the design specification). 
</p>
<p>Differences between hydraulic diameter values estimated with analytical 
</p>
<p>methods and values determined by means of experimental flow data highlight the 
</p>
<p>need for improved models, as those presented in this thesis. Traditional hydraulic 
</p>
<p>diameter correlations do not account for the impact of surface texture of its value, 
</p>
<p>leading to significant errors due to the much higher value of the actual perimeter. A 
</p>
<p>new method for calculating the hydraulic diameter (Dh), which is made dependent 
</p>
<p>also on the values of the as-fabricated area and of the core surface texture (Sk), has 
</p>
<p>been proposed and validated.  The correlation improves the accuracy of the friction 
</p>
<p>factor prediction to within 20% of the value obtained with experimental data, 
</p>
<p>compared to 50% of the previous methods. 
</p>
<p>None of the existing friction factor correlations, including the Colebrook-White 
</p>
<p>correlation, can correctly predict the value of the Darcy friction factor obtained from 
</p>
<p>experimental data. Using the newly proposed hydraulic diameter definition, a 
</p>
<p>modified sand grain roughness correlation was developed in order to predict values 
</p>
<p>associated with surfaces with different textures. This method, accounting for surface 
</p>
<p>roughness and waviness, provides a more accurate prediction of the equivalent sand 
</p>
<p>grain roughness (KS) and of the resulting friction factor. Experimental data showed 
</p>
<p>that the average directional roughness normalized by the value of the hydraulic 
</p>
<p>diameter (Ra/Dh) provided the best fit for determining equivalent sand grain 
</p>
<p>roughness, in agreement with experimental observations. Additional linear fit 
</p>
<p>methods incorporated surface texture parameters Pp, Sa, and Sk and normalized by </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—193 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Dh. It was also observed that Pp, which accounts for the effects of surface roughness 
</p>
<p>and waviness, provides a reasonable first-order approximation of the equivalent sand 
</p>
<p>grain roughness for as-built surfaces. 
</p>
<p>In summary, being able to control the surface texture of thin-wall internal 
</p>
<p>microchannels manufactured with LP-DED requires a combination of effective post-
</p>
<p>processing techniques and improved modeling correlations. The driving mechanisms 
</p>
<p>of surface texture affecting friction factor reduction were identified, including 
</p>
<p>roughness peaks, peak smoothing/reduction, minimized roughness and combined 
</p>
<p>waviness, and valley reduction. Abrasive methods like CMP allow to achieve a high 
</p>
<p>level of planarization, whereas chemical milling leaves macro waviness remnants. A 
</p>
<p>minimum removal thickness of 60 µm per side is necessary to achieve a friction factor 
</p>
<p>below 0.03, with diminishing returns observed below this threshold. Removing 84 
</p>
<p>µm of material (40% more than the minimum) resulted in a further 7% reduction in 
</p>
<p>friction factor. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.2 KEY INNOVATIONS 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The research work has contributed to the body of knowledge on the general topic 
</p>
<p>by providing a baseline understanding of the various aspects associated to surface 
</p>
<p>texture and their relation to process parameters, geometry (such as channel sizes, 
</p>
<p>angles, and surface orientation), key surface texture parameters, mechanisms that 
</p>
<p>affect flow resistance and friction factors, and material removal targets using surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement post-processing to achieve a required surface specification. Research 
</p>
<p>results provide designers with a variety of options to properly tailor the surface to the 
</p>
<p>flow requirements, like the specification of the pressure drop due to the flow through 
</p>
<p>the microchannels in compact heat exchangers.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>The main research innovations achieved with this research include:  
 
</p>
<p>1. Determination of the build factors and relationships associated with the 
</p>
<p>surface texture of 1 mm thick NASA HR-1 alloy surfaces obtained with the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED process (see Chapter 3 and Chapter 4). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• Previous state-of-the-art research primarily examined the 
</p>
<p>microstructural aspects and mechanical properties of samples produced 
</p>
<p>using the LP-DED process, with wall thicknesses ranging from 1 to 3 
</p>
<p>mm. There was very limited research on the effect of process 
</p>
<p>parameters and on surface texture (roughness, waviness) of 1 mm 
</p>
<p>vertical walls. No information related to thin walls made with the 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 alloy was available. Prior studies concentrated on 
</p>
<p>roughness without considering waviness or distinguishing between 
</p>
<p>these surface texture differences. Additionally, the impact of build 
</p>
<p>parameters on surface texture was not extensively explored, especially 
</p>
<p>for the NASA HR-1 alloy. </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—194 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>2. Characterization and identification of geometric constraints for repeatable 
</p>
<p>LP-DED fabrication of heat exchangers (see Chapter 4 and Chapter 5). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• To the best of the author's knowledge, no prior research on 
</p>
<p>microchannels produced using the LP-DED process is documented in 
</p>
<p>the open literature. Additionally, prior literature on the subject lacks’ 
</p>
<p>information on the geometric aspects, angled wall limitations, surface 
</p>
<p>texture, and the process mechanisms causing these geometric 
</p>
<p>variations. The characterization of geometry and surface texture of 
</p>
<p>internal microchannels and of various enclosed boxes fabricated at 
</p>
<p>different angles provides foundational information for heat exchanger 
</p>
<p>designers. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>3. Evaluation and characterization of surface enhancement techniques for 2.5 
</p>
<p>mm square internal microchannel features manufactured with the LP-DED 
</p>
<p>process to tailor required flow characteristics (see Chapter 6 and Chapter 7). 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• Prior investigations on surface enhancement techniques focused 
</p>
<p>exclusively on their application to additively manufactured laser 
</p>
<p>powder bed fusion (L-PBF) samples. The geometric characterization of 
</p>
<p>the process and channels allowed to conclude that the LP-DED process 
</p>
<p>is fundamentally different from L-PBF. Conclusions from L-PBF 
</p>
<p>research cannot be applied broadly to other AM processes, including 
</p>
<p>LP-DED. This experimental study is the first concerning various 
</p>
<p>surface enhancements applied to microchannels manufactured with the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED process. For the first time, different surface texture 
</p>
<p>mechanisms caused by distinct enhancement techniques were 
</p>
<p>identified, including peak smoothing/reduction, minimized roughness, 
</p>
<p>combined waviness, and valley reduction. Previous L-PBF samples 
</p>
<p>were limited to less than 250 mm due to build constraints. This research 
</p>
<p>develops and characterizes surface-enhanced samples that are 600 mm 
</p>
<p>long, a 140% increase in length. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>4. Development of an analytical model and correlation to estimate the pressure 
</p>
<p>drop of high-roughness channels fabricated with LP-DED. The correlation 
</p>
<p>is validated with experimental data (Chapter 7).  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>• Friction factors and pressure drop associated with LP-DED made 
</p>
<p>microchannels in both the as-built and surfaced enhanced state have 
</p>
<p>been investigated for the first time. Considered surface treatments 
</p>
<p>include abrasive flow machining, chemical milling, and chemical 
</p>
<p>mechanical polishing. A wide range of surface conditions were 
</p>
<p>obtained and allowed to develop a new correlation for the calculation 
</p>
<p>of the hydraulic diameter of square channels. Additionally, based on 
</p>
<p>experimental data resulting from flow tests, a new correlation to </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—195 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>determine sand grain roughness, friction factors and pressure drop was 
</p>
<p>developed. The correlation allows prediction of these values with a 
</p>
<p>deviation of less than 20% with respect to experimental data. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.3 FUTURE APPLICATIONS AND RESEARCH 
</p>
<p>AREAS  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.3.1 Initial Application Cases 
</p>
<p>The research effort documented in this dissertation is integrated into a broader 
</p>
<p>investigation concerning the use of the LP-DED process for the fabrication of 
</p>
<p>components of liquid rocket engines. Although demonstrating and testing 
</p>
<p>components were outside the scope of the work documented here, findings on channel 
</p>
<p>characterization and build limitations from this research contributed to the design of 
</p>
<p>nozzle and combustion chamber prototypes. Several channel-cooled nozzles made 
</p>
<p>with the NASA HR-1 alloy were constructed and underwent hot-fire testing aimed at 
</p>
<p>proving initial feasibility. These nozzles were tested using various propellant 
</p>
<p>combinations including Liquid Oxygen/Liquid Hydrogen (LOX/LH2), 
</p>
<p>LOX/Kerosene (LOX/RP-1), and LOX/Methane (LOX/CH4). The hot-fire testing 
</p>
<p>campaigns took place at NASA Marshall Space Flight Center (MSFC) at thrust 
</p>
<p>classes ranging from 8.9 to 178 kN. The testing demonstrated the suitability of the 
</p>
<p>LP-DED process and of the alloy in a relevant environment, validated design 
</p>
<p>assumptions, and allow to determine the heat transfer and pressure drop 
</p>
<p>characteristics associated with various designs. A total of 488 starts were performed 
</p>
<p>and the total duration of these tests was 16,291 seconds, whereby 12 nozzles were 
</p>
<p>examined [13,321]. 
</p>
<p>A single nozzle featuring a thrust of 8.9 kN accumulated 207 starts and 6,756 
</p>
<p>seconds of testing time with consistent performance between tests [254,321]. Figure 
</p>
<p>8.1 displays nozzles that were fabricated and successfully tested with as-built surface 
</p>
<p>channels. While the NASA HR-1 alloy was demonstrated in the extreme 
</p>
<p>environment, additional design and post-processing lessons were learned, like, for 
</p>
<p>example, those related to more advanced polishing techniques. All this knowledge is 
</p>
<p>essential to progress towards the use of these nozzles in actual missions. It can be 
</p>
<p>argued that the results of the research documented here is essential to mature this 
</p>
<p>manufacturing technology to the level that is needed for space flight and possibly 
</p>
<p>other industrial applications.  
</p>
<p>In addition to the hot-fire demonstrations, large-scale manufacturing trials were 
</p>
<p>also completed.  A 1.52 m dia. and 1.78 m height integral channel nozzle made of 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 alloy was built in 90 days. This is estimated to be four to six times faster 
</p>
<p>than producing a traditionally manufactured unit for the first time. Additionally, a 
</p>
<p>chamber was fabricated, also made of NASA HR-1, and used as a simulator for a 
</p>
<p>nuclear thermal propulsion (NTP) engine. Examples of these large-scale </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—196 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>manufacturing trials are shown in Figure 8.1. These manufacturing trials 
</p>
<p>demonstrated a significant opportunity for schedule and cost reductions compared to 
</p>
<p>traditional manufacturing technologies. The results have garnered active interest 
</p>
<p>across the commercial space sector, highlighting the potential for manufacturing 
</p>
<p>large-scale channel wall nozzles and combustion chambers using LP-DED. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p> 
Figure 8.1. Examples of LP-DED nozzles during hot-fire testing and 
</p>
<p>fabrication. A) 178 kN integral channel on the test stand, B) Hot-fire testing of 
</p>
<p>the 178 kN nozzle using LOX/LH2, C) 8.9 kN integral channel nozzle, D) 
</p>
<p>NASA HR-1 nozzle accumulating 207 starts and 6,756 sec during test, E) 
</p>
<p>Manufacturing trial of 1.52 m dia. and 1.78 m height integral channel nozzle, 
</p>
<p>F) Manufacturing trial of NTP LP-DED chamber. (Courtesy: NASA)  
</p>
<p>[254,321] </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—197 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.3.2 Future Applications 
</p>
<p>Several applications of the LP-DED process for the manufacturing of large 
</p>
<p>compact heat exchangers for extreme environments can be envisaged for both 
</p>
<p>terrestrial use and space exploration. Efficient and sustainable power generation will 
</p>
<p>require novel high temperature compact heat exchangers. Examples are primary heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers and recuperators of supercritical CO2 cycle power plants, heat exchangers 
</p>
<p>in hydrogen gas turbines, etc. Transitioning to hydrogen as a fuel source can 
</p>
<p>significantly reduce carbon dioxide emissions, and compact novel suitable heat 
</p>
<p>exchangers are key enablers of these new power technologies. Other potential uses in 
</p>
<p>power generation include heat exchangers in solar thermal energy power plants. 
</p>
<p>However, these heat exchangers will require the adoption of other materials and 
</p>
<p>manufacturing processes, different from those treated in this dissertation.  
</p>
<p>The aviation industry is also exploring hydrogen as a more sustainable fuel. 
</p>
<p>Future propulsion systems may include gas turbine engines, fuel cells, or hybrid 
</p>
<p>systems that incorporate compact heat exchangers [322].The realization of  
</p>
<p>supersonic commercial aircraft and hypersonic vehicles will require active cooling in 
</p>
<p>leading edges and control surfaces, which can be obtained with microchannels 
</p>
<p>integrated into large structures. 
</p>
<p>Future exploration missions will require in-space cryocooler storage for 
</p>
<p>propellants with active cooling to prevent boil-off. The stored fuel can be used for 
</p>
<p>propulsion systems, lunar or Martian power plants, resource extraction, and chemical 
</p>
<p>or life support systems. Additionally, managing the extreme temperature fluctuations 
</p>
<p>in space will require efficient spacecraft radiators. The complexity of these systems 
</p>
<p>can be addressed through manufacturing methods like LP-DED and the use of 
</p>
<p>hydrogen-resistant alloys.  
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.4 RECOMMENDATIONS 
</p>
<p>8.4.1 Design and Analysis 
</p>
<p> Chapter 7 of this thesis describes the evaluation of various surface 
</p>
<p>conditions and provides new correlations for determining the hydraulic diameter of 
</p>
<p>square microchannels. Many numerical simulation methods do not take into account 
</p>
<p>the complex effect of surface texture, and these new correlations should be integrated 
</p>
<p>into flow models to enhance prediction accuracy. Additionally, the data sets from this 
</p>
<p>research offer an opportunity to develop a new version of the Moody diagram, 
</p>
<p>accounting for the intricacies of additively manufactured surfaces. Machine learning 
</p>
<p>techniques could be employed to analyze the extensive data sets generated during this 
</p>
<p>research. This approach could include not only the experimental test data but also the 
</p>
<p>images, allowing for further improvement of the predictions of friction factors and 
</p>
<p>flow resistances related to detailed surface topography. 
</p>
<p>The design and analysis of heat exchangers are highly interdisciplinary, 
</p>
<p>involving both heat transfer and fluid dynamics. Future experiments should </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—198 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>incorporate the investigation of heat transfer phenomena to determine Nusselt 
</p>
<p>correlations depending on variations of the surface textures. These experiments 
</p>
<p>should encompass varying channel sizes, materials, and fluids, including both liquids 
</p>
<p>and gases, across a range of Reynolds numbers and surface conditions. Flow 
</p>
<p>visualization on a micro-scale could also be included. Although the correlation 
</p>
<p>between different types of roughness and waviness with respect to the variation in 
</p>
<p>friction factors was established, visual confirmation of flow structures would enhance 
</p>
<p>future modeling efforts. A challenge with this type of experiment is that using clear 
</p>
<p>acrylic for visualization may diminish some of the three-dimensional flow effects. 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>8.4.2 Process Specific 
</p>
<p>If other materials will be studied in the future, factors such as build parameters, 
</p>
<p>solidification rates, geometry (including channel sizes), powder efficiency, and heat 
</p>
<p>treatment operations may alter the absolute values of surface texture. Evaluating other 
</p>
<p>materials is essential to expand the database of alloys suitable for future compact 
</p>
<p>high-temperature heat exchangers fabricated with LP-DED. Varying heat treatments 
</p>
<p>of the NASA HR-1 alloy may slightly change the surface texture due to oxidation or 
</p>
<p>the removal of partially adhered powder resulting from high thermal gradients. 
</p>
<p>Chapter 4 reports a study of surface texture with varying angles. Further research 
</p>
<p>could investigate complex surfaces or variable channel geometries and the results 
</p>
<p>would contribute to making available additional design solutions. These surfaces 
</p>
<p>might include complex freeform surfaces or structures optimized through shape or 
</p>
<p>topology optimization. 
</p>
<p>The post-processing surface modifications resulted in observations that included 
</p>
<p>remnant powder, jagged edges and crystals, scratches, pitting or selective grain 
</p>
<p>boundary attack. Some of these conditions merit an additional characterization effort 
</p>
<p>since they could affect mechanical properties of the material. The jagged edges, 
</p>
<p>scratches, pitting, and grain boundary attack might result in reduced fatigue life due 
</p>
<p>the surface defects. Controlled mechanical testing should be conducted with the 
</p>
<p>identical processing parameters to determine any debits to properties.  
</p>
<p>An important aspect related to this research in relation to the industrialization of 
</p>
<p>the LP-DED is the tradeoff between the specification of the surface texture and the 
</p>
<p>associated processing time and cost. This aspect was not addressed, and more 
</p>
<p>specifically the lifecycle processing cost, specialty tooling, processing challenges, or 
</p>
<p>processing timeline. The post-processing methods documented in Chapters 6 and 7 
</p>
<p>focused on more mature techniques, which are becoming accessible to the industry. 
</p>
<p>Combining multiple techniques may provide an optimal solution that meets both 
</p>
<p>technical and cost/programmatic specifications. 
</p>
<p>8.4.3 Production and Operation 
</p>
<p>As discussed in Chapter 2, the implications of surface texture on the production 
</p>
<p>of system and components are often overlooked during the initial design phase. 
</p>
<p>Bridging the gap between theoretical analysis and the practical challenges of </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—199 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>manufacturing and achieving design specifications in the shortest possible time may 
</p>
<p>require additional education or training for designers. Surface conditions can also 
</p>
<p>change during operation due to factors such as erosion, wear, corrosion, oxidation, or 
</p>
<p>other environmental influences. As AM processes become more common in industry, 
</p>
<p>the long-term effects on surface texture during operation will need to be investigated. 
</p>
<p>More data should be collected and publicly shared to identify performance shifts, 
</p>
<p>determine their root causes, and assess their relation to potential changes in surface 
</p>
<p>texture. 
</p>
<p>Early collection of surface texture data during the manufacturing process can 
</p>
<p>help reduce uncertainty. The use of surface texture witness specimens is important as 
</p>
<p>components using the LP-DED process enter production. An open channel, as 
</p>
<p>proposed by Thiam [323], can be built alongside the part and measured to represent 
</p>
<p>the closed internal microchannels. These data can then be used to reduce uncertainty 
</p>
<p>in the analysis prior to actual testing, and early in the manufacturing phase. Based on 
</p>
<p>the surface texture, the KF value from this dissertation can be applied to improve 
</p>
<p>predictions and reduce uncertainty.  
</p>
<p>8.5 REFLECTIONS 
</p>
<p>Designing components, such as compact heat exchangers, may seem "easy" if 
</p>
<p>compared to the challenges encountered during their manufacturing. Manufacturing 
</p>
<p>can yield non-conformances due to incorrect design assumptions, human error, 
</p>
<p>integration or planning issues, or simply unforeseen circumstances. Similarly, 
</p>
<p>planning might appear straightforward, but execution can falter. Research is no 
</p>
<p>different—what seems like a simple path from A to B is rarely a straight line. This 
</p>
<p>iterative process defines becoming an independent researcher. It involves 
</p>
<p>methodically exploring every aspect to fully characterize the process using the 
</p>
<p>scientific method, connecting previously unrelated topics, and constructing a 
</p>
<p>comprehensive story. 
</p>
<p>I initially spent considerable time on literature searches regarding various heat 
</p>
<p>exchanger topics, but felt overwhelmed by too many papers lacking focus and 
</p>
<p>relevance. Although I thought I had scoped my topic, I did not grasp all the nuances 
</p>
<p>until I started laboratory work and understood which questions needed to be asked. 
</p>
<p>Developing a new process for manufacturing heat exchangers revealed many 
</p>
<p>unknowns in the planning stage. I recommend maintaining flexibility in planning, as 
</p>
<p>seemingly unimportant aspects might become crucial later. A schedule should be a 
</p>
<p>living document, continuously updated. This approach proved true when 
</p>
<p>characterizing the LP-DED samples at various angles, leading to insights that resulted 
</p>
<p>in an unplanned but valuable journal article. Further characterization of surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement techniques for LP-DED samples at varying angles is recommended to 
</p>
<p>determine optimal processing parameters. 
</p>
<p>Challenging prior "facts" can be difficult, as I found when determining the 
</p>
<p>hydraulic diameter. Using a known relationship did not match my assumptions, 
</p>
<p>leading me to question my methods. Initially, I believed I had collected too many 
</p>
<p>data, which caused me months of doubt. While it was ultimately beneficial for 
</p>
<p>revealing challenges with the hydraulic diameter and evaluating various surface </p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Chapter 8 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>Chapter 8—200 
</p>
<p> 
</p>
<p>conditions, it was exhausting to measure, characterize, and evaluate dozens of 
</p>
<p>measurements per tube (resulting in hundreds of channel sections to evaluate). In 
</p>
<p>hindsight, a smaller data set might have led to similar results, if the importance of 
</p>
<p>each measurement was correctly prioritized. Early test trials with one or two samples 
</p>
<p>could help prioritize data sets, saving time in the end. 
</p>
<p>My current career trajectory has led me to the point where I contribute and lead 
</p>
<p>research instead of solely “consuming” it. Throughout the years that led to this thesis, 
</p>
<p>the term "independent researcher" resonated with me, a definition first mentioned by 
</p>
<p>my supervisors. I noticed a gradual shift in how I reviewed and evaluated prior 
</p>
<p>published research, often feeling frustrated by missing steps, incomplete data, or 
</p>
<p>incorrect terminology. The term "roughness" is frequently misused in journal articles, 
</p>
<p>defined incorrectly by data filtering. This realization led me to consult both experts 
</p>
<p>and literature to ensure I used terms correctly. Accepted common terms might not 
</p>
<p>always be accurate, and repeated incorrect references do not make them true. 
</p>
<p>My interactions with my supervisors, Dr. Angelo Cervone and Prof. Piero 
</p>
<p>Colonna, were invaluable. These meetings often led to realizations, criticisms, and 
</p>
<p>adjustments in planning and execution. While I sometimes sought confirmation that 
</p>
<p>I had done something correctly, I did not always receive it. This was not negative; it 
</p>
<p>made me realize that at a detailed research level, my supervisors might not have all 
</p>
<p>the answers. Instead, they questioned my methodology and offered feedback, helping 
</p>
<p>me to affirm or further probe my approach. From that process, I also learned how to 
</p>
<p>guide others in their research, even if I am not the expert. A skill that an independent 
</p>
<p>researcher is required to have. As undergraduates, we are taught that answers are in 
</p>
<p>the back of the book. In the real world, we realize multiple answers exist. This 
</p>
<p>research showed that we are developing new correlations, questioning prior 
</p>
<p>assumptions and research, and adding to the overall body of knowledge as an 
</p>
<p>independent researcher. 
</p>
<p>In the final six months of my analysis and writing, several questions arose, as 
</p>
<p>outlined in section 8.4, but remained unanswered due to time and resource 
</p>
<p>constraints. Developing and characterizing a new process for this application required 
</p>
<p>substantial resources for maturation. Fully exploring every detail demanded more 
</p>
<p>effort than initially anticipated, leading to a necessary rescoping of the research to a 
</p>
<p>more achievable focus. I hope to collaborate with future researchers to expand this 
</p>
<p>topic further. I look forward to the opportunity to incorporate heat transfer with 
</p>
<p>pressure drop for different fluids, integrate this information into simulation codes, 
</p>
<p>and conduct future experiments using flow visualization. Additionally, it will be 
</p>
<p>critical to obtain mechanical properties (e.g., fatigue) with different surface 
</p>
<p>enhancement methods and understand the processing economics to incorporate into 
</p>
<p>actual applications. Although this dissertation concludes the research, my curiosity 
</p>
<p>and desire to see it used by others is ultimately the highest compliment I strive for. 
</p>
<p> </p>

<p>10 Acknowledgements 
 
</p>
<p>In exploring the world around us, I have come to appreciate the diverse surfaces 
</p>
<p>that define our lives. From meticulously polished playground bars to the undulating 
</p>
<p>textures of natural elements like trees and mountains, surfaces are omnipresent. They 
</p>
<p>influence the wind, control motion and fluids, create beauty in children's toys, and 
</p>
<p>reflect our own images in the mirror. My reflection is not just about surfaces, but 
</p>
<p>about the unique skills and support contributed by my family, friends, and colleagues. 
</p>
<p>This thesis integrates the profound understanding of the world, artistic prowess, 
</p>
<p>perfectionism, planning, organization, drive, passion, and leadership instilled by each 
</p>
<p>of you. 
</p>
<p>Dax, your insightful observations of the world consistently astound me. Your 
</p>
<p>ability to articulate complex concepts, such as the physics behind a straw resisting 
</p>
<p>the wind and the defects in a glass car window, reflects a curiosity that is truly 
</p>
<p>inspiring. The anecdote of our windy walk after playing hockey, where you 
</p>
<p>confidently explained the principles at play on the straw, stands as a testament to your 
</p>
<p>intellectual depth and curiosity. I drew upon your curiosity to spark my own within 
</p>
<p>my research. 
</p>
<p>Tess, your exceptional artistic flair never ceases to amaze me. Your meticulous 
</p>
<p>planning and relentless pursuit of perfection in translating ideas onto paper showcase 
</p>
<p>an unwavering commitment to your craft. Your ability to come up with artistic ideas 
</p>
<p>and solutions through Legos, yarn, paper, and crafts is amazing. I drew upon your 
</p>
<p>perfectionism and creativeness to devise unique and artistic solutions to problems. 
</p>
<p>Leah, my life partner, epitomizes qualities of drive, visionary thinking, a love of 
</p>
<p>life, supportiveness of everyone around you, effective communication, 
</p>
<p>compassionate and servant leadership, and organizational prowess. Your unwavering 
</p>
<p>support throughout this journey fueled my inspiration. I am immensely grateful for 
</p>
<p>the shared moments and eagerly anticipate the future we will navigate together. I 
</p>
<p>drew upon many of your skills, support, and compassion, but I certainly cannot 
</p>
<p>compete with your amazing qualities. Love u!  
</p>
<p>To my parents and family, I extend heartfelt gratitude for instilling in me the 
</p>
<p>values of hard work, motivation, and boundless love. Your enduring guidance has 
</p>
<p>been a guiding light in my pursuit. To my family, I appreciate your support and for 
</p>
<p>challenging me over the years. I drew upon all of these values that each of you 
</p>
<p>instilled in me throughout my life. 
</p>
<p>Angelo and Piero, I deeply appreciate the time we spent together as friends and 
</p>
<p>colleagues throughout this challenging and rewarding journey. Your critical input, 
</p>
<p>discussions, challenges, advice, support, and friendship have been invaluable. I 
</p>
<p>thoroughly enjoyed each of our conversations, whether in the office or over meals, 
</p>
<p>and I learned so much from both of you on the path to becoming an independent 
</p>
<p>researcher. Thank you for welcoming me into your research groups. I know that 
</p>
<p>lifelong friendships were formed, and I hope to continue working with you and 
</p>
<p>making you proud. 
</p>
<p>To my NASA, industry, and academic colleagues, I express sincere 
</p>
<p>appreciation for your invaluable contributions—whether through engaging </p>
